\title{Airy amplitudes for relaxed trees, compacted trees\\ and finite-language automata\\[4pt]
\large Positive amplitudes and expansions to every fixed order,\\ binary and every fixed arity (OEIS A082161 and relatives)}
\author{A merged research report built from six AI-assisted manuscripts\\
(batch 77, manuscripts 62, 15, 18, 66, 29 and 48)\\
and three AI-assisted bundle reports (batch 102, Reports 100, 101 and 103; Part V)}
\date{Manuscripts dated 2 October 2026; merged 2 October 2026; Part V added 5 October 2026}
\begin{document}
\maketitle
\begin{abstract}
Relaxed trees, compacted trees and minimal deterministic automata accepting finite languages form one family of triangular recurrences indexed by the arity, or alphabet size, $k$. Their counts grow like $(n!)^{k-1}$ times an exponential, times $\exp(cn^{1/3})$ with $c$ a multiple of the largest Airy zero $z=-2.338107410459767\ldots$, times a power of $n$. For $k=2$ the three counts are OEIS A082161, A254789 and A331120; for $k=3$ the relaxed counts are A082162, for $k=4$ A102102. The order of growth was known (Elvey Price, Fang and Wallner; Ghosh Dastidar and Wallner); the existence of a limiting amplitude was open. This report prints six manuscripts as four Parts. Part~\ref{air:part:rb} (relaxed binary trees) and Part~\ref{air:part:cb} (compacted binary trees) prove convergence to a positive amplitude and a Poincar\'e expansion to every fixed order in $n^{-1/3}$, with three explicit logarithmic corrections. Part~\ref{air:part:rb} uses an exact Jacobi symmetrization; Part~\ref{air:part:cb} treats the compacted recurrence as a signed delay whose scalar drift cancels, and shows that the $n^{-1/3}$ term cancels in the compacted-to-relaxed ratio. Part~\ref{air:part:ka} proves the same for every fixed $k\ge3$ and all three models, with common first two corrections, the first model-dependent defects, eventual decrease of the normalized ratios and a cancellation identity. Its ternary section gives the $k=3$ base proof, the third coefficient and a second route to the automaton amplitude; its comparison section gives the earliest, $\Theta$-level form of the positive completed-run comparison, with an explicit constant uniform in $k$ and $n$. Part~\ref{air:part:dfa} proves the binary automaton theorem, with power $n^{7/8}$, by the method of Part~\ref{air:part:cb}. Every Part inverts its expansion with Lambert $W$ and separates smooth inverses from integer thresholds. The amplitudes are proved positive but are not evaluated; no transseries, convergent series, uniformity in $k$ or certified decimal is claimed. The report is AI-assisted and unrefereed, and none of it is formalized.

[5 October 2026, batch 102.] Part~\ref{air:part:jp} adds three bundle reports that prove the binary theorems of Parts~\ref{air:part:rb}, \ref{air:part:cb} and~\ref{air:part:dfa} again, in even time, with a positive semidefinite two-step Jacobi product and three-lag signed memory. New with it: a proof that the relaxed amplitude is positive which uses no published lower bound (Lemma~\ref{air:jp:rb:lem:positive}); three further logarithmic coefficients for relaxed and compacted trees and one for automata; ratio expansions through $n^{-3}$ (trees) and $n^{-7/3}$ (automata); the next explicit term of the automaton inverse; and uncertified amplitude estimates, among them $76.438$ for automata.
\end{abstract}
\setcounter{tocdepth}{1}

\part*{Part 0. Guide: the family, its normalizations and this merge}
\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Part 0. Guide: the family, its normalizations and this merge}
\phantomsection\label{air:part:guide}
This guide was written when the six manuscripts were merged. Everything after it, from Part~\ref{air:part:rb} on, is the manuscripts' text, except for the passages marked \textbf{[write, 2 October 2026]} and the changes listed in ``Conventions of the merge'' below.

\section*{What the report proves}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What the report proves}
\phantomsection\label{air:guide:results}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Part~\ref{air:part:rb}} (source 62, relaxed binary trees, A082161). There is $\gamma>0$ with $a_n=\gamma n!4^ne^{3zn^{1/3}}n(1+\sum_{k\le M}c_kn^{-k/3}+O_M(n^{-(M+1)/3}))$ for every $M$, $c_k\in\Q[z]$; logarithmic corrections $\frac{53z^2}{90}$, $\frac{44z}{27}$, $\frac{141}{140}-\frac{1304z^3}{42525}$ (equations (F1)--(F3) in Section~\ref{air:rb:statement-and-scope}). Also the rational coefficient field, and Lambert-$W$ inverses with discrete threshold brackets.
\item \textbf{Part~\ref{air:part:cb}} (source 15; compacted binary trees, A254789). By Theorem~\ref{air:cb:thm:main}, $c_n=\gamma_cn!4^ne^{3zn^{1/3}}n^{3/4}(1+\sum\mathfrak c_kn^{-k/3}+O_M)$ with $\gamma_c>0$ and logarithmic corrections $\frac{53z^2}{90}$, $\frac{271z}{216}$, $\frac{393}{1120}-\frac{1304z^3}{42525}$; exact positive four-state, three-state and renewal models and their symmetry obstructions; the ratio corollary \eqref{air:cb:eq:ratio-log}, in which the $n^{-1/3}$ term of $\log(c_n/r_n)$ cancels; inverses. Appendix~\ref{air:app:jacobi} is its self-contained proof of the shared Jacobi estimates.
\item \textbf{Part~\ref{air:part:ka}} (sources 18, 66 and 29, every fixed arity $k\ge3$). Theorem~\ref{air:ka:thm:all}: positive amplitudes $A_k^R,A_k^C,A_k^B$ and expansions to every fixed order for relaxed, compacted and automaton counts, with the common corrections \eqref{air:ka:eq:c1}--\eqref{air:ka:eq:c2}. Theorem~\ref{air:ka:thm:ratio}: the first model-dependent defects $(q/k)^k/(k-2)\cdot n^{-(k-2)}$ and half of it, and eventual decrease of both normalized ratios. The cancellation identity \eqref{air:ka:eq:cancellation} and an inverse. The ternary section (source 66): the $k=3$ base proof, Theorems~\ref{air:t3:thm:main} and~\ref{air:t3:thm:orders} with the explicit $c_3$ and scalar coefficients $\mathfrak s_3,\ldots,\mathfrak s_6$, the explicit ternary weight with $1\le\omega\le3/2$, and the bridge-tail route to the automaton amplitude. The comparison section (source 29): Theorem~\ref{air:ka:al:thm:main}, the comparison $P_{k,n}\le B_n/(2^{n-1}R_n)\le1$ with $P_{k,n}=\prod_{j=2}^n(1-1/(2j^{k-1}))$ and $P_k\ge(2\sqrt2/\pi)\sin(\pi/\sqrt2)$; a uniform finite-level approximation; a $\Theta$-only inverse enclosure; and the boundary obstruction that the weights of the ternary section and of Lemma~\ref{air:ka:lem:weight} resolve.
\item \textbf{Part~\ref{air:part:dfa}} (source 48, binary finite-language automata, A331120). Theorem~\ref{air:dfa:thm:main}: $b_n=\gamma n!8^ne^{3zn^{1/3}}n^{7/8}(1+\sum\mathfrak c_kn^{-k/3}+O_M)$ with $\gamma>0$ and logarithmic corrections $\frac{53z^2}{90}$, $\frac{623z}{432}$, $\frac{3497}{4480}-\frac{1304z^3}{42525}$; a nonnegative renewal representation; a selected smooth inverse.
\end{itemize}
\begin{writenotejp}
\textbf{Part~\ref{air:part:jp}} (bundle Reports 100, 101, 103; relaxed trees, compacted trees, automata, $k=2$) proves the theorems of Parts~\ref{air:part:rb}, \ref{air:part:cb} and~\ref{air:part:dfa} a second time, in even time $n=N/2$. Report~100 proves the relaxed amplitude positive from the exact recurrence alone (Lemma~\ref{air:jp:rb:lem:positive}; Remark~\ref{air:jp:rem:positivity} applies it to Part~\ref{air:part:rb}) and gives the relaxed logarithmic coefficients $b_4,b_5,b_6$, the normalized ratio $r_n/(4nr_{n-1})$ through $n^{-3}$, a sharper endpoint law and a telescoping series for the projection constant. Report~101 proves the compacted $b_4,b_5,b_6$; with Corollary~\ref{air:jp:cor:compacted} the compacted ratio holds through $n^{-3}$. Report~103 gives the automaton $d_4=-5429z^2/19440$, $c_4$, $\sigma_7$, the ratio $b_n/(8nb_{n-1})$ through $n^{-7/3}$, the $X^{-1/3}$ term of the inverse and the uncertified amplitude estimate $76.438$. Section~\ref{air:jp:sec:map} maps every result to its counterpart here.
\end{writenotejp}

\section*{One family, indexed by the arity}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{One family, indexed by the arity}
\phantomsection\label{air:guide:family}
In the coordinates of Part~\ref{air:part:ka} (Section~\ref{air:ka:sec:arrays}), fix $k\ge2$, put $q=k-1$, and for $m\ge1$, $x\ge qm$ let
\begin{align*}
r(x,m)&=r(x,m-1)+(m+1)r(x-1,m),\\
c(x,m)&=c(x,m-1)+(m+1)c(x-1,m)-(m-1)c(x-k,m-1),\\
b(x,m)&=2b(x,m-1)+(m+1)b(x-1,m)-mb(x-k,m-1),
\end{align*}
with base rows $r(x,0)=c(x,0)=b(x,0)=1$ for $x\ge0$, the automaton auxiliary value $b(-1,0)=1$, and all other missing values zero. The relaxed, compacted and automaton counts are $R_n=r(qn,n)$, $C_n=c(qn,n)$ and $B_n=b(qn,n)$, where $n$ counts internal nodes of the trees and transient states of the automata (each automaton has one further, rejecting, sink). At $k=2$ the three recurrences are, letter for letter, Part~\ref{air:part:rb}'s $r_{n,m}$, Part~\ref{air:part:cb}'s \eqref{air:cb:eq:count} and Part~\ref{air:part:dfa}'s \eqref{air:dfa:eq:b-count}. The initial terms below were recomputed from these recurrences when the report was written.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lllll@{}}
\toprule
$k$ & model & OEIS & $n=0,1,2,\ldots$ & Part\\
\midrule
2 & relaxed & A082161 & $1,1,3,16,127,1363,18628,311250,\ldots$ & \ref{air:part:rb}\\
2 & compacted & A254789 & $1,1,3,15,111,1119,14487,230943,\ldots$ & \ref{air:part:cb}\\
2 & automata & A331120 & $1,1,6,60,900,18480,487560,15824880,\ldots$ & \ref{air:part:dfa}\\
3 & relaxed & A082162$^{a}$ & $1,1,7,139,5711,408354,45605881,\ldots$ & \ref{air:part:ka}\\
3 & compacted & --- & $1,1,7,133,5299,371329,40898599,\ldots$ & \ref{air:part:ka}\\
3 & automata & ---$^{b}$ & $1,1,14,532,42644,6011320,1330452032,\ldots$ & \ref{air:part:ka}\\
4 & relaxed & A102102 & $1,1,15,1000,189035,79278446,\ldots$ & \ref{air:part:ka}\\
4 & automata & ---$^{b}$ & $1,1,30,3900,1460700,1220162880,\ldots$ & \ref{air:part:ka}\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}

\smallskip
\raggedright\footnotesize $^{a}$The entry starts at $n=1$; the sources adjoin $R_0=1$. The array A128249 lists the relaxed counts of all arities (Section~\ref{air:ka:sec:results}).\\
$^{b}$Source 29's bounded search verified no OEIS identifier for automata over $k\ge3$ letters; A082162 counts relaxed ternary trees, not ternary automata.
\end{center}

\textbf{Where the method changes.} After normalization, the compacted and automaton recurrences are the relaxed one plus a delayed term (Part~\ref{air:part:ka}, equation~\eqref{air:ka:eq:delay}) whose relative size per step is $O_k(i^{-(k-1)})$ (Section~\ref{air:ka:subsec:global}); its cumulative effect on the logarithm is of order $n^{-(k-2)}$ (Theorem~\ref{air:ka:thm:ratio}). For $k\ge3$ this is summable, the completed-run products $P_C$, $P_B$ of \eqref{air:ka:eq:products} are positive, and the three models share the scale $\G_{k,n}=(n!)^q(k^k/q^q)^ne^{\gamma n^{1/3}}n^{(2k-1)/3}$ (automata with an extra $2^n$). For $k=2$ the delay is of order $N^{-1}$ and not summable: it changes the power of $n$ instead of adding a correction. Parts~\ref{air:part:cb} and~\ref{air:part:dfa} cancel the resulting scalar drift, $1/(4N)$ and $1/(8N)$, against the factors $N^{-1/4}$ and $N^{-1/8}$, which gives $n^{3/4}$ and $n^{7/8}$ in place of the relaxed $n$. Source 29 notes the same threshold from the other side: at $k=2$ its lower product tends to zero like $n^{-1/2}$. Source 48's remark on the exclusion family $(\alpha m+\beta)$, with heuristic power $1-\alpha/4$, is a heuristic it states without proof.

\textbf{The binary and fixed-arity coefficients agree where they should.} With $k=2$, $q=1$, Part~\ref{air:part:ka}'s general formulas \eqref{air:ka:eq:c1}--\eqref{air:ka:eq:c2} give $c_{2,1}=53z^2/90$ and $c_{2,2}=z(2809z^3+26400)/16200$, exactly Part~\ref{air:part:rb}'s $c_1,c_2$ in (F3), and with $k=3$ they give the $c_1,c_2$ of Theorem~\ref{air:t3:thm:orders}. Both reductions were checked symbolically (difference zero). Source 18's proof note \repopath{18-arity-all-orders-proof.md} observes the $k=2$ reduction of $c_{k,1}$ itself. Part~\ref{air:part:ka} claims nothing for $k=2$, and must not be read as doing so: at $k=2$ the compacted and automaton second coefficients differ from the relaxed one ($\frac{271z}{216}$ and $\frac{623z}{432}$ against $\frac{44z}{27}$ in the logarithm; the differences $-\frac38z$ and $-\frac3{16}z$ are in ratio two, as Theorem~\ref{air:ka:thm:ratio} has it for $k\ge3$, while the $n^{-1}$ differences $-\frac{21}{32}$ and $-\frac{29}{128}$ are not).

\section*{Normalization dictionary}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Normalization dictionary}
\phantomsection\label{air:guide:normalization}
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.2\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.36\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.36\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
Notion & Parts I, II, IV (sources 62, 15, 48) and source 29 & Part III (sources 18, 66)\\
\midrule
Largest Airy zero & $z=-2.338107410459767\ldots$ & $a$ (also $a_1$; $a_m$ the $m$th zero)\\
Scaled zero & $a:=2^{-1/3}z$ (not the zero itself; source 29 has none) & $\lambda:=a/B$, $B=(2/q)^{1/3}$; at $k=2$, $\lambda$ is Parts I, II, IV's $a$\\
Airy profile & $F(x)=\Ai(z+2^{1/3}x)$, $F''=2(x+a)F$ & $f(x)=\Ai(a+Bx)$, $f''=(2/q)(x+\lambda)f$; source 66: $B=1$, $f(x)=\Ai(a+x)$\\
Time, height & $N=n+m$, $j=n-m$; endpoint $N=2n$, $j=0$ & $i=x+m$, $j=x-qm$; endpoint $i=kn$, $j=0$\\
Normalized array & $d_{N,j}=r/((N+j)/2)!$ (I); $e_{N,j}$ (II, IV) & $d_i(j)=q^{2x}r(x,m)/x!$; at $q=1$ this is Part I's $d_{N,j}$\\
Stretched exponent & $3zn^{1/3}$ & $\gamma n^{1/3}$, $\gamma=3(kq/2)^{1/3}a$; source 29: $\beta_k$\\
Power of $n$ & $n$ (I), $n^{3/4}$ (II), $n^{7/8}$ (IV) & $n^{\eta}$, $\eta=(2k-1)/3$ (source 29: $\alpha_k$)\\
Amplitude & $\gamma$ (I, IV), $\gamma_c$ and $\gamma_r$ (II) & $A_k^R$, $A_k^C$, $A_k^B$ (18); $C_R$, $C_B$ (66)\\
Index $n$ & internal nodes (I, II); transient states (IV, 29) & internal nodes ($R_n$, $C_n$); transient states ($B_n$)\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Source 29 also counts by total states $N=n+1$ (its Remark on total states), which shifts the power of $n$ to $(2-k)/3$. Source 48's array $c_{n,m}=b_{n,m}/2^m$ is a normalized automaton array, not Part~\ref{air:part:cb}'s compacted count.
\begin{writenotejp}
Part~\ref{air:part:jp} uses a third normalization: the Airy zero is $a$ or $a_1$ (as in Part~\ref{air:part:ka}), $\kappa=\varkappa=2^{2/3}a$ is twice the $a$ of Parts~\ref{air:part:rb}, \ref{air:part:cb}, \ref{air:part:dfa}, time is halved ($n=N/2$, $\eps=n^{-1/3}$, half-grid $x_k=(k+\frac12)\eps$), and its scalar coefficients are $\sigma_m=2s_m$. Its own table of conventions is Section~\ref{air:jp:sec:notation}.
\end{writenotejp}

\textbf{One polynomial--Airy identity.} Every Part solves its formal profiles with the same identity. For $L=\partial_x^2-cx-\ell$ and polynomials $P,Q$,
\[
L(PF+QF')=\bigl(P''+2(cx+\ell)Q'+cQ\bigr)F+\bigl(2P'+Q''\bigr)F',
\]
which reduces $L(PF+QF')=RF+SF'$ to a triangular polynomial equation for $Q$. Parts~\ref{air:part:rb}, \ref{air:part:cb} and~\ref{air:part:dfa} use $c=2$, $\ell=2a$ (equation (F28) and \eqref{air:cb:eq:triangular}); source 66 uses $c=1$, $\ell=a$ (\eqref{air:t3:eq:K}); source 18's \eqref{air:ka:eq:polyinv} is the case $c=2/q$, $\ell=2\lambda/q$ after division by $q/2$. The batch-77 report on tree-child networks, \repopath{SetTheory/Cardinals/docs/reports/generating-functions-and-asymptotics/oeis-sequence-asymptotics/a213863-tree-child-networks}, proves the same identity with general $c,\ell$ for $d$-combining tree-child networks and uses the same zero-limit projection bootstrap; the two reports neither cite nor depend on each other.
\begin{writenotejp}
That report now also prints bundle Reports 104 and 102 (its Parts~III and~IV), of the same delivery series as Part~\ref{air:part:jp}'s Reports 100, 101 and 103. They use the same lemma family as Report~100: positive Jacobi products, the killed half-line kernel, the forced-product bootstrap, and the restart comparison of Lemma~\ref{air:jp:rb:lem:positive} for positivity. Report~102's ``companion relaxed-tree article'', which it says it does not need, is presumably Report~100. The reports stay separate.
\end{writenotejp}

\section*{Notation: one letter, several meanings}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Notation: one letter, several meanings}
\phantomsection\label{air:guide:notation}
No symbol of any source was renamed: each Part, and each source section of Part~\ref{air:part:ka}, keeps its source's letters. The sources use one letter for several notions and one notion under several letters. The table fixes the meaning in each Part and prints the tempting false reading beside the true one.
\begin{writenotejp}
The table covers Parts~\ref{air:part:rb}--\ref{air:part:dfa}. Part~\ref{air:part:jp} has its own (Section~\ref{air:jp:sec:notation}); the readings most likely to mislead there are $a$ (the Airy zero itself), $S_n$ (a diagonal matrix, not the Jacobi matrix), $\lambda_n\approx4$, and Report~103's $d_j$ (logarithmic) and $c_j$ (multiplicative).
\end{writenotejp}

{\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.11\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.5\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.33\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
Symbol & Meaning and where & Do not read it as\\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
Symbol & Meaning and where & Do not read it as\\
\midrule
\endhead
\bottomrule
\endfoot
$z$, $a$ & See the dictionary above. In Part~\ref{air:part:rb}, $a_n$ is the counting sequence and $a_1^{\operatorname{Ai}}$ the Airy zero. & Parts I, II, IV's $a$ is \emph{not} the Airy zero; Part III's $a$ is.\\
$\gamma$ & Parts I and IV: the positive amplitude. Part II: $\gamma_c$, with $\gamma_r$ the relaxed amplitude of Part I. Part III: $\gamma=3(kq/2)^{1/3}a<0$, the coefficient of $n^{1/3}$ (source 66: $\gamma=3\cdot3^{1/3}a$). Part I's (F23): $\gamma_E$ is Euler's constant. & Part III's $\gamma$ is not an amplitude.\\
$k$, $q$ & Part III: the arity $k\ge3$ and $q=k-1$. Parts I, II, IV: $k$ is an order index ($c_kn^{-k/3}$) or a run length ($w_m(k)$, $W_m(k)$); $q$ is $p\log x+C$ in the inverse sections, $q_0$ a limiting projection, $q$ an exponent in the bootstraps. Source 66: $p_j,q_j$ are row probabilities. & In Parts I, II, IV, $k$ is never an arity.\\
$c$, $\mathfrak c$ & Part II: $c_{n,m}$, $c_n$ compacted counts. Part IV: $c_{n,m}=b_{n,m}/2^m$. Part III: $c(x,m)$, $C_n$ compacted $k$-ary counts, $c_i(t)$ column sums, $c^X_{k,r}$ coefficients. Relative coefficients: $c_k$ (I), $\mathfrak c_k$ (II, IV), $c_r$ (66). & Part IV's $c_{n,m}$ is not a compacted count.\\
$b$, $B$ & $b_{N,j}$ Jacobi edge weights and $b_N=\ip{g_N}{B_Ng_{N-3}}$ (II, IV), $B_N$ the normalized delay (II, IV). Part IV: $b_{n,m}$, $b_n$ automaton counts. Part III: $b(x,m)$, $B_n$ automaton counts, $B=(2/q)^{1/3}$. Source 66: $b_i$ a scalar projection, $B$ a polynomial in $Af+Bf'$ and the weighted adjoint $B=\omega^{-1}T^*\omega$. Parts I, II: $B=53z^2/90$ in the inverse sections. & Part IV uses $b$ three ways, as its source did.\\
$\lambda$ & Parts I, II, IV: $\lambda_N$, the top eigenvalue of $S_N$ ($\approx2$). Part III: $\lambda=a/B$. Source 29: $\lambda_k=C_k^{1/q}/e$. & Not the same quantity in any two of these.\\
$R$, $P$ & Part III: $R_n$ relaxed counts; Parts II, IV: $R_N=P_NN^{-1/4}$, resp.\ $P_NN^{-1/8}$; Part I: $R(a,\eps)$, $R_m$. $P_N$ spectral product (I, II, IV); $P_C$, $P_B$, $P_i$ (18); $P_3$, $P_i$ (66); $P_{k,n}$, $P_k$ (29); $p=3/2$ (I) and $p=5/4$ (II) in the inverses, $p$ also the residual order. & \\
$C$, $A$ & $C$: matching constant $A_\infty/q_0$ (I; $C_*$ in II, IV), $\log(\gamma\sqrt{2\pi})$ in Part I's inverse, compacted counts $C_n$ (III), amplitudes $C_R,C_B$ (66), growth constant $C_k=2k^k/q^q$ (29). $A$: amplitudes $A_k^X$ and growth constant $k^k/q^q$ (III), $3z$ in the inverses (I, II), completed-run arrays $A(n,m)$, $A(x,m)$ (IV, 29), $A_\infty$ limiting projections. & Source 29's $C_k$ is a growth constant, not an amplitude.\\
$\eta$, $\alpha$, $\beta$ & $\eta=(2k-1)/3$ (18; $\alpha_k$ in 29); $\eta_N$ (II, IV); $\eta_i$ (66); $\eta_k=\alpha_k+q/2$ (29). $\alpha_N,\alpha_i$ projection errors; $\beta(x,\eps)$ delay coefficients (II, IV); $\beta=(7k-6)/6$, $\beta^X$ (18); $\beta_k$ (29); $\beta_i$ (66); $(\alpha,\beta)$ exclusion parameters (48). & \\
$\omega$, $h$ & $\omega$: a primitive cube root of unity (I, II, IV); the weight $\omega_j$ (III). $h$: $h_N$ parity vector, $h_k$ amplitude coefficients, $h(n)$ inverse model (I, II); $h_j=j+1$ (III). & \\
$\G$, $\mathfrak s$, $\sigma$ & $\mathcal G_k(a,x)$ normalized profiles (I); $\G_{k,n}$ the common scale (III). $s_m$ ratio coefficients (I, II, IV); $\sigma(\eps)$ scalar series and $\sigma_{i,m}$ singular values (III); source 66's $\mathfrak s(\eps)$ is Part III's $\sigma$ at $k=3$. & \\
$D$, $Q$, $L$, $U$, $W$ & $D_N$ gauge, $Q_N$ contraction (I, II, IV); $D_i$ delay and $Q_i$ kernel (III); $D=q(w+1)$ slope (III); $D_N$ total-state count (29). $L_N$ delay (II, IV); $L_q$ (III); $L=\log y$ (III). $U$ upward shift (II, IV); $U_i(j)$ up coefficients (III, 29). $W_0$ Lambert; $W_N$ transverse error (I, II, IV); $W_m$ run weights (IV, 29, 66). & \\
Thresholds & $N(X)$ (I), $N_c(Y)$ (II), $N(y)$ (18), $\nu(y)$ (66), $I_k(X)$ (29), ``the first integer index with $b_n\ge y$'' (IV): the least $n$ with count at least the target. & Not the inverse of a smooth model.\\
\end{longtable}
}

\textbf{Pins.} No source continues a repository path. Source 48's overlap receipt cites repository files at commit \texttt{a68b926cf}. Source 15's provenance note (shipped as \repopath{15-compacted-src-literature-and-provenance.md}) records a repository search at commit \texttt{89daa64f} that found no A254789 material, and source 29's raw search receipt (not shipped; see the README) refers to commit \texttt{4b874cea0}. All three commits are ancestors of the placement commits. Those searches were right when made: before this batch the repository had no report on any of these sequences. This report is now that material; the sentences in the Parts that report the empty searches are kept, with a note.

\textbf{Where the merge had to choose.}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Base and order.} Source 62 is the base: source 15 cites it, source 18's formulas reduce to it at $k=2$, and it treats the binary problem that Elvey Price, Fang and Wallner left open. Part~\ref{air:part:cb} follows because its ratio corollary uses Part~\ref{air:part:rb}. Part~\ref{air:part:ka} collects every fixed $k\ge3$. Part~\ref{air:part:dfa} is last because its source was placed by a later commit, into the Part number reserved for it; mathematically it is the automaton twin of Part~\ref{air:part:cb}.
\item \emph{One Jacobi appendix.} Source 15's appendix ``Self-contained Jacobi estimates'' is printed once, as Appendix~\ref{air:app:jacobi}. Source 48's appendix is the same text with one word changed (``DFA theorem'' for ``compacted theorem'') and is not reprinted. Source 62's own Sections~\ref{air:rb:exact-recurrence-and-contractive-symmetrization}--\ref{air:rb:convergence-of-the-scalar-amplitude}, which prove the same estimates in the base text's own words, are kept; the appendix is a second, self-contained write-up of them.
\item \emph{Part~\ref{air:part:dfa} prints what is specific to automata.} Source 48's Sections 3--8 and 10 follow Part~\ref{air:part:cb} line by line. Five passages are word for word Part~\ref{air:part:cb}'s: the definitions and Jacobi lemma of Section 3, the finite-memory lemma, the formal-equation paragraph of Section 7, the bootstrap of Section 8.2 and the rational-field paragraph of Section 10. They are replaced by pointers. Every display in which the automaton differs is printed: the renewal model, the delay coefficient with its $m=1$ term, the seeds, the drift $1/(8N)$, the exponents, the scalar and logarithmic coefficients, the amplitude formula and the inverse.
\item \emph{Source 66 inside Part~\ref{air:part:ka}.} Source 66 is the $k=3$ case of source 18 and the base proof that 18 generalizes: 18's proof notes say so and cite 66's proof file by hash (SHA-256 \texttt{18cdbc4e\ldots}, the file shipped as \repopath{66-ternary-amplitude-proof-candidate.md}). Its two theorems are kept in its section, marked as the $k=3$ instance of Theorems~\ref{air:ka:thm:all} and~\ref{air:ka:thm:ratio}, because the proof printed after them is that base proof. Its own additions are named there.
\item \emph{Source 29 inside Part~\ref{air:part:ka}.} The positive completed-run comparison appears three times in the sources: as source 29's main theorem (every $k\ge3$, explicit finite-$n$ constant, $\Theta$ only), as step \eqref{air:ka:eq:products} of source 18's proof (every $k\ge3$, infinite product) and as step \eqref{air:t3:eq:comparison} of source 66's ($k=3$). It is stated as a theorem once, Theorem~\ref{air:ka:al:thm:main}, credited to source 29 as its earliest form; the two proof steps carry pointers to it. Source 29's other results are printed in full.
\item \emph{Nothing else is omitted} except the title blocks, the tables of contents and the separate bibliographies (merged at the end). Each Part, and each source section of Part~\ref{air:part:ka}, opens with its source's abstract as delivered.
\end{enumerate}

\section*{How the Parts depend on one another}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{How the Parts depend on one another}
\phantomsection\label{air:guide:dependencies}
\begin{itemize}
\item Part~\ref{air:part:rb} is self-contained apart from the Elvey Price--Fang--Wallner lower bound \cite{epfw}, used for positivity.
\item Part~\ref{air:part:cb}'s main theorem is self-contained through Appendix~\ref{air:app:jacobi} and the same lower bound. Only its ratio corollary (Section~\ref{air:cb:sec:ratio}) uses Part~\ref{air:part:rb}'s (F2).
\item Part~\ref{air:part:ka} uses neither Part~\ref{air:part:rb} nor Part~\ref{air:part:cb}. Its only external input is the relaxed lower bound of Ghosh Dastidar and Wallner \cite{gdw}. Its general proof extends the ternary proof of source 66; the comparison of source 29 is a $\Theta$-level statement that the general theorem upgrades.
\item Part~\ref{air:part:dfa} uses Part~\ref{air:part:cb}'s Section~\ref{air:cb:sec:jacobi}, its finite-memory Lemma~\ref{air:cb:lem:memory}, its bootstrap and Appendix~\ref{air:app:jacobi}, together with the relaxed array of Part~\ref{air:part:rb} (for domination) and the Elvey Price--Fang--Wallner automaton lower bound \cite{dfa}.
\end{itemize}
\begin{writenotejp}
Part~\ref{air:part:jp} uses no earlier Part. Its Report~100 needs no external analytic input at all: Lemma~\ref{air:jp:rb:lem:positive} replaces the published lower bound, and by Remark~\ref{air:jp:rem:positivity} Part~\ref{air:part:rb}'s positivity step may rest on that lemma instead of \cite{epfw}. Report~101 uses Report~100's Sections 2--5 and the compacted lower bound of \cite{epfw}; Report~103 uses Report~100's Sections 2--5, the comparison $b_n\ge2^{n-1}c_n$ of \cite{dfa} and the same compacted lower bound.
\end{writenotejp}

\section*{Status, scope and non-claims}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Status, scope and non-claims}
\phantomsection\label{air:guide:status}
The six manuscripts are AI-assisted and unrefereed; each disclaims publication and peer review, and their audits are research verification. Their finite computations (exact recurrences, symbolic coefficient recursions, floating diagnostics) check algebra and indexing and are not proofs of the analytic steps. Nothing in this report is formalized in Lean or Rocq, and placing it in this repository confers no formal status.

The union of the sources' non-claims, all kept in the Parts:
\begin{itemize}
\item No amplitude is evaluated or enclosed. The decimals $166.9506$ and $166.9515$ (Part~\ref{air:part:rb}), $173.12105$ (Part~\ref{air:part:cb}) and the normalized ratios of Part~\ref{air:part:dfa} are evidence, not enclosures; Part~\ref{air:part:ka} gives no amplitude values at all.
\item Every expansion is a Poincar\'e expansion to each fixed order. No convergence of the series, Gevrey bound, optimal truncation, exponentially small term or transseries is claimed, and no canonical real interpolation of an integer sequence.
\item Part~\ref{air:part:ka} is not uniform in $k$ and proves eventual, not everywhere, decrease of the normalized ratios.
\item Inverses: smooth-model inverses, interpolation inverses and integer thresholds are kept apart; unspecified $O$-constants give eventual statements, not certified finite thresholds.
\item The growth orders are due to Elvey Price, Fang and Wallner \cite{epfw,dfa} and to Ghosh Dastidar and Wallner \cite{gdw}; the sources claim only the amplitudes, the expansions and what follows from them. Their literature and repository checks are bounded searches, not priority claims.
\item Source 66's ``no general-alphabet theorem'' and ``no compacted ternary result'', source 29's ``no limiting amplitude'', source 62's ``nothing on compacted trees'' and source 15's higher-arity question are statements about each source alone; other Parts of this report now supply those results, as notes in the Parts say.
\end{itemize}
\begin{writenotejp}
Part~\ref{air:part:jp} keeps all non-claims of its three reports and adds uncertified evidence: $166.9520924395$ and the extrapolation $166.9520895742200$ (relaxed), $173.1267049959$ and $173.1267048501473$ (compacted), all at or near $n=3000$ after six corrections, and $76.438$ for automata ($G^{(4)}_{3000}=76.4383234197$). None is an enclosure. Report~100's ``no compacted amplitude theorem'' and its conditional compacted inverse are statements about Report~100 alone; Report~101 and Part~\ref{air:part:cb} supply both.
\end{writenotejp}

\textbf{A correction of the literature that this report has not verified.} Sources 18 and 66 state that the proof of the high-path lemma of \cite{gdw} (Lemma~14 of arXiv v1, Lemma~15 of the published version) contains a false pointwise bound $U_i(j)\le q$ ($U_i(j)\le2$ for $k=3$; source 66 gives $U_4(1)=8/3$). For the coefficient $U_i(j)$ as defined in Part~\ref{air:part:ka} the inequality does fail near the boundary. Whether the published text prints it was not checked against the paper when this report was written. Neither source uses the bound: source 18 avoids it, and source 66 repairs the lemma's conclusion.

\section*{Instances of results elsewhere in the repository; neighbouring reports}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Instances of results elsewhere in the repository; neighbouring reports}
\phantomsection\label{air:guide:repository}
Every inverse section is an instance of results proved in the repository's transseries volume, \repopath{Analysis/Transseries/docs/series-and-transseries/Transseries_And_Inversion/}. No novelty is claimed for these methods.
\begin{itemize}
\item The Lambert anchors $x\log(4x/e)=T$ (Parts~\ref{air:part:rb}, \ref{air:part:cb}), $X(\log X+\log8-1)=Y$ (Part~\ref{air:part:dfa}) and $qn_0\log n_0+(\log A-q)n_0=L$ (Part~\ref{air:part:ka}, sources 18, 66, 29) solve the dominant block exactly, as in theorem \repopath{p0:thm:lambert-core}.
\item The explicit corrections of (I10)--(I11), \eqref{air:cb:eq:inverse-full}, \eqref{air:ka:eq:inverse}, \eqref{air:t3:eq:inverse1}, \eqref{air:ka:al:eq:nu} and \eqref{air:dfa:eq:first-inverse} are reversions of a perturbed core, as in theorem \repopath{p0:thm:perturbed-inversion}.
\item The threshold brackets (I18), \eqref{air:cb:eq:threshold-bracket} and \eqref{air:ka:al:eq:inverse}, and the rules $N=\lceil x\rceil$ of sources 18 and 66, are the exact-rounding and separation steps of theorem \repopath{p0:thm:staircase}. Its order-theoretic parts are formalized as \repopath{Fabius.staircase_ceil} and \repopath{Fabius.staircase_separation} in \repopath{Analysis/FabiusFunction/Lean/FabiusFunction/StaircaseInversion.lean}; these declarations verify the general rounding step only, nothing specific to this report.
\end{itemize}
\begin{writenotejp}
The inverse sections of Part~\ref{air:part:jp} (Sections~\ref{air:jp:rb:sec:inverse}, \ref{air:jp:cb:sec:inverse}, \ref{air:jp:dfa:sec:inverse}) are instances of the same three theorems: the anchors $x_0=Y/W_0(4Y/\ee)$ and $X=Y/W_0(8Y/\ee)$, the corrected inverses $z_\alpha$, $z$, $Z$ and \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:explicitinverse}, and the one-comparison threshold rules.
\end{writenotejp}
Neighbouring reports. The tree-child network report named above shares the polynomial--Airy lemma family. The batch-77 report \repopath{SetTheory/Cardinals/docs/reports/automata-and-formal-languages/accessible-and-strong-automata} counts other automata (accessible and strongly connected deterministic automata, in the Korshunov--Liskovets regime of inverse-power corrections); it has no stretched exponential and shares no lemma with this report. The Airy law of \repopath{a290268-unbounded-deficits}, in the same collection directory, is an additive fold from an integral representation, unrelated to the Airy zero here. (Added 7~October 2026, batch~108.) The report \repopath{a397711-bounded-indegree-dags}, in the same collection directory, meets the same Airy zero~$z$ for labelled acyclic digraphs with every indegree at most two (OEIS A397711): $\log a_n=2n\log n-n+3\cdot2^{-1/3}z\,n^{1/3}+o(n^{1/3})$, with the coefficient $3\cdot2^{-1/3}z\,(c-1)^{2/3}$ for every fixed indegree bound $c\ge2$, through an exact generalized-parking reduction to a Poisson walk with a potential of order $1/i$ and Mallein's homogeneous killed-walk (area-tilt) estimates, which it cites without reproof. Its precision is logarithmic only (no amplitude, power prefactor or second term), and its constant $2^{-1/3}z$ is the $a$ of Parts~\ref{air:part:rb}, \ref{air:part:cb} and~\ref{air:part:dfa}. It shares no lemma with this report, and nothing here is changed by it.

\part{Relaxed binary trees}\label{air:part:rb}

\begin{partsource}
Batch-77 manuscript 62, archive \repopath{relaxed-binary-trees-reproducibility.zip}, main file \repopath{article/relaxed-binary-trees.tex} (1,221 lines; 23-page PDF), titled ``Asymptotic amplitude and all orders for relaxed binary trees'', dated 2 October 2026, empty author line. The base of this report: its text is printed in full, with label prefix \texttt{air:rb:}. Its numbered list of references is merged into the bibliography at the end.
\end{partsource}

\paragraph{Abstract of the source.}
\begin{quote}\small
The number of relaxed binary trees is known to have order
$n!4^n\exp(3zn^{1/3})n$, where $z$ is the largest negative Airy zero.
We give a spectral argument for convergence to a positive limiting amplitude and for a full expansion in powers of $n^{-1/3}$.
An exact time-dependent gauge turns the counting recurrence into a symmetric Jacobi operator followed by a diagonal contraction.
A finite Airy quasimode, the continuum spectral gap, and summable ground-mode drift establish norm tracking.
A Catalan kernel estimate recovers the boundary coordinate.
Polynomial--Airy approximate solutions and a zero-limit projection bootstrap then transfer every finite formal order to the exact sequence.
The known lower bound is used to prove strict positivity of the amplitude.
We also derive Lambert $W$ inverse expansions and distinguish them from certified discrete threshold decisions.
The accompanying reproducibility archive checks the exact algebra and numerical evidence; its amplitude estimates are not rigorous enclosures.
\end{quote}


\section{Statement and scope}\label{air:rb:statement-and-scope}

Let \(a_n\) be OEIS A082161, the number of relaxed binary trees with \(n\) internal nodes. Its defining triangular recurrence is

\[
r_{n,m}=r_{n,m-1}+(m+1)r_{n-1,m},\qquad
r_{n,0}=1,\quad r_{n,m}=0\ (m>n),\quad a_n=r_{n,n}.
\]

Let \(z=a_1^{\operatorname{Ai}}=-2.338107410459767\ldots\) be the largest real zero of \(\operatorname{Ai}\). (This Airy zero is denoted by \(z\) throughout, to avoid confusing it with the counting sequence.) Set

\[
a=2^{-1/3}z,\qquad F(x)=\operatorname{Ai}(z+2^{1/3}x).
\]

\textbf{Theorem.} There is a constant \(\gamma >0\) such that, for each integer \(M\ge 0\),

\[
a_n=\gamma n!4^n e^{3zn^{1/3}}n
\left(1+\sum_{k=1}^M c_kn^{-k/3}
+O_M(n^{-(M+1)/3})\right).
\tag{F1}
\]

The coefficients belong to \(\mathbb Q[z]\) and are determined by the polynomial--Airy recursion in \S\ref{air:rb:formal-profiles-at-every-finite-order}. The first logarithmic coefficients are

\[
\log\frac{a_n}{\gamma n!4^n e^{3zn^{1/3}}n}
=\frac{53z^2}{90}n^{-1/3}
+\frac{44z}{27}n^{-2/3}
+\left(\frac{141}{140}-\frac{1304z^3}{42525}\right)n^{-1}
+O(n^{-4/3}).
\tag{F2}
\]

Consequently

\[
\begin{aligned}
c_1&=53z^2/90,\\
c_2&=z(2809z^3+26400)/16200,\\
c_3&=(1042139z^6+28444320z^3+30836700)/30618000.
\end{aligned}
\tag{F3}
\]

Numerically these are \(3.2193061325653844\), \(1.3717168742447035\), and \(-5.306490818002809\). No rigorous numerical enclosure or elementary evaluation of \(\gamma\) is asserted.
\begin{writenotejp}
Part~\ref{air:part:jp} (Report~100) proves this theorem a second time and continues (F2): with $z$ for its $a$, the next logarithmic coefficients are $b_4=-422z^2/1215$, $b_5=z(717281z^3-298772235)/344452500$ and $b_6=(2250428z^3-20962395)/41334300$, \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:brelaxed}, and the normalized ratio $a_n/(4na_{n-1})$ is expanded through $n^{-3}$ in \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:ratiorelaxed}.
\end{writenotejp}

The counting recurrence, the Θ estimate, and the conjectural limiting amplitude and all-orders shape are due to Andrew Elvey Price, Wenjie Fang, and Michael Wallner, \href{https://arxiv.org/pdf/1908.11181}{Compacted binary trees admit a stretched exponential} \cite{epfw}, especially Theorem 1.1 and §3.4. Their paper explicitly leaves existence of the amplitude open and reports the fit \(166.95208957\). The contribution here is a convergence/stability argument for the relaxed recurrence. It does not extend without further work to compacted trees, whose recurrence is different. It proves a Poincaré expansion to every finite algebraic order, not an exponentially improved transseries or a canonical interpolation between integers.

\section{Exact recurrence and contractive symmetrization}\label{air:rb:exact-recurrence-and-contractive-symmetrization}
\begin{writenote}
Sections~\ref{air:rb:exact-recurrence-and-contractive-symmetrization}--\ref{air:rb:convergence-of-the-scalar-amplitude} prove the gauge, gap, quasimode and projection estimates that Parts~\ref{air:part:cb} and~\ref{air:part:dfa} also use. Source 15 proves the same estimates again, self-contained and with the explicit matrices of Part~\ref{air:part:cb}, in its appendix, printed as Appendix~\ref{air:app:jacobi}; source 48 ships a verbatim copy of that appendix. Both write-ups are kept: this one is the base text, the appendix the version the later Parts cite.
\end{writenote}


Put

\[
d_{N,j}=\frac{r_{(N+j)/2,(N-j)/2}}{((N+j)/2)!}
\]

when \(0\le j\le N\) and \(j\equiv N\pmod 2\), and zero otherwise. Direct substitution gives

\[
d_{0,j}=\mathbf 1_{j=0},\qquad d_{N,-1}=0,
\qquad
d_{N,j}=\frac{N-j+2}{N+j}d_{N-1,j-1}+d_{N-1,j+1}.
\tag{F4}
\]

In particular \(a_n=n!d_{2n,0}\). Values outside the admissible support are interpreted as zero.

For \(N\ge 1\), define on \(0\le j\le N+1\)

\[
D_N(j)^2=\frac{\Gamma(N+2)\Gamma(N+1)}
{\Gamma(N-j+2)\Gamma(N+j+1)},\qquad D_N(0)=1,
\]

and \(v_N(j)=d_{N,j}/D_N(j)\). All vectors are padded with zeros in \(\ell ^2(\mathbb Z_{\ge 0})\). Define \(S_N\) to be the symmetric Jacobi matrix on \(0,\ldots ,N+1\), padded by zero, with edge weights

\[
b_{N,j}=\sqrt{\frac{N-j+2}{N+j}},\quad 1\le j\le N+1,
\]

and define a diagonal contraction by

\[
Q_N(j)=\sqrt{1-\frac{j(j-1)}{N(N+1)}}
\quad(0\le j\le N+1),\qquad Q_N(j)=0\quad(j>N+1).
\]

The factorial identities

\[
\frac{D_N(j)}{D_N(j-1)}=b_{N,j},\qquad
\frac{D_{N-1}(j)}{D_N(j)}=Q_N(j)
\]

on the support where the second ratio is needed establish the exact identity

\[
v_N=S_NQ_Nv_{N-1}.
\tag{F5}
\]

Both \(b\) and \(Q\) lie in \([0,1]\). If \(J\) denotes the half-line adjacency matrix, then \(0\le S_NQ_N\le J\) entrywise. If \(\lambda _N\) is the largest eigenvalue of \(S_N\), then \(\Vert S_NQ_N\Vert \le \lambda _N\). The finite Jacobi matrix is irreducible, so its top eigenvector \(\psi _N\) can be taken positive and of norm one. Its spectrum is symmetric about zero because it exchanges the two parity subspaces. In particular its operator norm is \(\lambda _N\).

\section{The coarse Airy limit and a uniform spectral gap}\label{air:rb:the-coarse-airy-limit-and-a-uniform-spectral-gap}

Write \(\varepsilon =N^{-1/3}\) and \(x_j=(j+1)\varepsilon\). For vectors supported on the finite interval \(0,\ldots ,N+1\), the quadratic form is exactly

\[
\begin{aligned}
\langle v,(2I-S_N)v\rangle={}&
\sum_{j=0}^{N}b_{N,j+1}|v_{j+1}-v_j|^2+|v_0|^2\\
&+\sum_{j=1}^{N}(2-b_{N,j}-b_{N,j+1})|v_j|^2
+(2-b_{N,N+1})|v_{N+1}|^2.
\end{aligned}
\tag{F6}
\]

Here \(|v_0|^2\) is the first Dirichlet-gradient interval, with fictitious value \(v_{-1}=0\); no nonphysical edge coefficient \(b_{N,0}\) is introduced.

For \(1\le j\le N+1\),

\[
1-b_{N,j}
=\frac{2(j-1)}{(N+j)(1+b_{N,j})}
\ge\frac{j-1}{N+j}.
\]

It follows from (F6) that, for universal positive constants \(c,C\),

\[
\varepsilon^{-2}\langle v,(2I-S_N)v\rangle
\ge c\sum_{j=0}^{N+1}x_j|v_j|^2-C\varepsilon\|v\|^2.
\tag{F7}
\]

On each fixed \(x\) window the edge weights tend uniformly to one, and

\[
\varepsilon^{-2}(2-b_{N,j}-b_{N,j+1})\longrightarrow 2x_j
\]

uniformly there. Interpolate linearly with value \(\varepsilon ^{-1/2}v_j\) at \(x_j\) and zero at zero. Add one zero node immediately after the final finite node and extend by zero thereafter; the endpoint term in (F6) controls this final interpolation interval. The gradient terms in (F6) give local \(H^1\) bounds for any sequence with bounded scaled energy. Equation (F7) gives uniformly small \(L^2\) tails outside a large fixed window. Local compactness, followed by this tail bound, gives strong \(L^2\) compactness. The boundary interval enforces the trace zero at zero. The resulting liminf form is

\[
\int_0^\infty (|f'(x)|^2+2x|f(x)|^2)\,dx.
\]

For completeness, convergence of norms in this interpolation follows first on a fixed window from the local gradient bound and the Riemann-sum identity, and then globally by (F7). Sampling smooth compactly supported functions with zero boundary value gives the reverse, limsup bound. Applying the elementary min--max principle in both directions therefore gives convergence of each fixed low eigenvalue of \(\varepsilon ^{-2}(2I-S_N)\) to that of

\[
\mathcal H=-\frac{d^2}{dx^2}+2x,
\qquad f(0)=0.
\]

The latter eigenvalues are \(\mu _k=-2^{2/3}z_k\), where \(z_1=z>z_2>\ldots\) are the negative Airy zeros. Thus, if \(\lambda _{2,N}\) is the second-largest positive eigenvalue for large \(N\),

\[
\lambda_N=2+2a\varepsilon^2+o(\varepsilon^2),\qquad
\lambda_N-\lambda_{2,N}
\sim 2^{2/3}(z-z_2)\varepsilon^2.
\tag{F8}
\]

As a map between opposite parity subspaces, \(S_N\) has singular values equal to its positive eigenvalues (and possibly additional zeros). Hence, after deleting its top singular direction, its norm divided by \(\lambda _N\) is at most \(1-cN^{-2/3}\) for all sufficiently large \(N\). This parity formulation is essential: the negative eigenvalue \(-\lambda _N\) is not a second slow direction within the occupied parity space.

\section{Quantitative estimates from a finite quasimode}\label{air:rb:quantitative-estimates-from-a-finite-quasimode}

The Airy equation reads \(F''=2(x+a)F\). Define

\[
\begin{aligned}
F_2(x)&=\frac{2(-a+2x)}{15}F(x)
+\frac{x(2a+x)}{15}F'(x),\\
F_3(x)&=\frac{F(x)-xF'(x)}{3},\\
f_N(x)&=F(x)+\varepsilon^2F_2(x)+\varepsilon^3F_3(x),\\
\widehat\lambda_N&=2+2a\varepsilon^2+3\varepsilon^3
+\frac{13a^2}{15}\varepsilon^4+\frac{5a}{3}\varepsilon^5.
\end{aligned}
\tag{F9}
\]

Choose a fixed smooth cutoff \(\chi\), equal to one on \([0,1]\) and zero on \([2,\infty )\), and sample \(\chi (j/N^{1/2})f_N(x_j)\). Let \(\widehat f_N\) denote its \(\ell ^2\) normalization. Changing or omitting the cutoff in local calculations produces an error smaller than every inverse power of \(N\).

Here is an explicit way to check and bound the quasimode residual. At a sample point the two edge coefficients are

\[
b_-(x,\varepsilon)=\sqrt{\frac{1-x\varepsilon^2+3\varepsilon^3}
{1+x\varepsilon^2-\varepsilon^3}},\qquad
b_+(x,\varepsilon)=\sqrt{\frac{1-x\varepsilon^2+2\varepsilon^3}
{1+x\varepsilon^2}}.
\]

Expand \(b_- f_N(x-\varepsilon )+b_+ f_N(x+\varepsilon )-\widehat\lambda _N f_N(x)\) through degree five in \(\varepsilon\), reducing every derivative using \(F''=2(x+a)F\). Every coefficient vanishes. At the left boundary the omitted sample is zero because \(F(0)=F_2(0)=F_3(0)=0\). On the support of the cutoff, \(x\varepsilon ^2=O(N^{-1/2})\); Taylor remainders are bounded by \(C\varepsilon ^6\) times a fixed polynomial in \(x\) multiplying Airy decay. All such polynomial--Airy envelopes are square-integrable. Derivatives of the cutoff contribute only \(\exp(-cN^{1/4})\) times a power of \(N\). Since the unnormalized sampled norm is asymptotic to \(\varepsilon ^{-1/2}\Vert F\Vert _{L^2}\), this proves

\[
\|(S_N-\widehat\lambda_N)\widehat f_N\|=O(\varepsilon^6).
\tag{F10}
\]

The Airy gap (F8) identifies the nearby eigenvalue with \(\lambda _N\); projecting the residual on the complement of \(\psi _N\) then gives

\[
\lambda_N=\widehat\lambda_N+O(\varepsilon^6),\qquad
\|\psi_N-\widehat f_N\|=O(\varepsilon^4).
\tag{F11}
\]

The sign is fixed by the positive leading Airy profile. Several useful consequences follow without a separate quantitative localization theorem:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\|\psi_N-\psi_{N-1}\|&=O(N^{-1}),\\
\|(I-Q_N)\psi_N\|&=O(N^{-4/3}),\\
\psi_N(0)&=\sqrt2\,N^{-1/2}(1+O(N^{-1/3})).
\end{aligned}
\tag{F12}
\]

For the first, the explicitly normalized samples in (F9) have \(\ell ^2\) derivative \(O(N^{-1})\) with respect to continuous \(N\); the two approximation errors in (F11) are smaller. For the second, use \(1-\sqrt{1-t}\le t\) for \(0\le t\le 1\), the finite polynomial moments of the sampled Airy profile, and (F11). For the endpoint, the pointwise error is at most the \(\ell ^2\) error, and

\[
I:=\int_0^\infty F(x)^2\,dx=2^{-1/3}\operatorname{Ai}'(z)^2,
\qquad \frac{F'(0)}{\sqrt I}=\sqrt2.
\]

A Riemann-sum estimate with an integrable derivative gives a relative \(O(\varepsilon )\) normalization error; the endpoint Taylor error is \(O(\varepsilon ^2)\) and the normalized quasimode error divided by the leading endpoint is \(O(\varepsilon ^{5/2})\). These identities therefore verify the quantitative endpoint estimate in (F12). Standard Airy decay needed above follows, for example, directly from the decaying solution of the Airy ODE; only its polynomially weighted integrability and exponential tail are used.

\section{Convergence of the scalar amplitude}\label{air:rb:convergence-of-the-scalar-amplitude}

Set

\[
P_N=\prod_{i=1}^N\lambda_i,\qquad
u_N=v_N/P_N,\qquad M_N=S_NQ_N/\lambda_N.
\]

Then \(u_N=M_Nu_{N-1}\), \(\Vert M_N\Vert \le 1\), and \(\Vert u_N\Vert \le 1\). Let \(E_p\) be projection onto height parity \(p\). The positive eigenvector has equal squared mass on the two parities: this follows by taking the scalar product of each half of \(S_N\psi _N=\lambda _N\psi _N\) with that half. Therefore

\[
g_N=\sqrt2 E_{N\bmod2}\psi_N,\qquad
h_N=\sqrt2 E_{(N-1)\bmod2}\psi_N
\]

are unit vectors, with \(S_Nh_N=\lambda _Ng_N\). By (F12),

\[
\|h_N-g_{N-1}\|=O(N^{-1}),\qquad
\|(I-Q_N)h_N\|+\|(I-Q_N)g_{N-1}\|=O(N^{-4/3}).
\tag{F13}
\]

The second estimate for \(g_{N-1}\) uses the first estimate only for its approximation by explicit Airy samples, rather than the crude \(O(N^{-1})\) difference: direct use of (F11) at \(N-1\) gives the stated \(O(N^{-4/3})\) estimate with \(Q_N\). It also follows by observing that \(Q_N\ge Q_{N-1}\) pointwise on the old support and treating its negligible eigenvector tail.

Decompose

\[
u_N=A_Ng_N+w_N,\qquad w_N\perp g_N.
\]

Write \(\Pi _N=I-|g_N\rangle \langle g_N|\) on the occupied parity subspace. The singular gap from \S\ref{air:rb:the-coarse-airy-limit-and-a-uniform-spectral-gap} gives, for every vector \(y\) on the preceding parity,

\[
\|\Pi_NM_Ny\|
\le (1-cN^{-2/3})\|Q_Ny\|
\le (1-cN^{-2/3})\|y\|.
\tag{F14}
\]

Indeed \(\Pi _NS_N=S_N(I-|h_N\rangle \langle h_N|)\) on that parity; this projection removes the top singular direction, regardless of whether \(y\) is perpendicular to \(h_N\). Also, (F13) implies

\[
\|M_Ng_{N-1}-g_N\|=O(N^{-1}).
\]

Applying (F14) to \(w_{N-1}\) and the latter estimate to the scalar term yields

\[
\|w_N\|\le (1-cN^{-2/3})\|w_{N-1}\|+CN^{-1}.
\tag{F15}
\]

Iteration gives \(\Vert w_N\Vert =O(N^{-1/3})\). One general estimate used here and later is

\[
\sum_{k=N_0}^N k^{-r}
\prod_{i=k+1}^N(1-ci^{-2/3})=O_r(N^{2/3-r}).
\tag{F16}
\]

To prove it, split at \(N/2\). The earlier half is exponentially small in \(N^{1/3}\) times a polynomial; on the later half bound the product by \(\exp(-c'(N-k)N^{-2/3})\) and sum a geometric series. A finite initial segment makes no difference.

The scalar equation is

\[
A_N=t_NA_{N-1}+\langle k_N,w_{N-1}\rangle,
\quad
t_N=\langle Q_Nh_N,g_{N-1}\rangle,
\quad k_N=Q_Nh_N-g_{N-1}.
\tag{F17}
\]

The unit-vector identity \(\langle h,g\rangle =1-\Vert h-g\Vert ^2/2\) and (F13) show

\[
t_N=1+O(N^{-4/3}),\qquad \|k_N\|=O(N^{-1}).
\tag{F18}
\]

Since \(|A_N|\le 1\), (F15), (F17), and (F18) give

\[
A_N-A_{N-1}=O(N^{-4/3}).
\]

Thus

\[
A_N=A_\infty+O(N^{-1/3}).
\tag{F19}
\]

Positivity gives \(A_N\ge 0\) and hence \(A_\infty \ge 0\); strict positivity is established in \S\ref{air:rb:the-limiting-constant-and-its-strict-positivity}.

\section{A boundary smoothing estimate}\label{air:rb:a-boundary-smoothing-estimate}

Norm tracking alone cannot read a coordinate whose leading size is \(N^{-1/2}\). A short final block of the original positive kernel provides the missing smoothing.

Let \(U(N,k)=M_N\cdots M_{k+1}\). For \(0\le \ell \le N^{2/3}\), positivity and \(S_iQ_i\le J\) give

\[
\|e_0^TU(N,N-\ell)\|_2
\le\frac{\|e_0^TJ^\ell\|_2}
{\lambda_{N-\ell+1}\cdots\lambda_N}.
\]

The squared numerator is the number of nonnegative walks of length \(2\ell\) returning to zero:

\[
\|e_0^TJ^\ell\|_2^2=(J^{2\ell})_{0,0}
=\frac{1}{\ell+1}\binom{2\ell}{\ell}
\le C4^\ell(\ell+1)^{-3/2}.
\]

Equation (F11) implies

\[
\frac{2^\ell}{\lambda_{N-\ell+1}\cdots\lambda_N}
\le\exp(C\ell N^{-2/3})\le C'
\]

over the same range. Therefore

\[
\|e_0^TU(N,N-\ell)\|_2\le C(\ell+1)^{-3/4}.
\tag{F20}
\]

The exact error recurrence for \(w_N\) is

\[
w_N=M_Nw_{N-1}+f_N^{\rm err},\qquad
f_N^{\rm err}=A_{N-1}M_Ng_{N-1}-A_Ng_N,
\quad \|f_N^{\rm err}\|=O(N^{-1}).
\]

Use Duhamel over \(L=\lfloor N^{2/3}\rfloor\) steps. Since all times in this block are comparable with \(N\), (F15) and (F20) imply

\[
\begin{aligned}
|w_N(0)|
&\le CL^{-3/4}\|w_{N-L}\|
+CN^{-1}\sum_{\ell=0}^{L-1}(\ell+1)^{-3/4}\\
&=O(N^{-5/6}).
\end{aligned}
\tag{F21}
\]

For even \(N\), (F12) gives \(g_N(0)=2N^{-1/2}(1+O(N^{-1/3}))\).

\section{The limiting constant and its strict positivity}\label{air:rb:the-limiting-constant-and-its-strict-positivity}

From (F11),

\[
\log\lambda_N=\log2+aN^{-2/3}+\frac32N^{-1}+O(N^{-4/3}).
\]

Summation gives

\[
P_N=\kappa\,2^N e^{3aN^{1/3}}N^{3/2}(1+O(N^{-1/3})),
\qquad \kappa>0.
\tag{F22}
\]

If desired, a convergent expression is

\[
\log\kappa=a\zeta(2/3)+\tfrac32\gamma_E
+\sum_{N=1}^\infty\left[
\log(\lambda_N/2)-aN^{-2/3}-\frac{3}{2N}\right].
\tag{F23}
\]

Here \(\gamma _E\) is Euler's constant, not the counting amplitude. This is an existence formula; it does not by itself give efficient certified numerical evaluation.

Equations (F19)--(F22), for even \(N\), now give

\[
d_{N,0}=2\kappa A_\infty\,2^N e^{3aN^{1/3}}N
+O\left(2^N e^{3aN^{1/3}}N^{2/3}\right).
\tag{F24}
\]

The known lower half of the Elvey Price--Fang--Wallner Θ theorem states that \(d_{2n,0}\) is bounded below by a positive constant times \(4^n e^{3zn^{1/3}}n\) for all sufficiently large \(n\). If \(A_\infty =0\), (F24) contradicts that bound. Hence \(A_\infty >0\) and

\[
\gamma=4\kappa A_\infty>0.
\tag{F25}
\]

Taking \(N=2n\) in (F24) proves (F1) with \(M=0\). This completes the amplitude-convergence argument, using the explicit prior lower-bound theorem and the elementary Airy facts used above.
\begin{writenotejp}
The prior lower bound is no longer needed here. Part~\ref{air:part:jp}'s Lemma~\ref{air:jp:rb:lem:positive} (Report~100) proves the relaxed amplitude positive from the exact recurrence alone, by restarting the positive contractive evolution from its Perron vector, and Remark~\ref{air:jp:rem:positivity} shows that (F24) and that lemma give $A_\infty=\gamma_r/(4\kappa)>0$ and $\gamma=\gamma_r$. With this substitution the relaxed theorem rests on no external analytic input (Report~100 alone already proves it so), and the Elvey Price--Fang--Wallner relaxed $\Theta$ theorem itself becomes a consequence.
\end{writenotejp}

\section{Formal profiles at every finite order}\label{air:rb:formal-profiles-at-every-finite-order}

Work now with the original recurrence (F4), rather than the symmetric gauge. Seek

\[
Z_{N,j}=H_N\sum_{k=0}^K\varepsilon^kG_k(x_j),\qquad
G_0=F,\quad G_k(0)=G_k'(0)=0\ (k\ge1),
\]

with

\[
\frac{H_N}{H_{N-1}}
\sim2\left(1+\sum_{m\ge2}s_m\varepsilon^m\right).
\tag{F26}
\]

At the previous time the two arguments and powers are

\[
y_\pm=(x\pm\varepsilon)(1-\varepsilon^3)^{-1/3},
\qquad
\varepsilon_{N-1}^k=\varepsilon^k(1-\varepsilon^3)^{-k/3},
\]

and the downward coefficient is

\[
\frac{1-x\varepsilon^2+3\varepsilon^3}
{1+x\varepsilon^2-\varepsilon^3}.
\]

After the leading orders, the coefficient at degree \(m\) has the form

\[
\mathcal LG_{m-2}-2s_mF+R_m(x)F+S_m(x)F',
\quad
\mathcal L=\frac{d^2}{dx^2}-2(x+a),
\tag{F27}
\]

where \(R_m,S_m\) are known polynomials over \(\mathbb Q[a]\), formed from earlier orders. In particular \(s_2=a\). This form follows by Taylor expansion: the new profile first appears through the second-order spatial operator; its lower, zeroth-order terms cancel between the two sides of (F4). Time-change terms involving this new profile enter only at later degree.

To solve \(\mathcal L(PF+QF')=RF+SF'\), compute

\[
\mathcal L(PF+QF')
=(P''+4(x+a)Q'+2Q)F+(2P'+Q'')F'.
\]

Eliminating \(P'=(S-Q'')/2\) reduces the problem to

\[
-\tfrac12 Q'''+4(x+a)Q'+2Q=R-\tfrac12S'.
\tag{F28}
\]

On polynomials of degree at most \(d\), the operator on the left is upper triangular by degree with nonzero diagonal entries \(4j+2\), \(0\le j\le d\); hence it is invertible. The new scalar \(s_m\) adds \(2s_m\) to the forcing \(R\), thus adds exactly \(s_m\) to the constant term of \(Q\). Choose it uniquely to enforce \(Q(0)=0\), which is the boundary condition. Integrating \(P'\) leaves one free constant; choose it so \(P(0)+Q'(0)=0\), which enforces the derivative normalization. This proves existence and uniqueness at every formal order within the stated normalization, not merely consistency of finitely many guessed coefficients.
\begin{writenote}
This polynomial--Airy identity, with its triangular reduction, is used by every Part of this report and by the tree-child network report; see ``One polynomial--Airy identity'' in Part~0.
\end{writenote}


The first scalar coefficients are

\[
s_2=a,\quad s_3=\frac43,\quad s_4=\frac{29a^2}{270},\quad
s_5=-\frac{23a}{81},\quad
s_6=\frac{74385-8783a^3}{85050}.
\tag{F29}
\]

For example,

\[
G_1=-\tfrac23x^2F,\quad
G_2=\frac{2(2a+15x^4+111x)}{135}F
+\frac{x(-8a+51x)}{270}F'.
\]

The amplitude factor can be chosen as a genuine smooth function of \(N\) with finite logarithmic expansion

\[
\log H_N=N\log2+3aN^{1/3}+\frac43\log N
+\sum_{k=1}^J h_kN^{-k/3}.
\tag{F30}
\]

Expanding its exact one-step ratio matches any prescribed finite number of coefficients in (F26). At degree \(\varepsilon ^{k+3}\), the new \(h_k\) has coefficient \(-k/3\), so the construction is again triangular and nonsingular. Crucially the leading multiplicative constant in (F30) is fixed to one for every truncation. This avoids an artificial truncation-dependent amplitude.

\section{Approximate solutions in the normalized norm}\label{air:rb:approximate-solutions-in-the-normalized-norm}

Fix any \(p>1\). Take enough orders in \S\ref{air:rb:formal-profiles-at-every-finite-order}, multiply the sampled profile by the cutoff \(\chi (j/N^{1/2})\), and restrict to the occupied parity. Call the result \(Z_N\). Choose the truncation so that its local formal residual starts at a power \(N^{-p'}\) with \(p'\ge p\). Define

\[
z_N=D_N^{-1}Z_N/P_N.
\]

Then

\[
\|z_N\|=O(1),\qquad
r_N:=z_N-M_Nz_{N-1}=O(N^{-p})\quad\hbox{in }\ell^2.
\tag{F31}
\]

Here are the needed global bounds. On \(j\le 2N^{1/2}\), the product formula for \(D_N\) gives \(D_N^{-1}\le C\) uniformly: \(-\log D_N(j)\le Cj^2/N\le C'\). On any fixed scaled window \(D_N^{-1}=1+O(N^{-1/3}(1+x)^2)\). Taylor remainders in the rational coefficients and the time dilation are bounded by \(N^{-p}\) times a fixed polynomial--Airy envelope on the cutoff support. Cutoff errors decay like \(\exp(-cN^{1/4})\) times a polynomial, also after multiplication by \(D_N^{-1}\). Finally,

\[
H_N/P_N\sim\kappa^{-1}N^{-1/6},
\]

while a parity-restricted sampled profile has norm asymptotic to \(N^{1/6}\sqrt{I/2}\). These bounds prove (F31) by direct summation. The same reasoning proves

\[
\langle g_N,z_N\rangle\longrightarrow
q_0:=\kappa^{-1}\sqrt{I/2}>0.
\tag{F32}
\]

Indeed, after the natural sample normalization, both profiles converge to the same positive Airy function. Neither (F31) nor (F32) needs a pointwise estimate on the unknown solution.

\section{A scalar bootstrap to every finite order}\label{air:rb:a-scalar-bootstrap-to-every-finite-order}

Set

\[
C=A_\infty/q_0,\qquad e_N=u_N-Cz_N.
\]

Then \(e_N=M_Ne_{N-1}-Cr_N\) and the ground projection tends to zero. Decompose

\[
e_N=\alpha_Ng_N+W_N,\qquad W_N\perp g_N,
\qquad \alpha_N\longrightarrow0.
\]

The exact same estimates (F14), (F17), and (F18) now give

\[
\begin{aligned}
\|W_N\|&\le(1-cN^{-2/3})\|W_{N-1}\|
+CN^{-1}|\alpha_{N-1}|+CN^{-p},\\
|\alpha_N-\alpha_{N-1}|&\le
CN^{-4/3}|\alpha_{N-1}|+CN^{-1}\|W_{N-1}\|+CN^{-p}.
\end{aligned}
\tag{F33}
\]

Suppose initially that \(\alpha _N=O(N^{-q})\), where \(q\ge 0\). Formula (F16) gives

\[
\|W_N\|=O(N^{-q-1/3}+N^{2/3-p}).
\]

Substitute this into the second inequality of (F33). Its right-hand side is summable because \(p>1\); summing from \(N+1\) to infinity and using the zero limiting projection gives

\[
|\alpha_N|=O(N^{-q-1/3}+N^{1-p}).
\tag{F34}
\]

The smaller tail \(O(N^{2/3-p})\) has been absorbed by \(O(N^{1-p})\). Start with \(q=0\), which holds since \(u_N,z_N\) are bounded. Finitely many repetitions of (F34) reach

\[
\alpha_N=O(N^{1-p}),\qquad
\|W_N\|=O(N^{2/3-p}),\qquad
\|e_N\|=O(N^{1-p}).
\tag{F35}
\]

There is no boundary-at-infinity assumption beyond the already proved, explicit limit (F32) and the choice of \(C\). No inverse operator with a small gap is silently assumed uniformly bounded.

Apply Duhamel directly to the inhomogeneous \(e\) recurrence on the final block \(L=\lfloor N^{2/3}\rfloor\). By (F20), (F31), and (F35),

\[
\begin{aligned}
|e_N(0)|&\le CL^{-3/4}N^{1-p}
+CN^{-p}\sum_{\ell=0}^{L-1}(\ell+1)^{-3/4}\\
&=O(N^{1/2-p})+O(N^{1/6-p})
=O(N^{1/2-p}).
\end{aligned}
\tag{F36}
\]

The main endpoint is of size \(N^{-1/2}\), so the relative endpoint error is \(O(N^{1-p})\). For the remainder at order \(M\), take \(p\ge 1+(M+1)/3\) and enough formal terms. Since \(p\) can be taken as large as desired, every prescribed finite algebraic order of the explicit approximation transfers to the exact sequence. All truncations have the same \(q_0\), hence the same \(C\) and the same \(\gamma =2CF'(0)=4\kappa A_\infty\). This proves (F1).

\section{Coefficient extraction and computational checks}\label{air:rb:coefficient-extraction-and-computational-checks}

Solving the scalar ratio matching (F30) using (F29) gives

\[
h_1=53a^2/45,\qquad h_2=79a/27,\qquad
h_3=143/210-5216a^3/42525.
\]

The normalized endpoint profile is

\[
\frac{\sum_k\varepsilon^kG_k(\varepsilon)}{F'(0)\varepsilon}
=1+\frac a3\varepsilon^2+\frac43\varepsilon^3+O(\varepsilon^4).
\]

Consequently the logarithmic endpoint corrections in time \(N\) are

\[
\frac{53a^2}{45}N^{-1/3}
+\frac{88a}{27}N^{-2/3}
+\left(\frac{141}{70}-\frac{5216a^3}{42525}\right)N^{-1}.
\]

Substituting \(N=2n\) and \(a=2^{-1/3}z\) yields (F2), and exponentiation gives (F3).

\section{The rational coefficient field}\label{air:rb:the-rational-coefficient-field}

The recursion immediately puts every coefficient at time \(N\) in \(\mathbb Q[a]\), where \(a=2^{-1/3}z\). The substitution \(N=2n\) initially gives the weaker field \(\mathbb Q(2^{1/3})[z]\). To justify the stronger field \(\mathbb Q[z]\) asserted in the theorem, one needs the following mod-3 grading.

Let \(\phi_a\) be the formal power-series solution of

\[
\phi_a''=2(x+a)\phi_a,\qquad \phi_a(0)=0,\quad\phi_a'(0)=1.
\]

Every coefficient of \(\phi_a\) belongs to \(\mathbb Q[a]\). At the special Airy parameter used in the proof, this is exactly \(F(x)/F'(0)\). For any primitive cube root of unity \(\omega\), uniqueness of the initial-value solution gives

\[
\phi_{\omega a}(\omega x)=\omega\phi_a(x),\qquad
\phi'_{\omega a}(\omega x)=\phi'_a(x).
\tag{G1}
\]

Indeed, the left side of the first identity, as a function of \(x\), obeys the same ODE as \(\phi_a\) because \(\omega^3=1\), and its derivative at zero is \(\omega\).

Divide every formal profile in \S\ref{air:rb:formal-profiles-at-every-finite-order} by \(F'(0)\) and denote the resulting formal sum by

\[
\Phi(a,\varepsilon,x)=\sum_{k\ge0}\varepsilon^k\mathcal G_k(a,x),
\qquad \mathcal G_0=\phi_a.
\]

Its normalization is \(\Phi(a,\varepsilon,0)=0\) and \(\partial_x \Phi(a,\varepsilon,0)=1\). Denote the half-ratio of the amplitude factors by

\[
R(a,\varepsilon)=1+\sum_{m\ge2}s_m(a)\varepsilon^m.
\]

In the formal recurrence, the rational coefficient depends only on \(x \varepsilon^2\) and \(\varepsilon^3\); the dilation depends only on \(\varepsilon^3\); and the shifted arguments are \((x\pm \varepsilon)(1-\varepsilon^3)^{-1/3}\). Consequently the transformation

\[
(a,\varepsilon,x)\longmapsto(\omega a,\omega\varepsilon,\omega x)
\]

preserves the recurrence. The transformed profile

\[
\widetilde\Phi(a,\varepsilon,x)
=\omega^{-1}\Phi(\omega a,\omega\varepsilon,\omega x)
\]

has the same leading solution and the same boundary and derivative normalizations, by (G1). The order-by-order polynomial recursion in \S\ref{air:rb:formal-profiles-at-every-finite-order} is unique under exactly these normalizations. Therefore

\[
R(\omega a,\omega\varepsilon)=R(a,\varepsilon),\qquad
\Phi(\omega a,\omega\varepsilon,\omega x)
=\omega\Phi(a,\varepsilon,x).
\tag{G2}
\]

In particular,

\[
s_m(\omega a)=\omega^{-m}s_m(a).
\tag{G3}
\]

The uniquely determined coefficients \(h_k(a)\) of the finite logarithmic amplitude satisfy the same grading,

\[
h_k(\omega a)=\omega^{-k}h_k(a).
\tag{G4}
\]

One way to see (G4) without manipulating a logarithm branch is to use its ratio equation. The leading ratio contributions involve \(3a/\varepsilon\), multiplied by a series in \(\varepsilon^3\), and \(\log(1-\varepsilon^3)\). They are invariant under the transformation. The term involving \(h_k \varepsilon^k\) first enters at degree \(k+3\) with nonzero coefficient \(-k/3\), so the unique recursive solution must have the indicated grading.

The normalized endpoint factor is

\[
E(a,\varepsilon)=\frac{\Phi(a,\varepsilon,\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}.
\]

Equation (G2) shows \(E(\omega a,\omega \varepsilon)=E(a,\varepsilon)\). The constant term of \(E\) is one. Thus every coefficient of both \(E\) and \(\log E\) has the same mod-3 grading. Combining it with (G4), the time-\(N\) logarithmic and relative correction coefficients \(b_k(a)\) obey

\[
b_k(\omega a)=\omega^{-k}b_k(a).
\tag{G5}
\]

Since \(b_k\) is a polynomial with rational coefficients, every nonzero monomial \(a^r\) in it satisfies

\[
r+k\equiv0\pmod3.
\]

Finally its contribution after changing variables is

\[
a^rN^{-k/3}
=2^{-(r+k)/3}z^r n^{-k/3}.
\]

The exponent \((r+k)/3\) is an integer, so the factor of two is rational. This proves that every forward correction coefficient at time \(n\) lies in \(\mathbb Q[z]\), rather than merely in \(\mathbb Q(2^{1/3})[z]\).

\section{Asymptotic inversion and discrete thresholds}\label{air:rb:asymptotic-inversion-and-discrete-thresholds}
\begin{writenote}
The Lambert anchor, the explicit corrections (I10)--(I11) and the brackets (I18) are instances of theorems \repopath{p0:thm:lambert-core}, \repopath{p0:thm:perturbed-inversion} and \repopath{p0:thm:staircase} of the repository's transseries volume; see Part~0, ``Instances of results elsewhere in the repository''. No novelty is claimed for the inversion mechanics.
\end{writenote}


\subsection{Input, normalization, and scope}\label{air:rb:input-normalization-and-scope}

Let \(a_n=A082161(n)\), and let \(z=a_1^{\operatorname{Ai}}=-2.338107410459767...\) denote the first negative zero of the Airy function. To avoid confusing this zero with the first sequence term, this report uses \(z\) throughout. The forward theorem provides a fixed \(\gamma>0\) satisfying

\[
\log a_n=\log\gamma+\log\Gamma(n+1)+n\log4+3z n^{1/3}+\log n
+B n^{-1/3}+D n^{-2/3}+L_3 n^{-1}+O(n^{-4/3}),
\tag{I1}
\]

where

\[
B=\frac{53z^2}{90},\qquad D=\frac{44z}{27},\qquad
L_3=\frac{141}{140}-\frac{1304z^3}{42525}.
\tag{I2}
\]

The error in (I1) is initially only an assertion at integer \(n\). All conclusions about an actual sequence below use only that assertion, not unproved differentiability of its remainder.

There are three distinct inversion objects:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  the inverse of an explicit smooth asymptotic model;
\item
  the inverse of a chosen interpolation of the integer sequence;
\item
  the discrete threshold \(N(X)=\min\{n:a_n\ge X\}\).
\end{enumerate}

They have different error statements. In particular, no real-valued asymptotic expansion, by itself, proves a single unqualified floor/ceiling rule at thresholds arbitrarily close to sequence values.

\subsection{The factorial contributes a crucial logarithm}\label{air:rb:the-factorial-contributes-a-crucial-logarithm}

Stirling's formula gives

\[
\log\Gamma(n+1)=n\log n-n+\tfrac12\log n+
\tfrac12\log(2\pi)+\frac1{12n}+O(n^{-3}).
\]

Consequently, set

\[
A=3z,\quad p=\tfrac32,\quad C=\log(\gamma\sqrt{2\pi}),\quad
E=L_3+\tfrac1{12}=\frac{229}{210}-\frac{1304z^3}{42525},
\tag{I3}
\]

and define

\[
h(n)=n\log(4n/e)+A n^{1/3}+p\log n+C
+B n^{-1/3}+D n^{-2/3}+E n^{-1}.
\tag{I4}
\]

Then (I1) is \(\log a_n=h(n)+O(n^{-4/3})\). In particular, the coefficient of \(\log n\) is \textbf{3/2}, not 1. Numerically,

\[
\begin{aligned}
A&=-7.014322231379301\ldots,& B&=3.219306132565383\ldots,\\
D&=-3.810249113341843\ldots,& E&=1.482422558319535\ldots.
\end{aligned}
\]

An equally useful model retains the factorial exactly:

\[
h_\Gamma(n)=\log\gamma+\log\Gamma(n+1)+n\log4+A n^{1/3}+\log n
+B n^{-1/3}+D n^{-2/3}+L_3/n.
\tag{I5}
\]

Its difference from (I4) is \(-1/(360n^3)+O(n^{-5})\), below the precision needed here. Its inverse differs from the inverse of (I4) by \(O(n^{-3}/\log n)\).

For (I4),

\[
h'(n)=\log(4n)+\frac A3n^{-2/3}+\frac pn
-\frac B3n^{-4/3}-\frac{2D}3n^{-5/3}-E n^{-2}
\sim\log(4n)>0,
\tag{I6}
\]

and \(h''(n)\sim 1/n\). Thus \(h\) has a unique increasing large-argument inverse.

\subsection{Lambert W anchor and the first Airy correction}\label{air:rb:lambert-w-anchor-and-the-first-airy-correction}

Put

\[
T=\log X,\qquad x=\frac{T}{W_0(4T/e)},\qquad
s=\log(4x)=1+W_0(4T/e),\qquad q=p\log x+C.
\tag{I7}
\]

Here \(W_0\) is the principal real Lambert W branch and \(X\) is sufficiently large. The identity

\[
x\log(4x/e)=T
\]

holds exactly. The leading correction needed to undo the negative Airy term is positive:

\[
h^{-1}(T)=x-\frac{A}{s}x^{1/3}-\frac{q}{s}
+O\!\left(\frac{x^{-1/3}}s\right).
\tag{I8}
\]

Equivalently, the displayed first terms are

\[
x-\frac{3z}{\log(4x)}x^{1/3}-\frac32
+\frac{\frac32\log4-\log(\gamma\sqrt{2\pi})}{\log(4x)}.
\tag{I9}
\]

This shows the hierarchy clearly: the Airy correction grows like \(x^{1/3}/\log x\), the factorial/prefactor combination gives a constant shift \(-3/2\), and the unknown amplitude first enters at order \(1/\log x\).

Equation (I8), including its stated error scale for suitable interpolations or the discrete bracketing interpretation below, already follows from the weaker core hypothesis

\[
a_n=\gamma n!4^n e^{3z n^{1/3}}n\bigl(1+O(n^{-1/3})\bigr).
\]

It does not require the all-orders construction.

An alternative anchor explicitly absorbs the unknown amplitude: set \(y=T-C\), \(x=y/W_0(4y/e)\), and \(q=p \log x\). Every formula below remains valid with this replacement. The unshifted version (I7) makes the dependence on \(\gamma\) easier to see.

\subsection{Explicit inverse through the third fractional order}\label{air:rb:explicit-inverse-through-the-third-fractional-order}

Define

\[
\begin{aligned}
u={}&-\frac A s,\\
v={}&-\frac q s,\\
w={}&-\frac B s+\frac{A^2}{3s^2}-\frac{A^2}{2s^3},\\
r={}&-\frac D s+\frac{A(p+q/3)}{s^2}-\frac{Aq}{s^3},\\
k={}&-\frac E s+\frac{pq}{s^2}-\frac{AB+q^2/2}{s^3}
+\frac{A^3}{3s^4}-\frac{A^3}{2s^5}.
\end{aligned}
\tag{I10}
\]

Then the inverse of the explicit smooth model (I4) satisfies

\[
\boxed{\displaystyle
h^{-1}(T)=x+u x^{1/3}+v+w x^{-1/3}+r x^{-2/3}+k x^{-1}
+O\!\left(\frac{x^{-4/3}}{\log(4x)}\right).}
\tag{I11}
\]

The error order in (I11) is also the uncertainty imposed by the forward remainder in (I1). It is therefore the natural stopping point using the three displayed forward logarithmic coefficients.

\subsubsection{Derivation}\label{air:rb:derivation}

Write \(t=x^{-1/3}\) and

\[
\delta=u/t+v+wt+rt^2+kt^3,\qquad n=x+\delta.
\]

The leading function \(f(n)=n \log(4n/e)\) has expansion

\[
f(x+\delta)-f(x)=s\delta+\frac{\delta^2}{2x}
-\frac{\delta^3}{6x^2}+\cdots.
\]

For the perturbations, use

\[
\begin{aligned}
A(x+\delta)^{1/3}&=A/t+(A/3)ut+(A/3)vt^2
+\bigl((A/3)w-(A/9)u^2\bigr)t^3+O(t^4),\\
p\log(x+\delta)+C&=q+pu t^2+pv t^3+O(t^4),\\
B(x+\delta)^{-1/3}&=Bt-(B/3)u t^3+O(t^4),\\
D(x+\delta)^{-2/3}&=Dt^2+O(t^4),\\
E(x+\delta)^{-1}&=Et^3+O(t^5).
\end{aligned}
\]

The required equations, in order, are

\[
\begin{aligned}
su+A&=0,\\
sv+q&=0,\\
sw+u^2/2+Au/3+B&=0,\\
sr+uv+Av/3+pu+D&=0,\\
sk+v^2/2+(u+A/3)w-u^3/6-Au^2/9+pv-Bu/3+E&=0.
\end{aligned}
\tag{I12}
\]

Solving (I12) gives (I10); the substantial cancellation in \(k\) is exact. SymPy verifies every coefficient from \(t^{-1}\) through \(t^3\) is identically zero with \(A,B,D,E,p,q,s\) left symbolic.

Although \(q\) depends on \(x\), this calculation is a Taylor expansion about the fixed anchor \(x\). Its contribution to derivatives is already included in \(p \log(x+\delta)\); one must not separately differentiate \(q\) within (I12).

For the remainder, \(q=O(s)\), \(u=O(1/s)\), \(v=O(1)\), and \(w,r,k=O(1/s)\). Hence \(\delta=O(x^{1/3}/s)\) and \(\delta/x=O(x^{-2/3}/s)\). All omitted terms in the residual \(h(x+\delta)-T\) are \(O(x^{-4/3})\). Since the local derivative is asymptotic to \(s\), the mean-value theorem yields (I11). No derivative bound on the unknown sequence remainder was used.

\subsection{Two Newton steps are a simpler practical inverse}\label{air:rb:two-newton-steps-are-a-simpler-practical-inverse}

For computation it is usually preferable to avoid manually evaluating (I10). Starting from the Lambert anchor \(x_0=x\), take

\[
x_{j+1}=x_j-\frac{h(x_j)-T}{h'(x_j)},\qquad j=0,1.
\tag{I13}
\]

If \(n_*=h^{-1}(T)\), then

\[
\begin{aligned}
x_0-n_*&=O(x^{1/3}/s),\\
x_1-n_*&=O(x^{-1/3}/s^3),\\
x_2-n_*&=O(x^{-5/3}/s^7).
\end{aligned}
\tag{I14}
\]

Indeed, throughout the relevant interval, \(h'\) is comparable to \(s\) and \(h''=O(1/x)\), so one Newton step squares the error and divides it by \(xs\). The last bound is smaller than the forward uncertainty \(O(x^{-4/3}/s)\). Thus two Newton steps solve the available asymptotic inverse more accurately than its supplied coefficients justify.

The same procedure works using \(h_\Gamma\) from (I5), with

\[
h_\Gamma'(n)=\psi(n+1)+\log4+(A/3)n^{-2/3}+n^{-1}
-(B/3)n^{-4/3}-(2D/3)n^{-5/3}-L_3n^{-2},
\]

where \(\psi\) is the digamma function. Work with \(T=\log X\) rather than forming very large \(X\).

More generally, if a smooth model has forward logarithmic error \(O(n^{-\rho})\), its inverse uncertainty is \(O(x^{-\rho}/s)\). Additional Newton steps can make model inversion error smaller than any prescribed finite power. Starting from (I7), after \(j\) steps the bound is

\[
x_j-n_*=O\!\left(x^{\,1-2^{j+1}/3}s^{\,1-2^{j+1}}\right).
\]

\subsection{What ``smooth inverse'' means for an integer sequence}\label{air:rb:what-smooth-inverse-means-for-an-integer-sequence}

The asymptotic assertion (I1) alone does not specify a canonical interpolation between integers. It is incorrect simply to differentiate its \(O(n^{-4/3})\) remainder as though a differentiable real-variable asymptotic theorem had been proved.

There is, however, a precise conditional statement:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  If an eventually increasing interpolation \(L(t)\) satisfies \(L(n)=\log a_n\) and \(L(t)=h(t)+O(t^{-4/3})\) uniformly for real \(t\), then

  \[
  L^{-1}(T)=h^{-1}(T)+O(x^{-4/3}/s).
  \tag{I15}
  \]

  The argument is value bracketing using monotonicity and the slope of \(h\); no derivative estimate for the remainder of \(L\) is necessary.
\item
  Such smooth increasing interpolations \textbf{exist} under (I1). For example, put \(\varepsilon_n=\log a_n-h(n)\) and choose a fixed smooth bump \(\phi\), supported in \((-1/3,1/3)\), with \(\phi(0)=1\). For large real \(t\), set

  \[
  L(t)=h(t)+\sum_n\varepsilon_n\phi(t-n).
  \]

  The supports are disjoint, the perturbation and its first derivative are both \(O(t^{-4/3})\), and therefore \(L'(t)>0\) eventually. This supplies a smooth interpolation meeting (I15), but it is an arbitrary choice, not an intrinsic continuous extension of the combinatorial sequence.
\item
  The common interpolation that is piecewise linear in \(\log a_n\) has a weaker generic comparison. Because \(h''(t)\sim 1/t\), its interpolation error relative to \(h\) is \(O(1/t)\), larger than the forward remainder. Its inverse satisfies only

  \[
  L_{\mathrm{linear}}^{-1}(T)=h^{-1}(T)+O(1/(x\log x)).
  \tag{I16}
  \]
\item
  An arbitrary monotone interpolation can differ by order 1 in its inverse. Integer samples alone do not force the sharper real-valued estimate.
\end{itemize}

Every increasing interpolation through the exact sequence nevertheless gives the same discrete threshold after taking a ceiling, with \(N(X)=\operatorname{ceil}(L^{-1}(\log X))\) for large \(X\). Thus interpolation distinctions do not obstruct the direct discrete bracketing below.

\subsection{Discrete threshold bracketing and the rounding barrier}\label{air:rb:discrete-threshold-bracketing-and-the-rounding-barrier}

The forward hypothesis itself implies eventual strict monotonicity. In fact,

\[
\log a_{n+1}-\log a_n=\log(4n)+O(n^{-2/3})>0
\]

under (I1). Under just the weaker core hypothesis, the error can instead be written \(O(n^{-1/3})\), still sufficient. As \(a_n\) is unbounded, finitely many earlier terms do not affect \(N(X)\) for sufficiently large \(X\).

Suppose that an actual forward bound is available:

\[
|\log a_n-h(n)|\le K n^{-4/3}\qquad(n\ge n_0).
\tag{I17}
\]

Let \(r_*=h^{-1}(\log X)\) and \(K_*=\max(K,1)\). For all sufficiently large \(r_*\), one valid, deliberately conservative choice is

\[
\eta=\frac{8K_* r_*^{-4/3}}{\log(4r_*)}.
\]

Then

\[
\boxed{\quad
\lceil r_*-\eta\rceil\ \le\ N(X)\ \le\ \lceil r_*+\eta\rceil.
\quad}
\tag{I18}
\]

To verify (I18), test the integer immediately below \(\operatorname{ceil}(r_*-\eta)\) and the integer \(\operatorname{ceil}(r_*+\eta)\). They lie within a bounded distance of \(r_*\); there \(h'\) is at least half \(\log(4r_*)\), while the allowed forward error is at most twice \(K r_*^{-4/3}\). The displacement \(\eta\) leaves a strict margin on either side. Eventual monotonicity gives the result.

If the explicit approximation (I11) is used in place of \(r_*\), enlarge \(\eta\) by a bound for its error, which has the same order. If the two-step Newton approximation is used, its much smaller error from (I14) can be added instead.

Important qualifications:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Once \(2 \eta<1\), (I18) leaves either one integer or two adjacent candidates.
\item
  If \(\operatorname{dist}(r_*,\mathbb Z)>\eta\), both ceilings agree and \(N(X)=\operatorname{ceil}(r_*)\).
\item
  If \(r_*\) is within \(\eta\) of an integer, the next omitted term or exact sequence evaluation can decide which adjacent candidate is correct. The supplied asymptotic does not always decide it.
\item
  One may write \(N(X)=\operatorname{ceil}(r_*+O(r_*^{-4/3}/\log r_*))\) only in the existential/bracketing sense of (I18). It does \textbf{not} mean \(N(X)=\operatorname{ceil}(r_*)+o(1)\) uniformly in \(X\).
\item
  An unquantified \(O\) bound proves the existence of (I18) with some constant and starting point. It does not yield a numerically certified enclosure for a particular finite \(X\). That requires effective forward constants, a certified amplitude, and numerical error control.
\end{enumerate}

Under the weaker core theorem, the corresponding bracket radius is \(O(r_*^{-1/3}/\log r_*)\), still tending to zero. Therefore the core theorem alone already localizes the threshold to at most two adjacent integers asymptotically.

\subsection{Unknown gamma and numerical amplitude estimates}\label{air:rb:unknown-gamma-and-numerical-amplitude-estimates}

The constant \(\gamma\) is a fixed positive parameter, not a free constant that can be silently dropped after leading order. Changing \(\gamma\) to \(\widetilde{\gamma}\) shifts the smooth inverse, to first order, by

\[
-\frac{\log(\widetilde\gamma/\gamma)}{\log(4n)}.
\tag{I19}
\]

A bounded multiplicative amplitude uncertainty thus causes an inverse uncertainty \(O(1/\log n)\). This is \(o(1)\), but is much larger than the algebraic error in (I11), and can still change the discrete answer at near-integer thresholds.

If certified information gives

\[
|\log\gamma-\log\widetilde\gamma|\le\varepsilon,
\]

the forward error budget for a model using \(\widetilde{\gamma}\) becomes \(\varepsilon+K n^{-4/3}\). The threshold radius is then

\[
O\!\left(\frac{\varepsilon+r_*^{-4/3}}{\log(4r_*)}\right).
\]

An uncertified fitted decimal for \(\gamma\) does not supply such a bound. In particular, the supplied approximate value \(166.95063854245\) is suitable for illustrative computations, not for asserting rigorous threshold decisions.

\subsubsection{An amplitude-free locator and its existing provenance}\label{air:rb:an-amplitude-free-locator-and-its-existing-provenance}

If \(\gamma\) is unknown, one can still use

\[
r_0=x-\frac{3z}{\log(4x)}x^{1/3}-\frac32,
\qquad x=\frac{\log X}{W_0(4\log X/e)}.
\]

The real inverse uncertainty is \(O(1/\log x)=o(1)\). In discrete form, for some fixed constant \(K\) and sufficiently large \(X\),

\[
\left\lceil r_0-\frac K{\log x}\right\rceil
\le N(X)\le
\left\lceil r_0+\frac K{\log x}\right\rceil.
\]

This coarse result already follows from the published Elvey Price--Fang--Wallner Θ theorem: its logarithm has an \(O(1)\) remainder, which is divided by the slope \(\sim \log x\) on inversion. It should therefore not be presented as a consequence unique to the amplitude proof. The amplitude convergence identifies the fixed \(\log \gamma\) term; the all-orders theorem provides the finer algebraic inverse corrections. As before, existence of an unspecified constant \(K\) is not a numerical finite-threshold certificate.
