A_N=\eta_Nt_NA_{N-1}-b_NA_{N-3}+\xi_N,\qquad
|\xi_N|\le CN^{-1}(\norm{w_{N-1}}+\norm{w_{N-3}}).
\end{equation}
The one-step cross term uses
$\norm{M_N^*g_N-g_{N-1}}=O(N^{-1})$ and orthogonality; the delayed cross
term uses only $\norm{B_N}=O(N^{-1})$. Set $\delta_N=A_N-A_{N-1}$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{air:dfa:eq:scalar-difference}
\delta_N=b_N(\delta_{N-1}+\delta_{N-2})
+(\eta_Nt_N-1-b_N)A_{N-1}+\xi_N.
\end{equation}
Equations \eqref{air:dfa:eq:drift}, \eqref{air:dfa:eq:initial-domination} and
\eqref{air:dfa:eq:first-transverse} give \eqref{air:cb:eq:memory2} with $r=29/24$.
Thus $\delta_N=O(N^{-29/24})$ is summable, and $A_N$ has a finite limit
$A_\infty$. Since $u_N$ and $g_N$ are nonnegative, $A_\infty\ge0$.

Now $A_N$ is bounded and $w_N\to0$, so $u_N$ is bounded. Repeating the
transverse and scalar estimates improves them to
\begin{equation}\label{air:dfa:eq:initial-tracking}
A_N=A_\infty+O(N^{-1/3}),\qquad
\norm{w_N}=O(N^{-1/3}),\qquad \norm{u_N}=O(1).
\end{equation}
There is not yet an endpoint conclusion: the normalized ground endpoint
itself is only of order $N^{-1/2}$.

\section{Formal profiles at every finite order}

Let $\eps=N^{-1/3}$ and $x=(j+1)\eps$. Seek an approximate solution to the
ungauged recurrence in the form
\begin{equation}\label{air:dfa:eq:formal-ansatz}
Z_{N,j}=H_N\Phi_N(x),\qquad
\Phi_N(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{K}\eps^kG_k(x),\qquad G_0=F,
\end{equation}
with $G_k(0)=G_k'(0)=0$ for $k\ge1$, and formal ratio
\begin{equation}\label{air:dfa:eq:formal-ratio}
\frac{H_N}{H_{N-1}}\sim2\left(1+\sum_{m\ge2}s_m\eps^m\right).
\end{equation}
The one-step arguments are $(x\pm\eps)(1-\eps^3)^{-1/3}$, and the delayed
argument is $(x-\eps)(1-3\eps^3)^{-1/3}$. The horizontal and delayed
coefficients in \eqref{air:dfa:eq:ungauged-delay} become
\begin{equation}\label{air:dfa:eq:formal-coeffs}
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal A(x,\eps)&=\frac{1-x\eps^2+3\eps^3}{1+x\eps^2-\eps^3},\\
\beta(x,\eps)&=\frac{\eps^3(1-x\eps^2+\eps^3)}
{(1+x\eps^2-\eps^3)(1+x\eps^2-3\eps^3)}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
After division by $H_{N-1}$, the delayed term has the extra factor
\[
\frac{H_{N-3}}{H_{N-1}}=
\left(\frac{H_{N-1}}{H_{N-2}}\frac{H_{N-2}}{H_{N-3}}\right)^{-1},
\]
whose leading constant is $1/4$. As $\beta$ starts at degree three, neither
the new profile nor the new scalar in those previous ratios changes the
leading Airy inversion at the current degree.

\begin{writenote}
Source 48 continues with the paragraph of Part~\ref{air:part:cb} that gives the formal equation at degree $m$, \eqref{air:cb:eq:formal-equation}, in which $R_m,S_m\in\Q[a][x]$ are already known and $s_2=a$, and its triangular polynomial reduction \eqref{air:cb:eq:triangular}, with existence and uniqueness under $Q(0)=0$ and $P(0)+Q'(0)=0$. The paragraph is word for word Part~\ref{air:part:cb}'s and is not reprinted; here the delayed coefficient is this Part's \eqref{air:dfa:eq:formal-coeffs}.
\end{writenote}

The initial scalar coefficients are
\begin{equation}\label{air:dfa:eq:ratio-coefficients}
\begin{aligned}
s_2&=a,& s_3&=\frac{29}{24},& s_4&=\frac{29a^2}{270},\\
s_5&=-\frac{103a}{648},&
s_6&=\frac{5341815-562112a^3}{5443200}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
A genuine finite logarithmic amplitude may consequently be chosen as
\begin{equation}\label{air:dfa:eq:H}
\log H_N=N\log2+3aN^{1/3}+\frac{29}{24}\log N
+\sum_{k=1}^{J}h_kN^{-k/3}.
\end{equation}
At degree $k+3$ in the one-step ratio, the new $h_k$ has coefficient $-k/3$,
so any finite list of ratio coefficients can be matched uniquely. For every
truncation the leading multiplicative constant in $H_N$ is one. Initially,
\begin{equation}\label{air:dfa:eq:h-coefficients}
h_1=\frac{53a^2}{45},\qquad h_2=\frac{551a}{216},\qquad
h_3=\frac{2371}{6720}-\frac{5216a^3}{42525}.
\end{equation}

\section{Global residuals and the zero-limit bootstrap}\label{air:dfa:sec:bootstrap}

\subsection{Approximate solutions in the normalized norm}
Fix any $p>1$. Take sufficiently many terms of \eqref{air:dfa:eq:formal-ansatz} and
\eqref{air:dfa:eq:H}, multiply by the cutoff $\chi(j/N^{1/2})$, restrict to occupied
parity, and define
\[
z_N=D_N^{-1}Z_N/R_N.
\]
Then
\begin{equation}\label{air:dfa:eq:residual}
\norm{z_N}=O(1),\qquad
r_N:=z_N-\eta_NM_Nz_{N-1}+B_Nz_{N-3},\qquad
\norm{r_N}=O(N^{-p}),
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{air:dfa:eq:q0}
\ip{g_N}{z_N}\longrightarrow q_0=\kappa^{-1}\sqrt{I/2}>0,\qquad
I=\int_0^\infty F(x)^2\,dx.
\end{equation}
Here is the required global justification. On $j\le2N^{1/2}$ the factorial
product for $D_N$ has $\log D_N^{-1}=O(j^2/N)$, so $D_N^{-1}$ is uniformly
bounded there. Taylor remainders in \eqref{air:dfa:eq:formal-coeffs}, the time
dilations and the finite amplitude ratios are bounded by an arbitrarily
prescribed power of $N^{-1/3}$ times a fixed polynomial--Airy envelope.
All such envelopes are square-integrable. The coefficient denominators
stay uniformly away from zero. Cutoff derivative errors, and differences
between one- and three-step cutoff supports, are exponentially small in
$N^{1/8}$, up to powers of $N$. The bounded gauge preserves this property.
At the left boundary all fictitious samples vanish exactly, because every
$G_k(0)=0$; the upper moving boundary lies outside the cutoff for large $N$.
These facts give the norm residual after summation.
\begin{writenote}
Part~\ref{air:part:cb} has $N^{1/4}$ here. The cutoff $\chi(j/N^{1/2})$ is the same, so both statements hold; the one with $N^{1/4}$ is the stronger.
\end{writenote}


For the normalization, $H_N/R_N\sim\kappa^{-1}N^{-1/6}$, and a
parity-restricted Airy sample has norm asymptotic to
$N^{1/6}\sqrt{I/2}$. Its direction tends to $g_N$, proving
\eqref{air:dfa:eq:q0}. Only the leading asymptotic of $P_N$ has been used:
the original approximate recurrence is divided by the exact $R_N$, so an
all-orders expansion of the relaxed spectral product is unnecessary.

\subsection{A bootstrap with vanishing limiting projection}
\begin{writenote}
Source 48's Section~8.2 is, word for word, Part~\ref{air:part:cb}'s subsection of the same title (Section~\ref{air:cb:sec:bootstrap}), equations \eqref{air:cb:eq:matching}--\eqref{air:cb:eq:arbitrary-error}, read with this Part's $\eta_N$, $B_N$, $r_N$, $z_N$ and Lemma~\ref{air:cb:lem:memory}. It is not reprinted. It sets
\begin{equation}\label{air:dfa:eq:matching}
C_* = A_\infty/q_0,\qquad E_N=u_N-C_*z_N
=\alpha_Ng_N+W_N,\qquad W_N\perp g_N,
\end{equation}
and concludes
\begin{equation}\label{air:dfa:eq:arbitrary-error}
\norm{E_N}=O(N^{1-p}),
\end{equation}
assuming neither positivity nor contractivity of the signed delayed propagator.
\end{writenote}

\section{The endpoint and the common positive amplitude}

Point evaluation has norm one on $\ell^2$, so
$|E_N(0)|\le\norm{E_N}=O(N^{1-p})$. For even $N$, the leading normalized approximate
endpoint is of order $N^{-1/2}$, because
\[
H_N/R_N\sim\kappa^{-1}N^{-1/6},\qquad
\Phi_N(\eps)\sim F'(0)\eps.
\]
We may choose $p$ arbitrarily large. For the relative remainder in
\eqref{air:dfa:eq:main}, it is enough to take
$p\ge3/2+(M+1)/3$ and retain enough formal terms. Thus
\eqref{air:dfa:eq:arbitrary-error} supplies every fixed finite relative endpoint
order; no endpoint smoothing estimate for the signed kernel is needed.

If $A_\infty=0$, then $C_*=0$. Equation \eqref{air:dfa:eq:arbitrary-error} with
$p>3/2$ would imply $u_N(0)=o(N^{-1/2})$. On the other hand, the known
lower bound \eqref{air:dfa:eq:known-theta}, divided by $2^n n!R_{2n}$, implies
$u_{2n}(0)\ge cN^{-1/2}$ for large even $N=2n$. This contradiction proves
$A_\infty>0$.

Since $D_N(0)=1$ and $b_n=2^n n!e_{2n,0}$, conversion from $N$ to $2n$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{air:dfa:eq:gamma}
\begin{gathered}
\gamma=2^{7/8}C_*F'(0)=2^{15/8}\kappa A_\infty>0,\\
 A_\infty=\lim_{N\to\infty}\ip{g_N}{v_N/(P_NN^{-1/8})}.
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
We used the Airy identity $F'(0)/\sqrt I=\sqrt2$, proved in the appendix.
Every truncation has the same $q_0$ and the same leading normalization of
$H_N$. Hence $C_*$ and $\gamma$ are independent of the expansion order.
This completes the analytic part of Theorem~\ref{air:dfa:thm:main}.

\section{Explicit coefficients and their rational field}\label{air:dfa:sec:coefficients}

The normalized endpoint factor begins
\[
\frac{\Phi_N(\eps)}{F'(0)\eps}
=1+\frac a3\eps^2+\frac{29}{24}\eps^3+O(\eps^4).
\]
Combining this with \eqref{air:dfa:eq:h-coefficients}, then setting $N=2n$,
gives \eqref{air:dfa:eq:log-corrections}. Exponentiation gives
\begin{equation}\label{air:dfa:eq:relative-coefficients}
\begin{aligned}
\mathfrak c_1&=\frac{53z^2}{90},\\
\mathfrak c_2&=\frac{z(5618z^3+46725)}{32400},\\
\mathfrak c_3&=\frac{8337112z^6+200508660z^3+191198475}{244944000}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
These coefficients were independently checked by a direct polynomial
linear-system solve, separate from the triangular recursion.
\begin{writenotejp}
The next coefficients are in Part~\ref{air:part:jp} (Report~103, which writes $a$ for $z$, $d_j$ for the logarithmic and $c_j$ for the relative coefficients): $d_4=-5429z^2/19440$ and $\mathfrak c_4=z^2(220933468z^6+10228873860z^3+53801804475)/44089920000$, \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:logcoeff}--\eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:coefficients}; its scalar coefficients $\sigma_m$ are $2s_m$ in the variable $2^{2/3}z$ (Remark~\ref{air:jp:rem:sigma}), and it adds $\sigma_7=-767\varkappa^2/23328$, \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:sigmas}.
\end{writenotejp}

\begin{writenote}
The rest of source 48's Section~10, the proof that every coefficient lies in $\Q[z]$ by the grading under $(a,\eps,x)\mapsto(\omega a,\omega\eps,\omega x)$, is word for word the second half of Part~\ref{air:part:cb}'s Section~\ref{air:cb:sec:coefficients}, its invariance statement covering ``the full formal recurrence, including its delayed dilation and both previous ratios''. It is not reprinted.
\end{writenote}

\section{Selected smooth inverse and discrete thresholds}\label{air:dfa:sec:inverse}
\begin{writenote}
The anchor $X=Y/W((8/e)Y)$ and the corrections are instances of theorems \repopath{p0:thm:lambert-core} and \repopath{p0:thm:perturbed-inversion} of the repository's transseries volume, and the threshold remark is the separation step of \repopath{p0:thm:staircase} (Part~0). No novelty is claimed for the inversion mechanics.
\end{writenote}

Write $\ell_k$ for the logarithmic correction coefficients in
Theorem~\ref{air:dfa:thm:main}. For each finite $M$ define a positive-real model
by exponentiating
\begin{equation}\label{air:dfa:eq:inverse-model}
\mathcal F_M(x)=\log\gamma+\log\Gamma(x+1)+x\log8+3zx^{1/3}
+\tfrac78\log x+\sum_{k=1}^{M}\ell_k x^{-k/3}.
\end{equation}
Its derivative is $\log(8x)+O(x^{-2/3})$, positive for sufficiently large
$x$. Therefore $\mathcal F_M$ has a unique eventual inverse $x_M(Y)$.
No values of $b_n$ at nonintegers are asserted.

Put
\[
Y=\log y,\qquad w=W((8/\ee)Y),\qquad X=Y/w.
\]
The principal real Lambert function is used. Then $X$ solves
$X(\log X+\log8-1)=Y$. Stirling expansion gives
\[
\mathcal F_M(x)=x(\log x+\log8-1)+3zx^{1/3}
+\tfrac{11}{8}\log x+\log(\gamma\sqrt{2\pi})+O(x^{-1/3}).
\]
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{air:dfa:eq:first-inverse}
x_M(Y)=X-
\frac{3zX^{1/3}+\frac{11}{8}\log X+\log(\gamma\sqrt{2\pi})}{1+w}
+O\!\left(\frac{X^{-1/3}}{\log X}\right).
\end{equation}
Indeed the correction is $O(X^{1/3}/\log X)$; the change in the stretched
term's derivative contributes $O(X^{-1/3}/(\log X)^2)$ to the index, and
the quadratic contribution from the factorial core is
$O(X^{-1/3}/(\log X)^3)$. The first omitted forward coefficient is at
most the remainder displayed in \eqref{air:dfa:eq:first-inverse}.
\begin{writenotejp}
Report~103 in Part~\ref{air:part:jp} makes the $X^{-1/3}$ term explicit: with $L=\log(8X)=1+w$ and $d_1=53z^2/90$, it is $X^{-1/3}[-d_1/L+3z^2/L^2-9z^2/(2L^3)]$, with remainder $O(X^{-2/3}/L)$, \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:explicitinverse}.
\end{writenotejp}

Higher corrections can be generated by reversion of
\eqref{air:dfa:eq:inverse-model}, or by Newton iteration
\[
x^{(r+1)}=x^{(r)}-
\frac{\mathcal F_M(x^{(r)})-Y}{\mathcal F_M'(x^{(r)})}.
\]
On $x\asymp X$, $\mathcal F_M''(x)=O(1/x)$ and
$\mathcal F_M'(x)\asymp\log X$, so each sufficiently accurate Newton
step squares the index error up to the factor $O((X\log X)^{-1})$.
Finite forward orders and finite Newton depth therefore give any desired
finite algebraic inverse accuracy. They remain distinct errors: solving
a fixed model more accurately does not improve its agreement with $b_n$.
Precisely, Theorem~\ref{air:dfa:thm:main} and the mean-value theorem imply
\begin{equation}\label{air:dfa:eq:index-error}
x_M(\log b_n)-n=
O_M\!\left(\frac{n^{-(M+1)/3}}{\log n}\right).
\end{equation}
For arbitrary $y$, the first integer index with $b_n\ge y$ is a different
object. The asymptotics show eventual strict increase, since
$b_{n+1}/b_n\sim8n$, but a threshold still requires checking the adjacent
integers or a certified bracket. An unspecified big-$O$ constant is not a
certified finite threshold algorithm.

\section{Verification and numerical limitations}
The accompanying code separates analytic proof from finite evidence.
The exact script checks 5,050 triangular cells through $n=100$ against
the positive renewal construction, verifies 5,049 normalized recurrence
instances, includes the nonzero $m=1$ delay, and reproduces all 20 displayed
OEIS terms. The symbolic coefficient generator implements the triangular
polynomial inversion. A separate direct-linear-system computation checks
its coefficients without importing the generator. The analytic estimates
above, rather than these finite checks, establish the theorem.

For orientation only, direct floating evaluation of the normalized
amplitude gives approximately $178.317$, $164.945$, $150.038$, $136.080$,
and $131.985$ at $n=10,20,40,80,100$, respectively. These slowly varying
values are \emph{not} enclosures of $\gamma$, do not give certified digits,
and are not used in the proof. Inverse numerical tests, when a trial
amplitude is chosen, verify only the specified model with that trial
parameter.
\begin{writenotejp}
Report~103 in Part~\ref{air:part:jp} carries the uncorrected values to $102.077$ ($n=1000$) and $94.052$ ($n=3000$), and, after dividing out four relative corrections, finds $76.4392$, $76.4385$, $76.4383$ at $n=1000,2000,3000$: evidence for $\gamma\approx76.438$, uncertified.
\end{writenotejp}

The package contains this standalone article, its source and build script,
exact and symbolic verification programs and JSON outputs, a separate
mathematical review, bounded literature and overlap receipts, a clean
replay script, and a file manifest. The PDF build and all pages were
visually checked separately from mathematical review.
\begin{writenote}
This paragraph describes source 48's delivered archive (commit \texttt{096ee7b87}); its manuscript, PDF, build script and checksum manifest are not shipped. The README of this report lists the shipped files.
\end{writenote}


\section{Literature boundary and further questions}\label{air:dfa:sec:questions}
The earlier binary-DFA result \cite{dfa} provides the two-sided order,
not the positive limiting amplitude. The later general-arity paper
\cite{gdw} gives a broader exact recurrence and a relaxed-tree asymptotic
theorem. Its general-arity setting does not supply the present endpoint
amplitude. Ordinary minimal automata for arbitrary languages have different
asymptotics and should not be confused with this finite-language class.
The official entry \cite{oeis}, the cited papers, the author's current
project bibliography, and focused 2025--2026 searches were checked on
2 October 2026. No later amplitude theorem was located; this bounded
search is not a guarantee of universal novelty.
\begin{writenote}
The repository searches recorded in \repopath{48-dfa-literature-status.md} (at commit \texttt{a68b926cf}) were right when made. The same batch delivered the relaxed, compacted and fixed-arity companions, printed as Parts~\ref{air:part:rb}--\ref{air:part:ka} of this report; source 48 cites neither its binary companions nor their shared text.
\end{writenote}


Several extensions deserve separate investigation.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Obtain certified decimal enclosures of $\gamma$ by effective error
bounds or positive interval propagation. Existence alone does not do this.
\item For a normalized exclusion coefficient $(\alpha m+\beta)$, the same
formal drift is $\alpha/(4N)$, suggesting power $1-\alpha/4$.
The relaxed, compacted, and present DFA cases have parameters
$(0,0)$, $(1,-1)$, and $(1/2,0)$, respectively. Nonnegative renewal
weights and upper domination can hold on a larger parameter set, but a
matching lower bound or another positivity proof is still required.
No general parameter theorem is claimed here.
\item Extend the method to the non-nearest-neighbor general-arity
recurrences, controlling the non-selfadjoint modes rather than assuming
that the binary Jacobi argument automatically applies.
\begin{writenote}
For every fixed alphabet size $k\ge3$ this is done in Part~\ref{air:part:ka} (source 18), with a fixed weighted norm and a summable delay instead of the binary Jacobi argument, as this item anticipates.
\end{writenote}

\item Analyze exponentially small corrections beyond the present
finite-order Poincar\'e expansion.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Reading Part V: sources, second routes and notation}\label{air:jp:sec:guide}
This section and Section~\ref{air:jp:sec:questions} were written when the three reports were added, on 5 October 2026; so were the passages marked \textbf{[write, 5 October 2026, batch 102]}. Everything else in Sections~\ref{air:jp:rb:sec:result}--\ref{air:jp:dfa:sec:reproduction} is the reports' text.

The three reports treat the three binary sequences of Parts~\ref{air:part:rb}, \ref{air:part:cb} and~\ref{air:part:dfa} and prove the same theorems: a positive limiting amplitude and a Poincar\'e expansion to every fixed order in $n^{-1/3}$, with Lambert-$W$ inverses. They were written without knowledge of those Parts and reach the theorems by a second route. Time is eliminated to even steps, $n=N/2$, so the relaxed evolution becomes a product of positive semidefinite two-step Jacobi matrices, and boundary values are recovered with a killed lazy-walk kernel. For compacted trees and automata the subtraction becomes signed memory with three lags, whose scalar drift cancels as in Parts~\ref{air:part:cb} and~\ref{air:part:dfa}. Report~100 is the base: Reports~101 and~103 cite its Sections 2--5 for every Jacobi estimate and ship its archive unchanged.

The bundle numbers 100, 101 and 103 are report numbers of the delivering pipeline, unrelated to batch numbers; the other ten reports of batch 102 went to other reports of the collection. Each report calls itself research, disclaims publication, repository modification and external review, and ships an exact checking suite. The intake reran every suite on copies: all pass (Report~100's \repopath{verify.py} and its 53 negative tests, Report~101's compacted verifier with its 10 negative cases and its release ledger, Report~103's verifier and negative tests), and its outputs match the delivered logs except for the recorded Python version and run time. The README of this report lists the shipped files, their delivered names and the rerun route.

\textbf{The sibling report.} Bundle Reports 104 and 102, of the same delivery series, prove the positive amplitude and all finite orders for tree-child networks of every fixed combining degree and for A213863, with the same lemma family: positive Jacobi products, the killed half-line kernel, the forced-product bootstrap, and the restart comparison of Lemma~\ref{air:jp:rb:lem:positive} for positivity. They are printed as Parts~III and~IV of the sibling report \repopath{../a213863-tree-child-networks} (its Sections ``A strictly positive limiting amplitude'' of Part~III and ``Convergence and strict positivity of the amplitude'' of Part~IV). Report~102 says that its all-order proof ``does not require a companion relaxed-tree article''; that article is presumably Report~100 (same series and wording), though Report~102 names neither title nor digest. The two reports stay separate; neither is an input to the other.

\subsection{How Part V was assembled}\label{air:jp:sec:assembly}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Order and numbering.} Report~100 is printed first, because the other two cite it; then Report~101 and Report~103. Sections are numbered on from this report's Section~65: Report~100's Section $k$ is Section $k+66$, Report~101's is $k+79$, Report~103's is $k+91$; theorems keep their position in their section and equations are numbered within sections. Appendix~\ref{air:app:jacobi} and every earlier number are unchanged.
\item \emph{Two restatements replaced by pointers.} Report~101's Section~3 (Section~\ref{air:jp:cb:sec:import}) and Report~103's Section~3 (Section~\ref{air:jp:dfa:sec:jacobi}) restate Report~100's definitions and summarize its proofs. Their definitions and summaries are replaced by notes that point to Report~100 and name every change of letter; the statements they cite, Lemma~\ref{air:jp:cb:lem:import} and Proposition~\ref{air:jp:dfa:prop:input}, are kept, with each line traced to Report~100. Six unreferenced display labels of those passages are therefore not printed.
\item \emph{Parallel text printed twice.} Reports~101 and~103 run the same signed-memory argument (exact elimination, gauged memory bounds, tracking with drift cancellation, forced memory lemma, formal algorithm, residuals, transfer) with different coefficients, drift $1/(4n)$ against $1/(8n)$, and different exponents. They are paraphrases of each other, not copies (the intake measured 6--7\% shared 8-grams), and are both printed. Report~101's inverse theorem and threshold corollary are Report~100's at $\alpha=3/4$; they are kept, with a note.
\item \emph{Citations.} The bibliographies are merged into the report's: \texttt{epflw} and \texttt{maple} are new entries; Report~100's \texttt{kary} is \texttt{gdw}; Report~103's \texttt{trees} is \texttt{epfw}; Report~100's \texttt{oeisr}, \texttt{oeisc} are \texttt{oeis}; the companion entry \texttt{relaxed} of Reports~101 and~103 is replaced by references to Report~100's sections here.
\item \emph{Omitted.} Only title blocks, tables of contents, running heads (Report~103's reads ``Report 103'') and the separate bibliographies. Each report opens with its abstract as delivered.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Consequences drawn at the write}\label{air:jp:sec:write}
The three statements below are the write's. Their proofs are complete and use only results printed or cited in this report; Corollary~\ref{air:jp:cor:compacted} rests, through Report~101 and Part~\ref{air:part:cb}, on the published compacted lower bound. (Dated note, 5~October 2026: the second sentence first read ``Their proofs are complete and use only results printed in this report.'' It is changed because the scope of the claim changes. Remark~\ref{air:jp:rem:positivity} uses no external analytic input, but Corollary~\ref{air:jp:cor:compacted} needs $\gamma_c>0$, which Report~101 and Part~\ref{air:part:cb} take from the published lower bound \eqref{air:jp:cb:eq:theta} of \cite{epfw}, and the identification of Report~101's printed $b_1,\ldots,b_6$ with the coefficients of its theorem rests on Report~100's exact certificate suite. The proof of the corollary now states both dependencies; see the independent check recorded at the end of Section~\ref{air:jp:sec:questions}.)

\begin{remark}[Positivity in Part~\ref{air:part:rb} without the published lower bound]\label{air:jp:rem:positivity}
The positive amplitude of Part~\ref{air:part:rb} follows without the lower half of the Elvey Price--Fang--Wallner $\Theta$ theorem: Part~\ref{air:part:rb}'s projection constant satisfies $A_\infty=\gamma_r/(4\kappa)>0$, and $\gamma=\gamma_r$. Consequently both halves of the relaxed $\Theta$ bound \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:theta} follow from Report~100 alone.
\end{remark}
\begin{proof}[Proof {\normalfont[write]}]
Report~100's proof of Theorem~\ref{air:jp:rb:thm:main} (Sections~\ref{air:jp:rb:sec:jacobi}--\ref{air:jp:rb:sec:amplitude}) uses no published lower bound: \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:theta} is invoked only in its Section~\ref{air:jp:rb:sec:inverse}, and positivity comes from Lemma~\ref{air:jp:rb:lem:positive}. With $a_1=z$ it gives $r_n=\gamma_rn!4^ne^{3zn^{1/3}}n(1+O(n^{-1/3}))$ with $\gamma_r>0$. Part~\ref{air:part:rb} proves (F24) before it uses any lower bound: for even $N$, $d_{N,0}=2\kappa A_\infty2^Ne^{3aN^{1/3}}N+O(2^Ne^{3aN^{1/3}}N^{2/3})$, with $a=2^{-1/3}z$ and $d_{2n,0}=r_{n,n}/n!=a_n/n!$ (Part~0, normalization dictionary). At $N=2n$ one has $2^Ne^{3aN^{1/3}}N=2\cdot4^ne^{3zn^{1/3}}n$, so
\[
 a_n=4\kappa A_\infty\,n!4^ne^{3zn^{1/3}}n+O\bigl(n!4^ne^{3zn^{1/3}}n^{2/3}\bigr).
\]
Since $a_n=r_n$, dividing both formulas by $n!4^ne^{3zn^{1/3}}n$ and letting $n\to\infty$ gives $4\kappa A_\infty=\gamma_r>0$. As $\kappa>0$ by (F22), $A_\infty>0$, which is what Part~\ref{air:part:rb} infers from the lower bound, and (F25) gives $\gamma=4\kappa A_\infty=\gamma_r$. The asymptotic formula of Theorem~\ref{air:jp:rb:thm:main} with $\gamma_r\in(0,\infty)$ implies both bounds of \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:theta} for $r_n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The two normalizations of the scalar series]\label{air:jp:rem:sigma}
For each of the three models, the formal problem of Report~100 (relaxed or compacted), Report~101 or Report~103 is the formal problem of Part~\ref{air:part:rb}, \ref{air:part:cb} or~\ref{air:part:dfa} in other letters. Hence the profiles agree, $f_j=G_j$; the carrier coefficients agree, $\ell_j=h_j$; and $\sigma_m=2s_m$ for every $m\ge2$, where $\sigma_m$ is written in $\kappa=\varkappa=2^{2/3}z$ and $s_m$ in the earlier Parts' $a=2^{-1/3}z=\kappa/2$.
\end{remark}
\begin{proof}[Proof {\normalfont[write]}]
Both sides use $t=N^{-1/3}$ (the earlier Parts' $\eps$) and the height variable $x=(m+1)t$ (there $x=(j+1)\eps$, $j$ the height). The carrier $A(x)=\Ai(z+2^{1/3}x)$ is the earlier Parts' $F$, and $v=2x+\kappa=2(x+a)$, so $A''=vA$ is $F''=2(x+a)F$. The downward coefficient $W$ of \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:formalequation}, \eqref{air:jp:cb:eq:formalrec} and \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:formal} is Part~\ref{air:part:rb}'s downward coefficient in its formal section and $\mathcal A$ of \eqref{air:cb:eq:formal-coeffs}, \eqref{air:dfa:eq:formal-coeffs}; Report~100's $B$ and Report~101's $\mathcal B$ are Part~\ref{air:part:cb}'s $\beta$, and Report~103's $D$ is Part~\ref{air:part:dfa}'s $\beta$; the shifted arguments $(x\pm t)(1-t^3)^{-1/3}$, $(x-t)(1-3t^3)^{-1/3}$ and the time dilations $t_j=t(1-jt^3)^{-1/3}$ are the same; and in both the delayed term is divided by $H_{N-1}/H_{N-3}=s(t_1)s(t_2)$. The gauges agree: $f_0=A=F=G_0$ and $f_j(0)=f_j'(0)=0=G_j(0)=G_j'(0)$ for $j\ge1$. Finally $s(t)=H_N/H_{N-1}$ on both sides, written $2+\kappa t^2+\sum\sigma_mt^m$ here and $2(1+\sum s_m\eps^m)$ there. Each side constructs its formal solution among series $\sum_jt^jf_j$ with $f_j=P_jA+Q_jA'$, $P_j,Q_j$ polynomials, and $s$ a formal power series, and proves that the solution with these gauges is unique in that class (Propositions~\ref{air:jp:rb:prop:formal}, \ref{air:jp:cb:prop:formal}, \ref{air:jp:dfa:prop:formal}; Section~\ref{air:rb:formal-profiles-at-every-finite-order}, Section~\ref{air:cb:sec:formal} and Part~\ref{air:part:dfa}'s formal section, which takes this proof from Part~\ref{air:part:cb}'s paragraph and cites it). The polynomial--Airy restriction is what excludes a $\operatorname{Bi}$-type homogeneous solution. At the first order where two solutions differ, the difference $g$ of the profiles satisfies $g''-vg=cA$ for a constant $c$, with $g(0)=g'(0)=0$; over all smooth functions uniqueness fails, since $g=\frac c2A'+dA+eB$, with $B$ a $\operatorname{Bi}$-type solution of $B''=vB$, meets both gauges for every $c$, whereas within the class the gauge $Q(0)=0$ forces $c=0$ and then $g=0$. So the two solutions coincide, which gives $f_j=G_j$ and $\sigma_m=2s_m$ ($\sigma_2=\kappa=2a=2s_2$). The carriers \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:carrier}, \eqref{air:jp:cb:eq:H}, \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:carrier} and (F30), \eqref{air:cb:eq:H}, \eqref{air:dfa:eq:H} have the same leading factors ($\frac32\kappa=3a$, exponents $\frac43$, $\frac{13}{12}$, $\frac{29}{24}$) and leading constant one, and both are fixed uniquely by matching $s$, so $\ell_j=h_j$. As checks: $\sigma_3,\ldots,\sigma_6$ of Report~100 (relaxed and compacted) and of Report~103 are twice (F29), \eqref{air:cb:eq:ratio-coefficients} and \eqref{air:dfa:eq:ratio-coefficients} (difference zero), and Report~100's $\ell_1=53\kappa^2/180$ equals $53a^2/45$, the value of $h_1$ in \eqref{air:cb:eq:h-coefficients} and \eqref{air:dfa:eq:h-coefficients} (it is common to the three models).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Report~100's compacted formal expansions are genuine]\label{air:jp:cor:compacted}
With $a$ the Airy zero and $b_j^{(c)}$ as in \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:bcompacted},
\[
 \log\frac{c_n}{n!4^ne^{3an^{1/3}}n^{3/4}}=\log\gamma_c+\sum_{j=1}^6b_j^{(c)}n^{-j/3}+O(n^{-7/3}),
\]
and the formal compacted ratio printed after \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:bcompacted} holds with the remainder $O(n^{-10/3})$ in place of ``$\cdots$''.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}[Proof {\normalfont[write]}]
Report~101's Theorem~\ref{air:jp:cb:thm:allorders} gives the genuine expansion \eqref{air:jp:cb:eq:logexpansion} to every depth $J$, with one $\gamma_c$, and its printed $b_1,\ldots,b_6$ are Report~100's $b^{(c)}_1,\ldots,b^{(c)}_6$ term by term. This is the first claim, with $J=6$. For the ratio take $J=9$ at $n$ and $n-1$ and subtract. The factorials give $n!/(n\,(n-1)!)=1$, $\gamma_c$ cancels, and
\[
 \log\frac{c_n}{4nc_{n-1}}=3a\bigl(n^{1/3}-(n-1)^{1/3}\bigr)+\tfrac34\log\frac n{n-1}+\sum_{j=1}^9b_j\bigl(n^{-j/3}-(n-1)^{-j/3}\bigr)+O(n^{-10/3}).
\]
Expanding $(n-1)^{-s}=n^{-s}(1-1/n)^{-s}$ in powers of $1/n$ shows that $b_j$ first enters at $n^{-j/3-1}$, so $b_7,b_8,b_9$ contribute $O(n^{-10/3})$. Exponentiating, the coefficients through $n^{-3}$ are polynomials in $a,b_1,\ldots,b_6$; with the printed values they are those of the display (symbolic recomputation at the write, difference zero). That the coefficients of Theorem~\ref{air:jp:cb:thm:allorders} are the printed $b_1,\ldots,b_6$ is Report~101's computation by the algorithm of Section~\ref{air:jp:cb:sec:formal}, certified by the exact formal suite of Report~100 (Section~\ref{air:jp:rb:sec:coefficients}); it is not derived in the text. Unlike Remark~\ref{air:jp:rem:positivity}, this corollary also inherits the published compacted lower bound \eqref{air:jp:cb:eq:theta}, through which both Report~101 and Part~\ref{air:part:cb} obtain $\gamma_c>0$. Part~\ref{air:part:cb}'s Theorem~\ref{air:cb:thm:main} gives the same conclusion independently: by Remark~\ref{air:jp:rem:sigma} its formal solution and carrier are those from which Report~100 computes the $b^{(c)}_j$.
\end{proof}

\addtocontents{toc}{\protect\smallskip\protect\noindent\textit{Report 100: relaxed binary trees}\protect\par}
\bigskip\noindent{\Large\bfseries Report 100: relaxed binary trees}\par\nopagebreak
\begin{partsource}
Bundle Report~100, archive \repopath{A082161_Positive_Airy_Amplitude_and_All_Orders_Source.zip}, main file \repopath{Relaxed_Tree_Amplitude_Source/relaxed_tree_amplitude.tex} (992 lines; 24-page PDF), titled ``A positive Airy amplitude for relaxed binary trees: A Jacobi product proof with all-order expansions and inverse asymptotics'', dated 2 October 2026, author line ``Research prepared for private review''. Printed in full, label prefix \texttt{air:jp:rb:}; its Sections 1--13 are Sections~\ref{air:jp:rb:sec:result}--\ref{air:jp:rb:sec:reproduce}.
\end{partsource}

\paragraph{Abstract of Report 100.}
\begin{quote}\small
Let $r_n$ be the number of relaxed binary trees of size $n$, OEIS A082161, and let $a_1$ be the largest zero of the Airy function. We prove that a constant $\gamma_r\in(0,\infty)$ exists such that
\[
 r_n=\gamma_r n!4^n\exp(3a_1n^{1/3})n\bigl(1+O(n^{-1/3})\bigr).
\]
The proof converts the exact counting recurrence into a product of positive, contractive operators obtained from finite Jacobi matrices. A quantitative Dirichlet Airy limit gives a spectral gap and a slowly varying Perron direction. Summable errors imply convergence of its scalar projection; a separate positivity argument prevents a zero limit. A killed lazy-walk kernel transfers the Hilbert-space estimate to the boundary coordinate that counts trees. We also prove a full Poincar\'e expansion to every fixed algebraic order, with one compatible positive amplitude, and give a convergent amplitude representation, an exact polynomial coefficient algorithm, and rigorous eventual inverse statements. Numerical checks and formal identities are distinguished from analytic theorems. The compacted-tree analogue, OEIS A254789, is included only for comparison and formal computation: its positive amplitude is not proved here. No worldwide priority claim or certified numerical value of either amplitude is made.
\end{quote}

\section{Result and relation to earlier work}\label{air:jp:rb:sec:result}

Write $R(n,m)=0$ for $n<m$ or $m<0$, and set $R(n,0)=1$ for $n\ge0$. For $n\ge m\ge1$, define
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:triangle}
 R(n,m)=(m+1)R(n-1,m)+R(n,m-1),\qquad r_n=R(n,n).
\end{equation}
Thus $r_0,r_1,\ldots=1,1,3,16,127,1363,18628,\ldots$.
This is the relaxed binary-tree counting triangle of \cite{epfw}; it fixes every startup and indexing convention used below. Let
\[
 a_1>a_2>\cdots
\]
be the negative zeros of $\Ai$, where $\Ai''(z)=z\Ai(z)$ and $\Ai$ decays at positive infinity. In particular $a_1=-2.338107410459767\ldots$.

\begin{theorem}[Positive amplitude]\label{air:jp:rb:thm:main}
There is a constant $\gamma_r\in(0,\infty)$ for which
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:main}
 r_n=\gamma_r n!4^n e^{3a_1n^{1/3}}n\left(1+O(n^{-1/3})\right).
\end{equation}
The constant has the convergent finite-matrix representation in Theorem~\ref{air:jp:rb:thm:representation}.
\end{theorem}

The 2020 version of Elvey Price, Fang, and Wallner's paper, published in 2021, proves the two bounds
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:theta}
 r_n=\Theta\!\left(n!4^ne^{3a_1n^{1/3}}n\right),\qquad
 c_n=\Theta\!\left(n!4^ne^{3a_1n^{1/3}}n^{3/4}\right),
\end{equation}
where $c_n$ counts compacted binary trees \cite[Theorem 1.1]{epfw}.
That paper explicitly leaves even existence of the two amplitudes unresolved and gives the numerical estimates
\[
 \gamma_r\approx166.95208957,\qquad \gamma_c\approx173.12670485.
\]
These numbers are the earlier authors' estimates, not newly certified values. Their Sections 3.3--3.4 already propose Airy expansions and use high-order numerical extrapolation. Accordingly the formal ansatz below is not presented as a new general idea.

The April 2026 revision of Elvey Price, Fang, Louf, and Wallner \cite[Section 10.1]{epflw} again distinguishes the tree problem from its asymptotic-equality results: the associated walk can leave the boundary and its asymptotic initial condition is unknown. Its general theorem therefore cannot be invoked without checking additional hypotheses. The present proof instead establishes convergence and positivity directly for the relaxed-tree transfer product.

Theorem~\ref{air:jp:rb:thm:main} is supported here by an analytic argument, not by fitted values or finite computation. The exact formal coefficient identities in Section~\ref{air:jp:rb:sec:formal} are a separate algebraic result. They are transferred to genuine all-order asymptotics of $r_n$ in Theorem~\ref{air:jp:rb:thm:allorders}, using uniform residual bounds and a forced-product bootstrap. The compacted recurrence has a subtraction term and does not inherit the positive product used in the proof. We do not claim an amplitude theorem for $c_n$, an exponentially improved transseries, a closed form for $\gamma_r$, or exhaustive literature priority.
\begin{writenotejp}
Here $a_1$ is Part~\ref{air:part:rb}'s $z$, and Theorems~\ref{air:jp:rb:thm:main} and~\ref{air:jp:rb:thm:allorders} are a second route to Part~\ref{air:part:rb}'s (F1): $\gamma_r$ is Part~\ref{air:part:rb}'s $\gamma$ (both are the limit of $r_n/(n!4^ne^{3zn^{1/3}}n)$) and $b_1,b_2,b_3$ of \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:brelaxed} are (F2). The route differs: an even-time, positive semidefinite two-step Jacobi product and a lazy-walk sine kernel, where Part~\ref{air:part:rb} uses the one-step matrix $S_N$ and a Catalan kernel. What Report~100 adds is listed in Section~\ref{air:jp:sec:map}; the chief item is Lemma~\ref{air:jp:rb:lem:positive}, which proves $\gamma_r>0$ without the Elvey Price--Fang--Wallner lower bound that every earlier Part uses. The compacted amplitude that this paragraph does not claim is proved by Report~101 in this Part (Theorems~\ref{air:jp:cb:thm:main} and~\ref{air:jp:cb:thm:allorders}) and by Part~\ref{air:part:cb}.
\end{writenotejp}

\section{The exact finite Jacobi product}\label{air:jp:rb:sec:jacobi}
\begin{writenotejp}
Sections~\ref{air:jp:rb:sec:jacobi}--\ref{air:jp:rb:sec:smoothing} are a third write-up of the estimates that Part~\ref{air:part:rb}, Sections~\ref{air:rb:exact-recurrence-and-contractive-symmetrization}--\ref{air:rb:convergence-of-the-scalar-amplitude}, and Appendix~\ref{air:app:jacobi} prove, now in even time: $n$ here is half of the earlier Parts' time $N$, $\eps=n^{-1/3}$, $S_n$ is a diagonal symmetrizer (there $S_N$ is the Jacobi matrix itself), $J_n$ is the two-step Jacobi matrix with top eigenvalue $\lambda_n\approx4$ (there $\lambda_N\approx2$), and $R_n$ is a diagonal gauge (there $R_N=P_NN^{-1/4}$). Reports~101 and~103 cite these four sections. The reading conventions of the whole Part are in Section~\ref{air:jp:sec:notation}.
\end{writenotejp}

\subsection{Rotated coordinates and the two step recurrence}
For integers $t\ge h\ge0$ of the same parity set
\[
 d(t,h)=\frac{R((t+h)/2,(t-h)/2)}{((t+h)/2)!}.
\]
Set $d(t,h)=0$ outside this wedge or off parity, and $d(0,0)=1$.
Substitution in \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:triangle} gives
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:walk}
 d(t,h)=\frac{t-h+2}{t+h}d(t-1,h-1)+d(t-1,h+1),
 \qquad d(t,-1)=0,
\end{equation}
for the noninitial allowed entries. In particular
\[
 r_n=n!d(2n,0).
\]
Define the finite vector $e_n(k)=d(2n,2k)$ for $0\le k\le n$, and extend it by zero to $\ell^2(\mathbb N_0)$. Eliminating the intermediate odd time in \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:walk} gives
\begin{align}
 (A_nu)(0)&=u(0)+u(1),\label{air:jp:rb:eq:boundaryA}\\
 (A_nu)(k)&=L_n(k)u(k-1)+D_n(k)u(k)+u(k+1),\label{air:jp:rb:eq:A}
\end{align}
where, for $1\le k\le n$,
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:LD}
 L_n(k)=\frac{(n-k+1)^2}{(n+k)(n+k-1)},\qquad
 D_n(k)=\frac{2n-2k+1}{n+k}.
\end{equation}
The last unused coordinate of $u$ is zero. Thus $e_n=A_ne_{n-1}$, after zero extension. The exceptional first row \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:boundaryA} must be retained; using the bulk formula at $k=0$ gives the wrong boundary phase later.

\subsection{Symmetrization and positive semidefiniteness}
Set $S_n(0)=1$ and
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:S}
 S_n(k)=\frac{n!}{(n-k)!}
 \sqrt{\frac{(n-1)!n!}{(n+k-1)!(n+k)!}},\qquad 1\le k\le n.
\end{equation}
We use the same letter for this positive diagonal matrix. Since
$S_n(k)/S_n(k-1)=\sqrt{L_n(k)}$, the conjugate
\[
 J_n=S_n^{-1}A_nS_n
\]
is symmetric. Its diagonal is $D_n(k)$ for $k\ge1$, its first diagonal entry is $1$, and its positive off-diagonal between $k-1$ and $k$ is
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:b}
 b_n(k)=\frac{n-k+1}{\sqrt{(n+k)(n+k-1)}}.
\end{equation}
In fact $J_n$ is positive semidefinite. Let $C_n$ be the $(n+1)\times n$ bidiagonal matrix with
\begin{align}
 C_n(k,k)&=\sqrt{\frac{n-k}{n+k}},&&0\le k<n,\\
 C_n(k,k-1)&=\sqrt{\frac{n-k+1}{n+k}},&&1\le k\le n.
\end{align}
A direct multiplication gives $J_n=C_nC_n^{\mathsf T}$, including both endpoints. Positivity of all off-diagonals and the finite Perron theorem give a simple largest eigenvalue $\lambda_n>0$ and a strictly positive unit eigenvector $\phi_n$ on $0,\ldots,n$. All eigenvalues lie in $[0,\lambda_n]$. For initialization put $\phi_0=\delta_0$.

\subsection{The change of diagonal gauge}
For $n\ge2$, define the zero-extended diagonal matrix
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:R}
 R_n(k)=\frac{S_{n-1}(k)}{S_n(k)}
 =\frac{n-k}{n}\sqrt{\frac{(n+k-1)(n+k)}{n(n-1)}},\quad 0\le k<n,
\end{equation}
and $R_n(k)=0$ for $k\ge n$. At $n=1$ put $R_1(0)=1$ and all other entries zero. The notation $R_n$ for the diagonal gauge is distinct from the two-variable counting triangle $R(n,m)$.

\begin{lemma}[Gauge contraction]\label{air:jp:rb:lem:gauge}
For all $n\ge1$ and $0\le k\le n$,
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:gauge}
 0\le R_n\le I,\qquad 0\le1-R_n(k)\le C\frac{k^2}{n^2}.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The case $n=1$ is immediate. For $n\ge2$, $R_n(0)=1$ and $R_n(1)^2=1-n^{-2}$. The logarithmic derivative of the continuous expression in \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:R}, for $k<n$, is
\[
 -\frac1{n-k}+\frac1{2(n+k-1)}+\frac1{2(n+k)},
\]
which is nonpositive for $k\ge1/2$. Thus $R_n(k)\le1$ at each integer $k$. For $1\le k\le n/2$, expand the square-root factors with uniform Taylor remainder. The terms linear in $k/n$ cancel and
$|1-R_n(k)|\le C(k^2+k)/n^2\le2Ck^2/n^2$.
For $k>n/2$, use $1-R_n(k)\le1\le4k^2/n^2$. The case $k=0$ has zero defect.
\end{proof}

Let $\Lambda_0=1$, $\Lambda_n=\prod_{j=1}^n\lambda_j$, and
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:v}
 v_n=\Lambda_n^{-1}S_n^{-1}e_n,\qquad
 H_n=J_n/\lambda_n,\qquad T_n=H_nR_n.
\end{equation}
The exact normalized evolution is
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:product}
 v_n=T_nv_{n-1},\qquad v_0=\delta_0.
\end{equation}
Every $T_n$ is entrywise nonnegative and has operator norm at most one. Hence $\norm{v_n}_2\le1$. Symmetry is used for $H_n$, not asserted for $T_n$; the matrices at different times need not commute.

\section{A quantitative frozen Airy theorem}\label{air:jp:rb:sec:airy}

\begin{proposition}[Frozen spectral estimates]\label{air:jp:rb:prop:frozen}
Let $\lambda_n^{(2)}$ denote the second largest eigenvalue of $J_n$. For all sufficiently large $n$,
\begin{align}
 \lambda_n&=4+4a_1n^{-2/3}+6n^{-1}+O(n^{-4/3}),\label{air:jp:rb:eq:eigen}\\
 0\le\lambda_n^{(2)}/\lambda_n&\le1-cn^{-2/3},\label{air:jp:rb:eq:gap}\\
 \norm{k^p\phi_n}_2&\le C_pn^{p/3}\quad(p\ge0\text{ fixed}),\label{air:jp:rb:eq:moments}\\
 \norm{\phi_n-\phi_{n-1}}_2&\le Cn^{-1},\label{air:jp:rb:eq:drift}\\
 \phi_n(0)&=\sqrt2\,n^{-1/2}\left(1+O(n^{-2/3})\right).\label{air:jp:rb:eq:endpoint}
\end{align}
Changing dimensions are always interpreted by zero extension.
\end{proposition}
The next three sections prove all parts, including the endpoint estimate. Put
\[
 \eps=n^{-1/3},\qquad x_k=\eps(k+1/2).
\]
The half-grid shift reflects the exceptional first row: the constant-coefficient limiting equation has ghost value $u(-1)=-u(0)$, so its Dirichlet point is $k=-1/2$.

\subsection{The continuum operator and its normalization}
Consider the Dirichlet operator
\[
 \mathcal H=-\frac{\mathrm d^2}{\mathrm dx^2}+8x\quad\text{on }L^2(0,\infty).
\]
Its closed quadratic form is $\int_0^\infty(|f'|^2+8x|f|^2)\,\mathrm d x$ with zero trace at $0$. The growing potential makes its form embedding compact: restriction to a finite interval is compact by the one-dimensional Rellich theorem and the potential bounds the remaining mass by $O(1/R)$. Solving the differential equation with the decaying Airy solution and the Dirichlet condition gives its eigenvalues and first unit eigenfunction as
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:continuum}
 E_j=-4a_j,\qquad
 f(x)=\frac{\sqrt2\,\Ai(2x+a_1)}{\Ai'(a_1)}.
\end{equation}
The normalization follows without an undetermined constant: the derivative of
$\Ai'(z)^2-z\Ai(z)^2$ is $-\Ai(z)^2$. Integrating from the zero $a_1$ to infinity gives
$\int_{a_1}^\infty\Ai(z)^2\,\mathrm d z=\Ai'(a_1)^2$.
Thus $\int_0^\infty f^2=1$, $f'(0)=2\sqrt2$, $f''(0)=0$, and $f'''(0)=4a_1f'(0)$.

\subsection{Variational identification and the spectral gap}
Set $b_n(0)=b_n(n+1)=0$, take $D_n(0)=1$, and put
\[
 V_n(k)=4-D_n(k)-b_n(k)-b_n(k+1).
\]
The exact quadratic form is
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:form}
 \ip{u}{(4I-J_n)u}
 =\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}b_n(k+1)|u(k+1)-u(k)|^2
  +\sum_{k=0}^nV_n(k)|u(k)|^2.
\end{equation}
For $k\ge1$, each term in
$(2-D_n(k))+(1-b_n(k))+(1-b_n(k+1))$ is nonnegative, and
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:potential}
 V_n(k)\ge\frac{4k-1}{n+k}\ge\frac{3k}{2n}.
\end{equation}
At zero, $V_n(0)=3-b_n(1)\ge2$.
On every fixed scaled interval the edge weights tend uniformly to $1$, and away from zero $V_n(k)/\eps^2\to8x_k$ uniformly.

We give both min--max bounds to avoid identifying the Perron eigenvalue from a quasimode alone. Sampling a fixed finite-dimensional space of smooth compactly supported Dirichlet trial functions as $\sqrt\eps\,g(x_k)$ gives convergence of its norm and scaled form to their continuum values. Smooth Dirichlet functions are dense in the continuum form domain, so the finite-dimensional min--max principle gives the upper bounds for each fixed low eigenvalue of $(4I-J_n)/\eps^2$.

For the lower bounds, suppose $\norm u_2=1$ and the form in \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:form} is at most $C\eps^2$. Equation~\eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:potential} implies
\[
 \sum k|u(k)|^2\le C/\eps,\qquad
 \sum_{x_k\ge R}|u(k)|^2\le C/R.
\]
Interpolate $u(k)/\sqrt\eps$ linearly between the nodes $x_k$, and linearly from value $0$ at $x=0$ to its first node. On each fixed interval the resulting functions have bounded $H^1$ seminorm. The first half-cell has derivative energy exactly
\[
 \frac{2|u(0)|^2}{\eps^2},
\]
controlled by the boundary term since $V_n(0)\ge2$. This proves the zero-trace condition with the correct coefficient. Local Rellich compactness and the uniform mass bound yield strong global $L^2$ compactness. More explicitly, on a fixed interval the linear interpolant differs from the piecewise-constant embedding by at most $C\eps$ times its $H^1$ seminorm in $L^2$; the first half-cell vanishes in the limit, and both tails have mass $O(1/R)$. Thus norms and, by polarization, inner products are preserved.

Weak lower semicontinuity gives the gradient-energy lower bound, including the first segment. On fixed intervals bounded away from zero, the uniform potential convergence gives the lower bound for $\int8x|g|^2$; the remaining potential is nonnegative. Increasing these intervals proves the full continuum form bound. Apply this compactness argument simultaneously to an orthonormal basis of each fixed-dimensional low-energy eigenspace. Preservation of inner products preserves its dimension, and continuum min--max gives the lower eigenvalue bound. Consequently, for every fixed $j$,
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:spectral-limit}
 \frac{4-\lambda_n^{(j)}}{\eps^2}\longrightarrow E_j=-4a_j.
\end{equation}
Since $E_2>E_1$, this proves \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:gap} and isolates the top eigenvalue on the $\eps^2$ scale.

\subsection{The quantitative quasimode and the coefficient six}
Sample $\widetilde q_n(k)=\sqrt\eps f(x_k)$ for $0\le k\le n$. Midpoint quadrature and Airy decay give
$\norm{\widetilde q_n}_2=1+O(\eps^2)$; let $q_n$ be its unit normalization. We claim
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:residual}
 \norm{(J_n-[4+4a_1\eps^2+6\eps^3])q_n}_2\le C\eps^4.
\end{equation}
For $k\ge1$, with $x=x_k$, the exact entries yield
\begin{align}
 D_n(k)&=2-4x\eps^2+3\eps^3+O(\eps^4(1+x^2)),\\
 b_n(k)&=1-2x\eps^2+\tfrac52\eps^3+O(\eps^4(1+x^2)),\\
 b_n(k+1)&=1-2x\eps^2+\tfrac12\eps^3+O(\eps^4(1+x^2)).
\end{align}
Each remainder is uniform for $1\le k\le n/2$: write its denominator as products of factors $1+x\eps^2-c\eps^3$, bounded away from zero in this range, and use Taylor's formula with a uniform remainder. Also
$|b_n(k+1)-b_n(k)|\le C\eps^3$.
Pair the two expansions of $f(x\pm\eps)$ before estimating them. The first, second, and third derivative terms are respectively
\[
 \eps(b_n(k+1)-b_n(k))f',\quad
 \frac{\eps^2}2(b_n(k+1)+b_n(k))f'',\quad
 \frac{\eps^3}6(b_n(k+1)-b_n(k))f'''.
\]
The third is $O(\eps^6|f'''|)$; the fourth-order remainder is at most $C\eps^4\sup_{|y-x|\le\eps}|f^{(4)}(y)|$. It follows that the absolute bulk error after subtracting
\[
 4f+\eps^2(f''-8xf)+6\eps^3f
\]
is bounded by $C\eps^4E(x)$, where one may take an envelope dominating
\[
 (1+x^2)|f|+(1+x)|f''|+|f'|+|f'''|
 +\sup_{|y-x|\le\eps,\ y\ge0}|f^{(4)}(y)|.
\]
The differential equation and Airy decay bound this by
$E(x)=C(1+x)^{10}e^{-cx^{3/2}}$, after enlarging $C$ near zero.
Therefore $\eps\sum_kE(x_k)^2\le C$ and the $\ell^2$ bulk residual of $\sqrt\eps f$ is $O(\eps^4)$.
For $k>n/2$, $x_k\ge n^{2/3}/2$ and all terms have exponentially small norm. The upper truncation is covered by the same estimate.

At the exceptional first row, use $D_n(0)=1$, $b_n(1)=\sqrt{n/(n+1)}$, and expand $f(3\eps/2)-3f(\eps/2)$. The terms of degree one cancel; those of degree three cancel the $4a_1\eps^2f(\eps/2)$ term because $f'''(0)=4a_1f'(0)$. What remains is $O(\eps^4)$ before the sampling factor $\sqrt\eps$, hence $O(\eps^{9/2})$. This proves \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:residual} globally.

For a finite symmetric matrix, the norm of a unit-vector residual bounds the distance of its proposed eigenvalue from the spectrum. By \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:spectral-limit}, the eigenvalue within $C\eps^4$ is the top one. This proves \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:eigen}. Expanding $q_n$ in an orthonormal eigenbasis and using the gap $c\eps^2$ gives, with positive signs,
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:profile}
 \norm{\phi_n-q_n}_2\le C\eps^2.
\end{equation}
This completes the frozen eigenvalue analysis; localization and boundary evaluation require further estimates.

\section{Localization and drift of the Perron vector}\label{air:jp:rb:sec:localization}

Let $J_\infty$ have diagonal $1$ at zero, diagonal $2$ elsewhere, and off-diagonals $1$. Entrywise $0\le J_n\le J_\infty$. Because $\phi_n$ is nonnegative, this comparison is valid in its quadratic form and gives
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:gradient}
 \sum_{k\ge0}|\phi_n(k+1)-\phi_n(k)|^2+2\phi_n(0)^2
 =\ip{\phi_n}{(4I-J_\infty)\phi_n}
 \le4-\lambda_n\le C\eps^2.
\end{equation}
The elementary discrete Sobolev inequality
\[
 \norm u_\infty^2\le2\norm u_2\norm{u(\cdot+1)-u}_2
\]
follows by telescoping $u(k)^2$ to zero at infinity and applying Cauchy--Schwarz. It implies $\norm{\phi_n}_\infty\le C\sqrt\eps$.

Fix a small $c_0>0$ and let $K=\lceil A/\eps\rceil$ for a sufficiently large constant $A$. For $k\ge K$ use the trial sequence
\[
 w(k)=\exp[-c_0\eps(k-K)].
\]
The quotient of $J_nw$ by $w$ equals
\[
 D_n(k)+b_n(k)e^{c_0\eps}+b_n(k+1)e^{-c_0\eps},
\]
with the absent final term omitted at $k=n$. The row sum is at most $4-ck/n$ by \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:potential}. The even perturbation from the exponentials is $O(\eps^2)$ and the odd one is $O(\eps/n)$, since $|b_n(k+1)-b_n(k)|\le C/n$. Thus, for large fixed $A$,
\[
 (J_nw)(k)\le(\lambda_n-\eps^2)w(k),\qquad k>K.
\]
At any strict maximum of $\phi_n(k)/w(k)$ in $K<k\le n$, positivity of the neighboring entries and the preceding strict supersolution inequality contradict $J_n\phi_n=\lambda_n\phi_n$. This finite maximum principle includes $k=n$, where the missing neighbor is omitted. Comparison at $K$ proves
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:localization}
 \phi_n(k)\le C\sqrt\eps\,e^{-c_0\eps(k-K)}\quad(k\ge K).
\end{equation}
Together with the supremum bound for $k<K$, this yields
\[
 \sum k^{2p}\phi_n(k)^2\le C_p\eps^{-2p}
\]
for every fixed $p\ge0$, proving \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:moments}.

The exact coefficient formulas also give, on the common domain of $J_n$ and $J_{n-1}$, differences bounded by $C(k+1)/n^2$. The new edge is $O(1/n)$, and at its old incident coordinate $k=n-1$ this has the same bound. The old terminal diagonal causes no difficulty; the new diagonal multiplies the zero extension. Since a tridiagonal matrix has only three contributions per coordinate, the first moment bound therefore gives
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:drift-residual}
 \norm{(J_n-J_{n-1})\phi_{n-1}}_2
 \le Cn^{-2}\norm{(k+1)\phi_{n-1}}_2
 \le Cn^{-5/3}.
\end{equation}
The spectral limit \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:spectral-limit} puts $\lambda_{n-1}$ in the isolated neighborhood of the largest eigenvalue of $J_n$; every other eigenvalue has distance at least $cn^{-2/3}$. Apply \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:drift-residual} to the residual $(J_n-\lambda_{n-1})\phi_{n-1}$, expand in the eigenbasis of $J_n$, and use the positive signs of both unit Perron vectors. This proves
$\norm{\phi_n-\phi_{n-1}}_2\le C/n$, including the change of dimension.
Finally Lemma~\ref{air:jp:rb:lem:gauge} and the second moment estimate imply
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:gauge-vector}
 \norm{(I-R_n)\phi_n}_2+\norm{(I-R_n)\phi_{n-1}}_2
 \le Cn^{-4/3}.
\end{equation}

\section{A killed lazy walk and boundary smoothing}\label{air:jp:rb:sec:smoothing}

Put $P=J_\infty/4$. Away from zero its row is that of a lazy nearest-neighbor walk with weights $1/4,1/2,1/4$. The first row has total mass $1/2$, so the walk can be killed at the boundary. The half-integer sine transform diagonalizes $P$: the functions
$\sin((k+1/2)\theta)$, $0<\theta<\pi$, have normalized eigenvalue $\cos^2(\theta/2)$ and the orthogonality factor is $2/\pi$. Consequently
\begin{align}
 P^m(0,0)
 &=\frac2\pi\int_0^\pi\cos^{2m}(\theta/2)\sin^2(\theta/2)\,\mathrm d\theta
 \le C(m+1)^{-3/2},\label{air:jp:rb:eq:heat}\\
 \norm{\delta_0^{\mathsf T}P^l}_2
 &=\sqrt{P^{2l}(0,0)}\le C(l+1)^{-3/4}.\label{air:jp:rb:eq:row}
\end{align}
For the inequality in \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:heat}, on a fixed neighborhood of zero use
$\cos^{2m}(\theta/2)\le e^{-cm\theta^2}$ and $\sin^2(\theta/2)\le C\theta^2$; away from zero the integral decays geometrically. The equality in \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:row} uses symmetry of $P$.

Positivity, $R_j\le I$, and $J_j\le4P$ entrywise imply
\[
 0\le T_n\cdots T_{n-l+1}
 \le\left(\prod_{j=n-l+1}^n4/\lambda_j\right)P^l
\]
entrywise. If $0\le l\le n^{2/3}$, then $j\asymp n$ on the interval and \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:eigen} bounds the scalar product uniformly: its logarithm is $O(ln^{-2/3})=O(1)$. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:smoothing}
 \norm{\delta_0^{\mathsf T}T_n\cdots T_{n-l+1}}_2
 \le C(l+1)^{-3/4},\qquad l\le n^{2/3}.
\end{equation}
No commutation is used; entrywise domination passes through products of nonnegative matrices. The same argument gives
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:frozen-smoothing}
 \norm{\delta_0^{\mathsf T}H_n^l}_2\le C(l+1)^{-3/4},
 \qquad 0\le l\le n^{2/3}.
\end{equation}

We can now complete Proposition~\ref{air:jp:rb:prop:frozen}. Let $z=\phi_n-q_n$. Equations~\eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:residual}, \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:profile}, and \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:eigen} give
\[
 \norm z_2\le C\eps^2,\qquad z=H_nz+\rho,\qquad \norm\rho_2\le C\eps^4.
\]
Iterate $L=\lfloor n^{2/3}\rfloor$ times and use \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:frozen-smoothing}:
\[
 |z(0)|\le C(L+1)^{-3/4}\eps^2
       +C\eps^4\sum_{l=0}^{L-1}(l+1)^{-3/4}
       =O(\eps^{7/2}).
\]
Since $f(0)=f''(0)=0$ and $f'(0)=2\sqrt2$, the normalized sample obeys
\[
 q_n(0)=\sqrt2\,\eps^{3/2}(1+O(\eps^2)).
\]
The preceding error is a relative $O(\eps^2)$, proving \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:endpoint}. A plain $\ell^2$ quasimode estimate would not suffice for this relative endpoint conclusion, because the boundary value itself is small.
\begin{writenotejp}
The relative endpoint error $O(n^{-2/3})$ of \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:endpoint} is sharper than the $O(N^{-1/3})$ of Part~\ref{air:part:rb}'s endpoint $g_N(0)=2N^{-1/2}(1+O(N^{-1/3}))$ (Section~\ref{air:rb:a-boundary-smoothing-estimate}, after (F21)). The leading constants agree: at $N=2n$, $2N^{-1/2}=\sqrt2\,n^{-1/2}$. The two statements concern different unit vectors (the Perron vector of the two-step $J_n$ here, that of the one-step $S_N$ there), so the sharpening is of the endpoint law, not of the earlier lemma as stated. Only the weaker rate is needed for the amplitude.
\end{writenotejp}

\section{Tracking the Perron direction and proving positivity}\label{air:jp:rb:sec:tracking}

Write
\[
 a_n=\ip{\phi_n}{v_n},\quad
 u_n=(I-\phi_n\phi_n^{\mathsf T})v_n,\quad b_n=\norm{u_n}_2.
\]
We first prove convergence and only then nonvanishing of the scalar limit. Since $\norm{v_n}_2\le1$, the gap, drift, and gauge estimates give
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:scalarbarrier}
 b_n\le(1-cn^{-2/3})b_{n-1}+C/n.
\end{equation}
For completeness, apply the orthogonal projection off $\phi_n$ to $H_nR_nv_{n-1}$. On that perpendicular space $H_n$ contracts by at most $1-cn^{-2/3}$. The old perpendicular part contributes at most $b_{n-1}$ before this contraction, since $R_n$ is contractive; the old parallel direction differs from $\phi_n$ after applying $R_n$ by $O(n^{-1})$, by \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:drift} and \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:gauge-vector}. This proves \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:scalarbarrier}.

Comparison with $Kn^{-1/3}$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:perp}
 b_n=O(n^{-1/3}).
\end{equation}
Indeed, for large $K$, the contraction loss $cK/n$ dominates the forcing $C/n$, while $K(n-1)^{-1/3}-Kn^{-1/3}=O(Kn^{-4/3})$. Enlarge $K$ for the finite initial range.
Since $H_n\phi_n=\phi_n$ and $R_n$ is symmetric, the scalar increment is exactly
\begin{align}
 a_n-a_{n-1}
 &=a_{n-1}\bigl[\ip{\phi_n}{R_n\phi_{n-1}}-1\bigr]\notag\\
 &\quad+\ip{R_n\phi_n-\phi_{n-1}}{u_{n-1}}.\label{air:jp:rb:eq:increment}
\end{align}
The first bracket is $O(n^{-4/3})$: its part with $R_n=I$ equals
$-\norm{\phi_n-\phi_{n-1}}_2^2/2=O(n^{-2})$, and the remaining part is controlled by \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:gauge-vector}. The second term is $O(n^{-1}b_{n-1})=O(n^{-4/3})$. Hence the increments are absolutely summable and
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:l2limit}
 a_n\longrightarrow a_\infty\ge0,\qquad
 |a_n-a_\infty|+\norm{v_n-a_\infty\phi_n}_2\le Cn^{-1/3}.
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[The limiting projection is strictly positive]\label{air:jp:rb:lem:positive}
The scalar $a_\infty$ in \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:l2limit} satisfies $a_\infty>0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Start the same normalized evolution at a sufficiently large time $N$ with $w_N=\phi_N$. It has norm at most one and zero initial perpendicular error. All constants above are independent of $N$; the scalar barrier gives $b_n(w)\le Cn^{-1/3}$ for $n\ge N$. Summing the analogue of \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:increment} yields
\[
 |a_\infty(w)-1|\le C\sum_{n>N}n^{-4/3}\le C'N^{-1/3}.
\]
Choose $N$ with this bound at most $1/2$. The actual vector $v_N$ has strictly positive coordinates on its entire support $0,\ldots,N$, by induction in the exact recurrence. So does $\phi_N$, and the finite minimum of their positive coordinate ratios gives $\delta>0$ with $v_N\ge\delta\phi_N$. Subsequent positive evolution preserves this inequality: $v_n\ge\delta w_n$. Taking inner products with $\phi_n\ge0$ and then limits gives $a_\infty\ge\delta a_\infty(w)>0$.
\end{proof}
This argument requires neither a uniform-in-$N$ lower bound for $\delta$ nor an assumption that the original vector starts near the Perron direction. Boundedness and positivity of each finite vector alone would not have proved a positive limiting projection.
\begin{writenotejp}
Lemma~\ref{air:jp:rb:lem:positive} is the one genuinely new step of Report~100 relative to this report. (The same restart comparison proves positivity in bundle Reports 104 and 102 for tree-child networks, Parts~III and~IV of the sibling report \repopath{../a213863-tree-child-networks}.) Every earlier Part obtains strict positivity of its amplitude from a published lower bound: Part~\ref{air:part:rb} from the lower half of the Elvey Price--Fang--Wallner $\Theta$ theorem (Section~\ref{air:rb:the-limiting-constant-and-its-strict-positivity}, after (F24)), Parts~\ref{air:part:cb} and~\ref{air:part:dfa} from the compacted and automaton lower bounds, Part~\ref{air:part:ka} from the Ghosh Dastidar--Wallner relaxed lower bound. The proof above uses only the positivity of the exact recurrence, the contraction $\norm{T_n}\le1$ and the tracking estimates, whose constants do not depend on the restart time $N$. It was checked at the intake: $v_N$ and $\phi_N$ are strictly positive on $0,\ldots,N$, every $T_n$ is entrywise nonnegative, and $\norm{w_n}_2\le1$ bounds the restarted scalar projections, so $|a_\infty(w)-1|\le C'N^{-1/3}$ holds with $C'$ independent of $N$. Consequences: Report~100 re-derives both halves of the relaxed bound \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:theta} with no external analytic input, and Part~\ref{air:part:rb}'s positivity step can rest on this lemma instead (Remark~\ref{air:jp:rem:positivity}). Whether the argument also replaces the published lower bounds in the signed-memory cases is Question~\ref{air:jp:q:intrinsic} of Section~\ref{air:jp:sec:questions}.
\end{writenotejp}

To recover the boundary coordinate, let $z_n=v_n-a_\infty\phi_n$. Its exact defect equation is
\[
 z_n=T_nz_{n-1}+a_\infty\eta_n,\qquad
 \eta_n=T_n\phi_{n-1}-\phi_n,\qquad \norm{\eta_n}_2\le C/n.
\]
Iterate over the final $L=\lfloor n^{2/3}\rfloor$ steps. By \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:smoothing} and \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:l2limit}, the starting error and the forcing satisfy
\begin{align*}
 |z_n(0)|
 &\le CL^{-3/4}n^{-1/3}+\frac Cn\sum_{l=0}^{L-1}(l+1)^{-3/4}\\
 &=O(n^{-5/6}).
\end{align*}
Combining with \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:endpoint} and Lemma~\ref{air:jp:rb:lem:positive} gives
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:vboundary}
 v_n(0)=\sqrt2\,a_\infty n^{-1/2}\left(1+O(n^{-1/3})\right).
\end{equation}

\section{The amplitude and its convergent representation}\label{air:jp:rb:sec:amplitude}

From \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:eigen},
\[
 \log(\lambda_n/4)=a_1n^{-2/3}+\tfrac32n^{-1}+O(n^{-4/3}).
\]
The differences between $n^{-2/3}$ and $3(n^{1/3}-(n-1)^{1/3})$, and between $n^{-1}$ and $\log(n/(n-1))$, are summable with tails smaller than $O(n^{-1/3})$. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:productlimit}
 C_\lambda=\lim_{n\to\infty}
 \frac{\Lambda_n}{4^ne^{3a_1n^{1/3}}n^{3/2}}\in(0,\infty),
\end{equation}
with relative error $O(n^{-1/3})$.

\begin{theorem}[A convergent amplitude formula]\label{air:jp:rb:thm:representation}
With $J_n,R_n,\lambda_n,\phi_n,v_n$ defined by the finite exact recurrences above,
\begin{align}
 C_\lambda
 &=\frac{\lambda_1}{4}e^{-3a_1}
 \prod_{j=2}^\infty\left\{
 \frac{\lambda_j}{4}
 e^{-3a_1(j^{1/3}-(j-1)^{1/3})}
 \left(\frac{j-1}{j}\right)^{3/2}\right\},\label{air:jp:rb:eq:Cproduct}\\
 a_\infty
 &=1+\sum_{n=1}^\infty
 \ip{R_n\phi_n-\phi_{n-1}}{v_{n-1}},\label{air:jp:rb:eq:aseries}\\
 \gamma_r&=\sqrt2\,a_\infty C_\lambda\in(0,\infty).\label{air:jp:rb:eq:gamma}
\end{align}
The product factors are $1+O(j^{-4/3})$ and the series summands are $O(n^{-4/3})$. Both normalizations are canonical once the positive unit Perron eigenvectors and the initial vector $v_0=\delta_0$ are specified.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Equation~\eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:Cproduct} is the telescoping normalization of \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:productlimit}. Equation~\eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:aseries} is the telescoping identity $a_n-a_{n-1}=\ip{R_n\phi_n-\phi_{n-1}}{v_{n-1}}$, with $a_0=1$; its summability was proved by \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:increment}, rather than by a norm bound of order $n^{-1}$ alone. The series sum is positive by Lemma~\ref{air:jp:rb:lem:positive}. Since $S_n(0)=1$, the original count is
\[
 r_n=n!\Lambda_nv_n(0).
\]
Multiplying \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:productlimit} and \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:vboundary} proves \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:main} and \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:gamma}.
\end{proof}

This is a convergent characterization, not a closed form. The proof's $O$-constants are not made explicit, so the representation alone does not certify decimal digits or a numerical cutoff. Its finite truncations may be evaluated and compared with exact recurrence data; such comparisons remain numerical checks until explicit tail bounds and interval arithmetic are supplied.
\begin{writenotejp}
The product \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:Cproduct} is the even-time counterpart of Part~\ref{air:part:rb}'s spectral product (F22)--(F23): a convergent product for the normalization of $\Lambda_n=\prod_{j\le n}\lambda_j$, where (F22) normalizes the product $P_N$ of the one-step eigenvalues. The two constants $C_\lambda$ and $\kappa$ are different numbers, and so are $a_\infty$ and Part~\ref{air:part:rb}'s $A_\infty$; only the products $\gamma_r=\sqrt2\,a_\infty C_\lambda$ and $\gamma=4\kappa A_\infty$ (F25) coincide. The telescoping series \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:aseries} for the projection constant is new in form; Part~\ref{air:part:rb} defines $A_\infty$ as a limit.
\end{writenotejp}

\section{An exact polynomial Airy coefficient algorithm}\label{air:jp:rb:sec:formal}

We first construct one compatible formal expansion to every order. Section~\ref{air:jp:rb:sec:allorders} will transfer it to the actual relaxed-tree solution. For comparison, define the compacted triangle by $C(n,0)=1$, zero outside its wedge, and
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:compacted}
 C(n,m)=(m+1)C(n-1,m)+C(n,m-1)-(m-1)C(n-2,m-1).
\end{equation}
The last term at $n=m=1$ is zero without evaluating a negative-index entry. Its diagonal is $c_n=C(n,n)$, beginning $1,1,3,15,111,1119,14487,\ldots$.
Both the recurrence and its nonnegative counting interpretation are from \cite{epfw}. The rotated compacted recurrence is
\begin{align}
 u_{N,m}&=\frac{N-m+2}{N+m}u_{N-1,m-1}+u_{N-1,m+1}\notag\\
 &\quad-\frac{2(N-m-2)}{(N+m)(N+m-2)}u_{N-3,m-1},\label{air:jp:rb:eq:compactedwalk}
\end{align}
with $u_{N,-1}=0$, and with startup entries supplied by the triangle. Only large $N$ is used in the formal calculation. At $m=0$ the ghost term is absent exactly.

Put
\[
 t=N^{-1/3},\quad x=(m+1)t,\quad a=a_1,\quad
 \kappa=2^{2/3}a,\quad A(x)=\Ai(a+2^{1/3}x),\quad v(x)=2x+\kappa.
\]
Here $A''=vA$ and primes denote differentiation in $x$. Seek
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:formalansatz}
 F(t,x)=\sum_{j\ge0}t^jf_j(x),\quad f_j=P_jA+Q_jA',\quad
 s(t)=2+\kappa t^2+\sum_{k\ge3}\sigma_kt^k,
\end{equation}
with $P_0=1,Q_0=0$, and with boundary and scalar gauge
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:formalgauge}
 Q_j(0)=0,\qquad P_j(0)+Q'_j(0)=0\quad(j\ge1).
\end{equation}
Equivalently $f_j(0)=f_j'(0)=0$ for $j\ge1$. The derivative gauge removes the freedom to move a scalar correction between $F$ and the carrier.

Let $t_q=t(1-qt^3)^{-1/3}$ and
\[
 W(t,x)=\frac{1-xt^2+3t^3}{1+xt^2-t^3},\qquad
 B(t,x)=\frac{2t^3(1-xt^2-t^3)}{(1+xt^2-t^3)(1+xt^2-3t^3)}.
\]
The exact formal relaxed equation is
\begin{align}
 s(t)F(t,x)
 &=W(t,x)F\bigl(t_1,(x-t)(1-t^3)^{-1/3}\bigr)\notag\\
 &\quad+F\bigl(t_1,(x+t)(1-t^3)^{-1/3}\bigr).\label{air:jp:rb:eq:formalequation}
\end{align}
For compacted trees one subtracts
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:formalcompactextra}
 \frac{B(t,x)}{s(t_1)s(t_2)}
 F\bigl(t_3,(x-t)(1-3t^3)^{-1/3}\bigr)
\end{equation}
from its right-hand side. These identities follow by substituting $u_{N,m}=h_NF(t,x)$ and $h_N/h_{N-1}=s(t)$ in the corresponding lattice recurrences.

\begin{proposition}[Formal existence and uniqueness at every order]\label{air:jp:rb:prop:formal}
For each of the two equations there are unique polynomials
$P_j,Q_j\in\Q[\kappa,x]$ and $\sigma_j\in\Q[\kappa]$ satisfying \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:formalansatz}--\eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:formalgauge}. They can be generated by a triangular recursion requiring only rational polynomial arithmetic.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The coefficients of $t^0,t^1$ cancel identically and the $t^2$ equation is $A''-(2x+\kappa)A=0$. Suppose all coefficients through $t^{k-1}$ have been removed. Because $A''=vA$, all derivatives of polynomial pairs multiplying $A,A'$ remain such pairs. Write the known coefficient of $t^k$ in right side minus left side as $R_0A+R_1A'$. The new unknown $f_{k-2}=PA+QA'$ and scalar $\sigma_k$ enter as
\[
 (D^2-v)(PA+QA')-\sigma_kA+R_0A+R_1A'=0.
\]
Indeed
\[
 (D^2-v)(PA+QA')=(P''+2vQ'+2Q)A+(2P'+Q'')A'.
\]
Eliminating $P$ leaves
\[
 \mathcal TQ=2R_0-R_1'-2\sigma_k,
 \qquad\mathcal TQ=Q'''-4(2x+\kappa)Q'-4Q.
\]
On $x^d$ the leading coefficient of $\mathcal T$ is $-4(2d+1)$, and its other terms have smaller degree. Hence it is invertible on every finite polynomial degree space over $\Q[\kappa]$. Put
\begin{align}
 \widetilde q&=\mathcal T^{-1}(2R_0-R_1'),&
 \sigma_k&=-2\widetilde q(0),\notag\\
 Q&=\widetilde q+\sigma_k/2,&
 P(x)&=\int_0^x\frac{-R_1(u)-Q''(u)}2\,\mathrm d u-Q'(0).\label{air:jp:rb:eq:triangular}
\end{align}
Since $\mathcal T$ sends a constant $c$ to $-4c$, these formulas satisfy both differential identities, $Q(0)=0$, and $P(0)+Q'(0)=0$. The same identities and gauges imply uniqueness.
For compacted trees, $B$ starts at order $t^3$, so the new pair and new scalar do not enter \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:formalcompactextra} at the current order $t^k$. The same induction applies. This proves an infinite \emph{formal} series without asserting its convergence.
\end{proof}

A direct descending implementation writes $\widetilde q=\sum q_dx^d$ and $2R_0-R_1'=\sum v_dx^d$, pads higher $q$'s with zero, and uses
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:descending}
 q_d=\frac{-v_d-4\kappa(d+1)q_{d+1}+(d+3)(d+2)(d+1)q_{d+3}}{4(2d+1)}.
\end{equation}
The first values are
\[
 f_1=-\tfrac23x^2A,\qquad
 \sigma_3=\begin{cases}8/3&\text{relaxed},\\13/6&\text{compacted},\end{cases}
 \qquad\sigma_4=\frac{29\kappa^2}{540}.
\]
For relaxed trees,
\begin{align*}
 f_2&=\frac{2(\kappa+15x^4+111x)}{135}A
       +\frac{x(-4\kappa+51x)}{270}A',\\
 \sigma_5&=-\frac{23\kappa}{81},\qquad
 \sigma_6=-\frac{8783\kappa^3-595080}{340200}.
\end{align*}
For compacted trees,
\begin{align*}
 f_2&=\frac{8\kappa+120x^4+753x}{540}A
       +\frac{x(-4\kappa+51x)}{270}A',\\
 \sigma_5&=-\frac{11\kappa}{324},\qquad
 \sigma_6=-\frac{17566\kappa^3-2054835}{680400}.
\end{align*}
The source archive records the full exact polynomials through $f_7$ and the scalars through $\sigma_9$. Finite symbolic verification is a check on these explicit values, not a replacement for the indefinite recursion proof above.

\section{From formal profiles to all algebraic orders}\label{air:jp:rb:sec:allorders}

\begin{theorem}[All-order relaxed-tree expansion]\label{air:jp:rb:thm:allorders}
There is one compatible sequence of real coefficients $b_1,b_2,\ldots$, computable by Proposition~\ref{air:jp:rb:prop:formal}, such that for every fixed integer $J\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:allorders}
 r_n=\gamma_r n!4^ne^{3an^{1/3}}n
 \exp\!\left(\sum_{j=1}^Jb_jn^{-j/3}\right)
 \left(1+O(n^{-(J+1)/3})\right).
\end{equation}
The positive constant $\gamma_r$ is the same at every depth and is the constant of Theorem~\ref{air:jp:rb:thm:main}. Equivalently the exponential has a full Poincar\'e expansion in $n^{-1/3}$. The constants implicit in the remainder may depend on $J$.
\end{theorem}
We prove this by finite truncations with genuine uniform residual bounds. Neither convergence of the infinite series nor positivity of the truncated higher-order profiles is needed.

\subsection{A compatible scalar carrier}
Define $\ell_j$ by the formal identity
\begin{align}
 \log(s(t)/2)
 &=\frac{3\kappa}{2t}\bigl[1-(1-t^3)^{1/3}\bigr]
   -\frac43\log(1-t^3)\notag\\
 &\quad+\sum_{j\ge1}\ell_jt^j\bigl[1-(1-t^3)^{-j/3}\bigr].\label{air:jp:rb:eq:carrieridentity}
\end{align}
The terms of degrees two and three agree because $\sigma_2=\kappa$ and $\sigma_3=8/3$. At degree $j+3$, the new $\ell_j$ has coefficient $-j/3$, so it is uniquely determined. In particular $\ell_1=53\kappa^2/180$.
For each fixed depth $M\ge1$, put
\begin{align}
 h_N^{[M]}&=2^N\exp\!\left(\tfrac{3\kappa}2N^{1/3}\right)N^{4/3}
              \exp\!\left(\sum_{j=1}^M\ell_jN^{-j/3}\right),\label{air:jp:rb:eq:carrier}\\
 F^{[M]}(t,x)&=\sum_{j=0}^Mt^j(P_jA+Q_jA').\label{air:jp:rb:eq:finiteprofile}
\end{align}
The exact ratio $h_N^{[M]}/h_{N-1}^{[M]}$ agrees with $s(t)$ through at least degree $M+3$. Generating the needed $\ell_j$ may use formal pairs beyond the retained profile depth, which Proposition~\ref{air:jp:rb:prop:formal} already supplies.
Crucially the carrier's leading multiplicative constant is $1$ at every depth. Independently initializing products of truncated ratios would introduce depth-dependent constants and is not an equivalent construction.

Use even-time variables
\[
 \eps=n^{-1/3},\quad X=(k+1/2)\eps,\quad
 N=2n,\quad m=2k,\quad t=2^{-1/3}\eps,\quad x=2^{2/3}X.
\]
Define the uncut approximation
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:Eapprox}
 E_n^{[M]}(k)=\frac{2^{-4/3}}{\Ai'(a)}h_{2n}^{[M]}F^{[M]}(t,x).
\end{equation}
Its leading term, writing
$\mathcal B_n=4^ne^{3an^{1/3}}n^{4/3}$, is
\[
 \frac{\mathcal B_n}{\Ai'(a)}\Ai(a+2X).
\]
More explicitly its scalar correction is $\exp(\sum_{j=1}^M2^{-j/3}\ell_j\eps^j)$ and its profile corrections have polynomial coefficients
\[
 \overline P_j(X)=2^{-j/3}P_j(2^{2/3}X),\qquad
 \overline Q_j(X)=2^{(1-j)/3}Q_j(2^{2/3}X),
\]
with $\kappa=2^{2/3}a$ substituted. Since $X_0=\eps/2$ and $\Ai(a)=0$,
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:Eendpoint}
 E_n^{[M]}(0)=4^ne^{3an^{1/3}}n\,(1+O(\eps)),
\end{equation}
with leading coefficient exactly $1$, independent of $M$. Its power coefficients stabilize under deeper truncation.

\subsection{Uniform residuals and the exact ghost boundary}
Fix $M$ and any constant $C_0>0$. On $0\le m\le C_0\sqrt N$, the one-step residual of $h_N^{[M]}F^{[M]}$, divided by $h_N^{[M]}$, is bounded by
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:pointresidual}
 Ct^{M+3}(1+x)^D e^{-cx^{3/2}},
\end{equation}
for constants depending on $M,C_0$. Here are the uniform remainder details. In this region $x\le Ct^{-1/2}$, so $|xt^2|\le Ct^{3/2}$; all denominators $1+xt^2-c_0t^3$ stay away from zero. Finite rational expansions with exact geometric remainders are bounded by a selected power of $t$ times a polynomial in $x$. The shifted profile argument differs from $x$ by
$\pm t+O(xt^3+t^4)=O(t)$. Every fixed derivative of a polynomial--Airy pair is bounded by $C(1+x)^De^{-cx^{3/2}}$, uniformly also on the Taylor interval after adjusting constants. Taylor's integral remainder has the same form. The finite-carrier ratio and the powers $(1-t^3)^r$ are analytic at $t=0$ after the known factors are removed. Products preserve the envelope bounds. Formal cancellation through degree $M+2$ proves \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:pointresidual}.

The statement includes $m=0$: the ghost evaluation $m=-1$ has $x=0$, and every $f_j(0)=0$ exactly before expansion. Thus no asymptotic boundary condition has been substituted for the true one. Eliminating the odd step gives the even residual $E_n^{[M]}-A_nE_{n-1}^{[M]}$ as a bounded combination of the residual at $N$ and two at $N-1$. All coefficients are uniformly bounded on this region, and the forbidden-site term is absent at zero. The same order therefore holds for the even-time residual.

Take a fixed smooth $\chi$ with $0\le\chi\le1$, $\chi=1$ on $[0,1/2]$, and $\chi=0$ on $[1,\infty)$, and define
\[
 \widetilde E_n^{[M]}(k)=\chi(k/\sqrt n)E_n^{[M]}(k).
\]
The transition region has $X\asymp n^{1/6}$, so all profiles and their neighbors there are polynomial multiples of $e^{-cn^{1/4}}$. The cutoff error is smaller than every power of $n$ after division by the carrier, including the change from the cutoff at $n-1$ to that at $n$. Applying $A_n$ may enlarge the residual support to $k\le\sqrt n+1$; the estimates are applied on this padded support.
Since the $X$-mesh is $\eps$,
\[
 \sum_{k\ge0}(1+X_k)^{2D}e^{-2cX_k^{3/2}}=O(\eps^{-1}).
\]
Consequently
\[
 \norm{\widetilde E_n^{[M]}-A_n\widetilde E_{n-1}^{[M]}}_2
 =O(\mathcal B_n\eps^{M+5/2}).
\]
The gauge is harmless on the padded support: the exact product for $S_n$ gives
$-\log S_n(k)=O(k^2/n+k/n)=O(1)$ for $k\le\sqrt n+O(1)$, so $S_n^{-1}$ is uniformly bounded there. Also $\mathcal B_n/\Lambda_n=O(\eps^{1/2})$ by \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:productlimit}. We have proved the genuine normalized residual estimate
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:normresidual}
 \norm{\Lambda_n^{-1}S_n^{-1}
 (\widetilde E_n^{[M]}-A_n\widetilde E_{n-1}^{[M]})}_2
 =O(\eps^{M+3})=O(n^{-(M+3)/3}).
\end{equation}

The normalized approximants $U_n^{[M]}=\Lambda_n^{-1}S_n^{-1}\widetilde E_n^{[M]}$ satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:profilecompatibility}
 \norm{U_n^{[M]}-\frac1{\sqrt2C_\lambda}\phi_n}_2=O(n^{-1/3}).
\end{equation}
To see the constant, the leading profile after normalization is
$n^{-1/6}\Ai(a+2X)/(C_\lambda\Ai'(a))=(\sqrt2C_\lambda)^{-1}\widetilde q_n(k)$.
The carrier corrections and the finitely many profile corrections have weighted relative $\ell^2$ norm $O(\eps)$. The exact gauge yields $S_n^{-1}-1=O(\eps(1+X^2))$ in that weighted norm; its remote tail is exponentially small. The normalized Perron vector differs from the leading Airy sample by $O(\eps^2)$, and \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:productlimit} has relative error $O(\eps)$. This proves \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:profilecompatibility}, uniformly in $n$ for each fixed depth.

\subsection{A forced product bootstrap}
\begin{lemma}[Transfer with a vanishing limiting projection]\label{air:jp:rb:lem:forced}
Suppose finite-support signed vectors obey
\[
 z_n=T_nz_{n-1}+\rho_n,\qquad \norm{\rho_n}_2=O(n^{-p}),\qquad p>4/3.
\]
If $\norm{z_n}_2=O(n^{-1/3})$ and $\ip{\phi_n}{z_n}\to0$, then
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:rb:eq:forcedresult}
 \norm{z_n}_2=O(n^{-(p-1)}),\qquad
 z_n(0)=O(n^{-1/2-(p-1)}).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $\alpha_n=\ip{\phi_n}{z_n}$ and
$\beta_n=\norm{(I-\phi_n\phi_n^{\mathsf T})z_n}_2$.
The same projection calculation as \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:scalarbarrier} and \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:increment}, with the additional forcing, gives
\begin{align*}
 \beta_n&\le(1-cn^{-2/3})\beta_{n-1}
              +Cn^{-1}|\alpha_{n-1}|+Cn^{-p},\\
 |\alpha_n-\alpha_{n-1}|&\le Cn^{-4/3}|\alpha_{n-1}|
              +Cn^{-1}\beta_{n-1}+Cn^{-p}.
\end{align*}
If $|\alpha_n|+\beta_n=O(n^{-q})$, the first stable scalar recurrence gives
\[
 \beta_n=O(n^{-q-1/3}+n^{-p+2/3}).
\]
The scalar estimate used here is elementary: a sufficiently large multiple of $n^{-r+2/3}$ is a supersolution of
$x_n\le(1-cn^{-2/3})x_{n-1}+O(n^{-r})$ for every fixed $r$; the finite initial part decays at least as $e^{-c'n^{1/3}}$.
Because $\alpha_n\to0$, sum the second inequality from $n+1$ to infinity, using the improved $\beta$ estimate. All terms are summable, initially with $q=1/3$ and thereafter with larger $q$. This gives
\[
 \alpha_n=O(n^{-q-1/3}+n^{-p+1}).
\]
Thus a bootstrap improves $q$ to $\min(q+1/3,p-1)$. Finitely many repetitions reach $p-1$, proving the norm estimate. Finally iterate the forced equation over $L=\lfloor n^{2/3}\rfloor$ steps and apply \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:smoothing}:
\[
 |z_n(0)|\le CL^{-3/4}n^{-(p-1)}
    +Cn^{-p}\sum_{l=0}^{L-1}(l+1)^{-3/4}
 =O(n^{-p+1/2})+O(n^{-p+1/6}).
\]
This is \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:forcedresult}. The proof uses no sign assumption on $z_n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{air:jp:rb:thm:allorders}]
Set $z_n=v_n-\gamma_rU_n^{[M]}$. By \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:gamma}, \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:l2limit}, and \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:profilecompatibility}, $\norm{z_n}_2=O(n^{-1/3})$ and its limiting Perron projection is zero. Equation~\eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:normresidual} supplies the forcing with $p=(M+3)/3$; take $M\ge2$ to ensure $p>4/3$. Lemma~\ref{air:jp:rb:lem:forced} gives a boundary error $O(n^{-1/2-M/3})$. Multiplication by $\Lambda_n$, and division by the diagonal carrier $4^ne^{3an^{1/3}}n$, changes this to the relative error $O(n^{-M/3})$. The endpoint series of $E_n^{[M]}$ has leading coefficient one and compatible coefficients at all depths. Given $J$, choose $M\ge\max(2,J+1)$, expand its endpoint through order $J$, and absorb its tail and the transfer error into $O(n^{-(J+1)/3})$. Formal logarithm converts the multiplicative coefficients to the $b_j$ in \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:allorders}. The amplitude is the already fixed $\gamma_r$, so it is not reselected at each depth.
\end{proof}
