\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:published}
 b_n=\Theta\!\left(n!8^ne^{3an^{1/3}}n^{7/8}\right).
\end{equation}
Thus the factorial, exponential, stretched-exponential, and polynomial factors are prior work. That paper also supplies the exact recurrence and Airy comparison construction. Its lower trial ratio contains the coefficient $29/(12N)$, which reappears in our independently derived formal series. Our contribution here is the proof of convergence to one positive amplitude, the compatible remainder theorem at every algebraic order, and the consequent refined inverses. This is a description relative to the inspected sources, not a worldwide novelty assertion.

The proof does not need \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:published} to establish its positive amplitude. Its published lower input is only
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:compactedlower}
 2^{n-1}c_n^{\rm tree}\le b_n,\qquad
 c_n^{\rm tree}\ge C n!4^ne^{3an^{1/3}}n^{3/4}
 \quad(n\text{ sufficiently large}),
\end{equation}
where $c_n^{\rm tree}$ counts compacted binary trees. The comparison is \cite[Equation (1)]{dfa}; the compacted asymptotic bound is stated on its next page and proved in \cite{epfw}. No positive-amplitude theorem for compacted trees is required.

\paragraph{The analytic dependency.}
The finite Jacobi matrices in this problem are exactly those of the companion relaxed-tree report, Report~100. Sections 2--5 of that report (Sections~\ref{air:jp:rb:sec:jacobi}--\ref{air:jp:rb:sec:smoothing} here) prove the spectral, localization, gauge, and boundary smoothing estimates used below. We state the complete needed input in Section~\ref{air:jp:dfa:sec:jacobi}; its proof is available in the enclosed, hash-pinned PDF and source archive. The DFA-specific signed-memory analysis and all-orders transfer are proved here. The \LaTeX\ source of the present article compiles on its own without external inputs or bibliography files.
\begin{writenotejp}
Theorem~\ref{air:jp:dfa:thm:main} is a second route to Part~\ref{air:part:dfa}'s Theorem~\ref{air:dfa:thm:main} ($a$ here is Part~\ref{air:part:dfa}'s $z$, $\gamma_D$ its $\gamma$), by the mechanism of Part~\ref{air:part:dfa} and of Report~101 in even time: uniform normalization by $2^{-m}$ (as in \eqref{air:dfa:eq:count}), domination by the relaxed array, an $O(n^{1/8})$ start, and cancellation of the scalar drift $1/(8n)$. The positivity input is slightly weaker than Part~\ref{air:part:dfa}'s: Part~\ref{air:part:dfa} uses the automaton $\Theta$ theorem \eqref{air:dfa:eq:known-theta}, Report~103 only the structural comparison $b_n\ge2^{n-1}c^{\rm tree}_n$ and the compacted lower bound \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:compactedlower}, from which Elvey Price, Fang and Wallner derive the automaton lower bound. New in Report~103 relative to Part~\ref{air:part:dfa}: $d_4$, $c_4$, $\sigma_7$, the ratio expansion \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:ratio}, the explicit inverse term \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:explicitinverse} and the numerical amplitude estimate near $76.438$ (uncertified). Report~103 calls Report~100 a ``companion research report prepared for private review''. The ``enclosed, hash-pinned PDF and source archive'' are byte copies of Report~100's delivered PDF and archive (SHA-256 recorded in Section~\ref{air:jp:dfa:sec:reproduction}); they are not shipped here, since Report~100 is printed in this Part and its archive survives in commit \texttt{60f54ea06}.
\end{writenotejp}

\paragraph{Meaning of all orders.}
Equation \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:main} is a Poincar\'e expansion: every \emph{fixed finite truncation} has its stated remainder. It neither asserts convergence of the infinite series nor permits summing it and subtracting an exact function. Section~\ref{air:jp:dfa:sec:inverse} supplies rigorous inverse expansions in fractional powers and logarithms. Contributions smaller than every algebraic power, sometimes intended by the word transseries, remain outside the proved result.

\section{The exact counting array and its startup}\label{air:jp:dfa:sec:exact}
Write $b(n,m)$ for the nonnegative integer array from \cite[Proposition 5]{dfa}:
\begin{align}
 b(n,m)&=2b(n,m-1)+(m+1)b(n-1,m)-m b(n-2,m-1),
       &&n\ge m\ge1,\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:triangle}\\
 b(n,m)&=0,&&n<m,\notag\\
 b(n,0)&=1,&&n\ge-1.\notag
\end{align}
Then $b_n=b(n,n)$. The negative-index seed $b(-1,0)=1$ is important: it makes $b(1,1)=1$, not $2$.

\subsection{Uniform normalization and rotation}
Define, including the entire $m=0$ boundary,
\[
 B(n,m)=2^{-m}b(n,m).
\]
Division of \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:triangle} gives
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:B}
 B(n,m)=B(n,m-1)+(m+1)B(n-1,m)-\frac m2B(n-2,m-1).
\end{equation}
For admissible integers $0\le h\le N$ with $N-h$ even put
\[
 e(N,h)=\frac{B((N+h)/2,(N-h)/2)}{((N+h)/2)!},
\]
and put $e=0$ off this domain. Its exact startup is
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:startup}
 e(0,0)=1,\quad e(1,1)=1,\quad e(2,0)=\tfrac12,\quad e(2,2)=\tfrac12.
\end{equation}
A direct substitution in \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:B} yields, for $N\ge3$,
\begin{align}
 e(N,h)&=\frac{N-h+2}{N+h}e(N-1,h-1)+e(N-1,h+1)\notag\\
 &\quad-\frac{N-h}{(N+h)(N+h-2)}e(N-3,h-1).\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:walk}
\end{align}
At $h=0$, both ghost terms vanish exactly and $e(N,0)=e(N-1,1)$. At $h=N$, the memory coefficient is zero and the upper boundary propagates $e(N,N)=1/N!$. Never evaluate the singular coefficient at $(N,h)=(2,0)$; use \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:startup}. The diagonal conversion is
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:diagonal}
 b_n=2^n n!e(2n,0).
\end{equation}

\subsection{Why the normalization is stated explicitly}
The nearby transformed arrays on pages 11:6--11:7 of \cite{dfa} use $b(n,m)/2^{m-1}$ for $m\ge1$, but leave $m=0$ unchanged. Their displayed recurrence for that first transformed array is restricted to $m>1$. If the subsequent rotated recurrence is read uniformly with that piecewise startup, its value at $(3,1)$ would be
\[
 1+\tfrac12-\tfrac14=\tfrac54,
\]
whereas its defining piecewise normalization gives $\tfrac32$. This discrepancy is visible in the typeset source, not just in text extraction. It does not establish a failure of the published asymptotic theorem. Our construction simply derives every identity from the unambiguous Proposition 5 using uniform division by $2^m$. With this convention, $e(3,1)=3/4$, and \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:walk} agrees with it.
\begin{writenotejp}
The values on the uniform side were rechecked at the write: $b(2,1)=2^2-1=3$, so $e(3,1)=B(2,1)/2!=\frac32/2=\frac34$, while the piecewise normalization $b(2,1)/2^{0}/2!$ gives $\frac32$. The statement about the printed pages 11:6--11:7 of the AofA 2020 paper, that their recurrence read uniformly gives $\frac54$, was not checked: the paper is not in the repository. It stays an unverified remark about a publication, like the remark on Ghosh Dastidar--Wallner in Part~0, and is Question~\ref{air:jp:q:normalization}. Part~\ref{air:part:dfa} uses the same uniform normalization (after \eqref{air:dfa:eq:count}) and remarks only that a normalization by $2^{m-1}$ for $m\ge1$ needs a separate boundary row. Report~103 itself says that the discrepancy is not a claim that the published asymptotic theorem is false.
\end{writenotejp}

\subsection{A counting comparison that survives the subtraction}
Let $R(n,0)=1$, $R(n,m)=0$ for $n<m$, and
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:relaxedtriangle}
 R(n,m)=R(n,m-1)+(m+1)R(n-1,m).
\end{equation}
This is the relaxed-tree triangle. Nonnegativity of the counting array $B$, followed by induction in $n$ and $m$ in \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:B}, gives
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:domination}
 0\le B(n,m)\le R(n,m).
\end{equation}
The exceptional lag $B(-1,0)=1$ at $(1,1)$ is nonnegative, so it does not invalidate the induction. This comparison concerns the actual counting solution. We will never infer positivity or contractivity for the signed recurrence on arbitrary initial vectors.

\section{The relaxed Jacobi estimates used by the proof}\label{air:jp:dfa:sec:jacobi}
All finite vectors are extended by zero to $\ell^2(\mathbb N_0)$. Matrix formulas below are restricted to their actual input and output supports.
\begin{writenotejp}
Report~103 continues by restating Report~100's definitions (Section~\ref{air:jp:rb:sec:jacobi}): the two-step operator $A_n$ on coordinates $0,\ldots,n$ with its exceptional first row, \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:boundaryA}--\eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:LD}; the symmetrizer $S_n$ \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:S}; the symmetric positive semidefinite $J_n=S_n^{-1}A_nS_n$, whose adjacent coefficient between $k-1$ and $k$ it calls $j_n(k)$ (Report~100's $b_n(k)$, \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:b}); the top eigenvalue $\lambda_n$, the positive unit eigenvector $\phi_n$, $H_n=J_n/\lambda_n$ and $\Lambda_n=\prod_{j\le n}\lambda_j$. These are not reprinted. Report~103 writes $R_n^g=S_n^{-1}S_{n-1}$ for Report~100's gauge $R_n$ \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:R} (defined on common support and zero otherwise; the superscript keeps $R$ free for the relaxed triangle \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:relaxedtriangle}) and $T_n=H_nR_n^g$, which is Report~100's $T_n$. Proposition~\ref{air:jp:dfa:prop:input} is kept, because the rest of Report~103 cites it display by display; its norms are Euclidean ($\normtwo{\cdot}$ throughout Report~103).
\end{writenotejp}

\begin{proposition}[Imported relaxed spectral theorem]\label{air:jp:dfa:prop:input}
For constants independent of sufficiently large $n$, and for each fixed $p\ge0$,
\begin{align}
 \lambda_n&=4+4an^{-2/3}+6n^{-1}+O(n^{-4/3}),\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:lambda}\\
 \normtwo{(I-\phi_n\phi_n^{\mathsf T})H_n}&\le1-cn^{-2/3},\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:gap}\\
 \normtwo{(k+1)^p\phi_n}&\le C_pn^{p/3},&
 \normtwo{\phi_n-\phi_{n-1}}&\le C/n,\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:drift}\\
 \normtwo{Z\phi_n-\phi_n}&\le Cn^{-1/3},&
 \phi_n(0)&=\sqrt2 n^{-1/2}[1+O(n^{-2/3})],\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:endphi}
\end{align}
where $(Zf)(k)=f(k-1)$ and $f(-1)=0$. The gauge satisfies
\begin{align}
 0\le R_n^g\le I,&\qquad 1-R_n^g(k)\le Ck^2/n^2,\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:gaugebound}\\
 \normtwo{(I-R_n^g)\phi_n}+\normtwo{(I-R_n^g)\phi_{n-1}}&\le Cn^{-4/3}.\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:gaugevector}
\end{align}
With $\eps=n^{-1/3}$ and $X_k=(k+1/2)\eps$, the normalized samples
\[
 q_n(k)=\sqrt{2\eps}\,\frac{\Ai(a+2X_k)}{\Ai'(a)}
\]
after a scalar normalization $1+O(\eps^2)$ satisfy $\normtwo{\phi_n-q_n}=O(\eps^2)$.
The relaxed counting solution $d_n(k)=R(n+k,n-k)/(n+k)!$ obeys
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:relaxednorm}
 v_n^r=\Lambda_n^{-1}S_n^{-1}d_n,\qquad \normtwo{v_n^r}\le1.
\end{equation}
Finally, for $0\le l\le\lfloor n^{2/3}\rfloor$,
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:smoothing}
 \normtwo{\delta_0^{\mathsf T}T_n\cdots T_{n-l+1}}
 \le C(l+1)^{-3/4}.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
The proof is in Report~100's Sections 2--5 (Sections~\ref{air:jp:rb:sec:jacobi}--\ref{air:jp:rb:sec:smoothing} here); all normalizations here coincide with that proof. In particular, $\Ai'$ denotes the derivative with respect to the Airy argument. The half-grid shift corresponds to the Dirichlet point $k=-1/2$, which is forced by the exceptional first row of $A_n$.

The smoothing bound \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:smoothing} is used later for signed forcing. Once a row norm bound is known, it bounds the row applied to \emph{any signed vector} by Cauchy--Schwarz.
\begin{writenotejp}
Report~103 adds an orientation paragraph and a subsection ``The smoothing mechanism'' that recall Report~100's proofs: the Dirichlet operator $-\partial_X^2+8X$ with eigenvalues $-4a_j$ and unit ground state $\sqrt2\Ai(a+2X)/\Ai'(a)$, normalized by $\int_a^\infty\Ai^2=\Ai'(a)^2$ (\eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:continuum}); the quasimode, gap, localization and drift (Sections~\ref{air:jp:rb:sec:airy}--\ref{air:jp:rb:sec:localization}); and the killed lazy-walk kernel $P=J_\infty/4$ with $P^m(0,0)\le C(m+1)^{-3/2}$ and the entrywise domination $0\le T_n\cdots T_{n-l+1}\le(\prod4/\lambda_j)P^l$ (Section~\ref{air:jp:rb:sec:smoothing}). They repeat Report~100 and are not reprinted; the Cauchy--Schwarz remark above is Report~103's own and is kept. Each line of Proposition~\ref{air:jp:dfa:prop:input} is a line of Report~100 or of Report~101's Lemma~\ref{air:jp:cb:lem:import} (the shift bound), the sampled profile bound is \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:profile}, and \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:relaxednorm} is the contraction stated after \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:product}.
\end{writenotejp}

\section{The exact signed three lag equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:sec:memory}
Put
\[
 u_n(k)=e(2n,2k)\quad(0\le k\le n),\qquad
 o_n(k)=e(2n-1,2k+1)\quad(0\le k\le n-1).
\]
The even and odd forms of \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:walk} are
\begin{align}
 u_n(0)&=o_n(0),\notag\\
 u_n(k)&=\alpha_n(k)o_n(k-1)+o_n(k)-g_n(k)o_{n-1}(k-1),&&k\ge1,\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:evenodd}\\
 o_n(k)&=p_n(k)u_{n-1}(k)+u_{n-1}(k+1)-q_n(k)u_{n-2}(k),\notag
\end{align}
where
\begin{align*}
 \alpha_n(k)&=\frac{n-k+1}{n+k},&g_n(k)&=\frac{n-k}{2(n+k)(n+k-1)},\\
 p_n(k)&=\frac{n-k}{n+k},&q_n(k)&=\frac{n-k-1}{2(n+k)(n+k-1)}.
\end{align*}
Eliminating odd arrays gives, for $n\ge4$,
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:memory}
 u_n=A_nu_{n-1}-\mathcal B_nu_{n-2}+\mathcal F_nu_{n-3}.
\end{equation}
The two memory matrices are
\begin{align}
 (\mathcal B_nw)(0)&=\frac{w(0)}{2n},& (\mathcal F_nw)(0)&=0,\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:memoryorigin}\\
 (\mathcal B_nw)(k)&=P_n(k)w(k-1)+Q_n(k)w(k),&
 (\mathcal F_nw)(k)&=W_n(k)w(k-1),\notag
\end{align}
with, for $k\ge1$,
\begin{align}
 P_n(k)&=\frac{(n-k)(2n-2k+1)}{2(n+k)(n+k-1)(n+k-2)},\notag\\
 Q_n(k)&=\frac{2n-2k-1}{2(n+k)(n+k-1)},\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:bands}\\
 W_n(k)&=\frac{(n-k)(n-k-1)}{4(n+k)(n+k-1)(n+k-2)(n+k-3)}.\notag
\end{align}
The input support of $\mathcal B_n$ is $0,\ldots,n-2$; that of $\mathcal F_n$ is $0,\ldots,n-3$. Outside these supports there is no entry. In particular, a formula that could look negative beyond its support is not a matrix coefficient. Every actual entry in both matrices is nonnegative, but the sign of $\mathcal B_n$ in \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:memory} is negative.

For completeness, the five elimination identities at $k\ge1$ are
\begin{align*}
 L_n(k)&=\alpha_n(k)p_n(k-1),&D_n(k)&=\alpha_n(k)+p_n(k),\\
 P_n(k)&=\alpha_n(k)q_n(k-1)+g_n(k)p_{n-1}(k-1),\\
 Q_n(k)&=q_n(k)+g_n(k),&W_n(k)&=g_n(k)q_{n-1}(k-1).
\end{align*}
At the origin, $p_n(0)=1$ and $q_n(0)=1/(2n)$, so
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:origin}
 u_n(0)=u_{n-1}(0)+u_{n-1}(1)-\frac1{2n}u_{n-2}(0).
\end{equation}
These identities prove \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:memory} at every row, including the boundary; finite exact checks in the source package supply additional transcription tests.

\subsection{Global conjugated memory bounds}
Set
\[
 \widetilde{\mathcal B}_n=S_n^{-1}\mathcal B_nS_{n-2},\qquad
 \widetilde{\mathcal F}_n=S_n^{-1}\mathcal F_nS_{n-3}.
\]
\begin{lemma}\label{air:jp:dfa:lem:memorybounds}
Uniformly on the full actual supports,
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:memorybounds}
 \normtwo{\widetilde{\mathcal B}_n}\le C/n,\qquad
 \normtwo{\widetilde{\mathcal F}_n}\le C/n^2,\qquad
 \normtwo{\widetilde{\mathcal B}_n\phi_{n-2}-\tfrac2n\phi_n}\le Cn^{-4/3}.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For a diagonal memory entry, the gauge ratio is $R_n^g(k)R_{n-1}^g(k)\le1$. A lower-diagonal entry has this two-factor product at $k-1$, divided by $j_n(k)$; for $\widetilde{\mathcal F}_n$ there is one further factor $R_{n-2}^g(k-1)$. Direct cancellation yields
\begin{align*}
 \frac{P_n(k)}{j_n(k)}
 &=\frac{n-k}{n-k+1}\frac{2n-2k+1}
 {2(n+k-2)\sqrt{(n+k)(n+k-1)}}\le\frac Cn,\\
 \frac{W_n(k)}{j_n(k)}
 &=\frac{(n-k)(n-k-1)}{n-k+1}
 \frac1{4(n+k-2)(n+k-3)\sqrt{(n+k)(n+k-1)}}\le\frac C{n^2}.
\end{align*}
Together with $Q_n$ and the exact row zero, these estimates prove the operator bounds because only finitely many bands occur.

On the actual input support of $\widetilde{\mathcal B}_n$, write
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:V}
 \widetilde{\mathcal B}_n=\frac1n(I+Z)-\frac1{2n}\Pi_0+V_n,
\end{equation}
where $\Pi_0$ is projection onto coordinate zero. Each band of $V_n$ has row-$k$ entry bounded by $C(k+1)/n^2$. For $k\le n/2$, expand the displayed rational and square-root coefficients. The gauge error is $O(k^2/n^3)$, which is at most $C(k+1)/n^2$. At zero, the correction $-\Pi_0/(2n)$ gives exactly the required $1/(2n)$. For $k>n/2$, the global $O(1/n)$ bounds already imply the stated estimate, including support-imposed zeros.

The moment estimate gives $\normtwo{V_n\phi_{n-2}}\le Cn^{-5/3}$. Applying \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:V}, the shift estimate, $|\phi_{n-2}(0)|\le\normtwo{Z\phi_{n-2}-\phi_{n-2}}$, and the two-step drift gives the last assertion of \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:memorybounds}.
\end{proof}

\section{Convergence of the leading amplitude}\label{air:jp:dfa:sec:leading}
Choose the scalar normalization
\begin{align}
 \kappa_n&=\lambda_n-\frac1{2n},&\Gamma_n&=\prod_{j=1}^n\kappa_j,&
 v_n&=\Gamma_n^{-1}S_n^{-1}u_n,\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:normalize}\\
 \rho_n&=\lambda_n/\kappa_n,&
 M_n&=\frac{\widetilde{\mathcal B}_n}{\kappa_n\kappa_{n-1}},&
 N_n&=\frac{\widetilde{\mathcal F}_n}{\kappa_n\kappa_{n-1}\kappa_{n-2}}.\notag
\end{align}
Every $\kappa_n$ is positive: $\lambda_1=3/2$ gives $\kappa_1=1$, and the zero-coordinate Rayleigh quotient gives $\lambda_n\ge1>1/(2n)$ for $n\ge2$. Equations \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:lambda} and \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:memorybounds} imply
\begin{align}
 \kappa_n&=4+4an^{-2/3}+\frac{11}{2n}+O(n^{-4/3}),\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:kappa}\\
 v_n&=\rho_nT_nv_{n-1}-M_nv_{n-2}+N_nv_{n-3},\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:normalizedmemory}\\
 \rho_n&=1+\frac1{8n}+O(n^{-5/3}),\notag\\
 \normtwo{M_n}&\le C/n,\qquad \normtwo{N_n}\le C/n^2,\notag\\
 \normtwo{M_n\phi_{n-2}-\tfrac1{8n}\phi_n}&\le Cn^{-4/3}.\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:normalizedbounds}
\end{align}
The scalar $1/(8n)$ in the last line is exactly the drift to be canceled.

\subsection{The initial bound comes from counting}
The diagonal gauges and products are positive. Equations \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:domination} and \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:relaxednorm} therefore imply, coordinatewise,
\[
 0\le v_n\le\frac{\Lambda_n}{\Gamma_n}v_n^r.
\]
Moreover,
\[
 \log\frac{\Lambda_n}{\Gamma_n}
 =\sum_{j=1}^n-\log\left(1-\frac1{2j\lambda_j}\right)
 =\tfrac18\log n+O(1).
\]
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:apriori}
 \normtwo{v_n}\le Cn^{1/8}.
\end{equation}
This weak polynomial bound is enough to start the argument. It is not obtained by treating the signed evolution as a contraction.

\subsection{A damped scalar estimate}
We use the following elementary fact. If $x_n\ge0$ obeys
\[
 x_n\le(1-cn^{-2/3})x_{n-1}+Cn^{-r}
\]
for all large $n$, then $x_n=O(n^{2/3-r})$; exponentially decaying contributions from a fixed initial segment are harmless. Indeed iteration bounds the propagation from $j$ to $n$ by
\[
 \exp\left(-c\sum_{i=j+1}^n i^{-2/3}\right).
\]
For $j\ge n/2$, summing this bound costs $O(n^{2/3})$ and $j^{-r}=O(n^{-r})$. For $j<n/2$, the exponential factor is $O(e^{-c'n^{1/3}})$ and absorbs every fixed polynomial. The same argument applies to a finite sum of polynomial forcings and will be used again for signed vectors after taking norms.

\subsection{Perpendicular tracking and scalar cancellation}
Write
\[
 A_n^*=\ip{\phi_n}{v_n}\ge0,\quad w_n=v_n-A_n^*\phi_n,
 \quad\beta_n=\normtwo{w_n}.
\]
The superscript distinguishes the scalar projection from the matrix $A_n$. Projection of \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:normalizedmemory} onto the perpendicular complement of $\phi_n$, using the gap and absorbing $\rho_n-1=O(n^{-1})$ into it, gives
\begin{align}
 \beta_n&\le(1-cn^{-2/3})\beta_{n-1}+\frac Cn|A_{n-1}^*|\notag\\
 &\quad+\frac Cn\normtwo{v_{n-2}}+\frac C{n^2}\normtwo{v_{n-3}}.\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:perp}
\end{align}
The forcing from \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:apriori} is $O(n^{-7/8})$, so the preceding scalar estimate yields
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:weakperp}
 \beta_n=O(n^{-5/24}).
\end{equation}
The crude scalar projection gives $\Delta A_n^*=O(n^{-7/8})$, where $\Delta A_n^*=A_n^*-A_{n-1}^*$.

To obtain a summable increment, use
\begin{align}
 \ip{\phi_n}{R_n^g\phi_{n-1}}&=1+O(n^{-4/3}),\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:scalaroverlap}\\
 \normtwo{R_n^g\phi_n-\phi_{n-1}}&\le C/n.\notag
\end{align}
The first equality follows from the gauge estimate and
$\ip{\phi_n}{\phi_{n-1}}-1=-\normtwo{\phi_n-\phi_{n-1}}^2/2$.
Combining \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:scalaroverlap} with \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:normalizedbounds} gives the exact scalar equation
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:cancel}
 \Delta A_n^*=\frac1{8n}\Delta A_{n-1}^*+\eta_n,
\end{equation}
where
\begin{align}
 |\eta_n|&\le Cn^{-4/3}(|A_{n-1}^*|+|A_{n-2}^*|)
       +Cn^{-2}|A_{n-3}^*|\notag\\
 &\quad+\frac Cn(\beta_{n-1}+\beta_{n-2})+\frac C{n^2}\beta_{n-3}.\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:eta}
\end{align}
All perpendicular contributions may have either sign. In particular the leading positive scalar term $A_{n-1}^*/(8n)$ is canceled by the negative memory projection $-A_{n-2}^*/(8n)$; their difference produces the delayed increment in \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:cancel}.

Substituting \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:apriori} and \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:weakperp} into \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:eta} gives $\eta_n=O(n^{-29/24})$. The crude increment makes the delayed term $O(n^{-15/8})$. Hence $\sum_n|\Delta A_n^*|<\infty$ and $A_n^*\to A_\infty\ge0$. This first proves boundedness of $v_n$. Repeating \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:perp} now gives $\beta_n=O(n^{-1/3})$; the crude scalar increment becomes $O(1/n)$, then \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:cancel} improves it to $O(n^{-4/3})$. Summing its tail proves
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:profilelimit}
 |A_n^*-A_\infty|+\normtwo{v_n-A_\infty\phi_n}\le Cn^{-1/3}.
\end{equation}
This order of estimates avoids any circular assumption of boundedness.

\subsection{Boundary smoothing and positivity}
Set $z_n=v_n-A_\infty\phi_n$. Rewrite the exact equation as
\[
 z_n=T_nz_{n-1}+h_n,
\]
where
\begin{align*}
 h_n={}&(\rho_n-1)T_nv_{n-1}-M_nv_{n-2}+N_nv_{n-3}\\
 &+A_\infty(T_n\phi_{n-1}-\phi_n).
\end{align*}
Boundedness of $v_n$, the memory bounds, and the drift imply $\normtwo{h_n}\le C/n$. Iterate over $L=\lfloor n^{2/3}\rfloor$ steps and use \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:smoothing}:
\begin{align*}
 |z_n(0)|&\le C(L+1)^{-3/4}n^{-1/3}
 +\frac Cn\sum_{l=0}^{L-1}(l+1)^{-3/4}
 =O(n^{-5/6}).
\end{align*}
It follows that
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:endlimit}
 v_n(0)=\sqrt2 A_\infty n^{-1/2}+O(n^{-5/6}).
\end{equation}
Summation of $\log\kappa_n$ in \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:kappa} gives a finite positive $K_D$ with
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:product}
 \Gamma_n=K_D4^ne^{3an^{1/3}}n^{11/8}[1+O(n^{-1/3})].
\end{equation}
If $A_\infty=0$, equations \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:diagonal}, \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:endlimit}, and \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:product} would give
\[
 b_n=O\!\left(n!8^ne^{3an^{1/3}}n^{13/24}\right).
\]
But \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:compactedlower} has polynomial power $3/4=18/24>13/24$, a contradiction. Therefore $A_\infty>0$ and
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:gamma}
 \gamma_D=\sqrt2 K_DA_\infty\in(0,\infty).
\end{equation}
Multiplication of \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:endlimit} by $2^n n!\Gamma_n$ proves Theorem~\ref{air:jp:dfa:thm:main} for $m=0$.
\begin{writenotejp}
The contradiction uses only \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:compactedlower}. With the compacted amplitude now proved (Report~101 and Part~\ref{air:part:cb}), the second half of \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:compactedlower} is also a consequence of $\gamma_c>0$; the analytic input is then the published compacted lower bound or, equivalently for this purpose, either compacted amplitude theorem, each of which itself rests on that bound. An intrinsic argument in the manner of Lemma~\ref{air:jp:rb:lem:positive} is Question~\ref{air:jp:q:intrinsic}.
\end{writenotejp}

\section{A convergent characterization of the amplitude}\label{air:jp:dfa:sec:amplitude}
The proof provides exact quantities rather than an unspecified subsequential constant. With $\kappa_n$ defined from finite Jacobi matrices,
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:canonicalproduct}
 K_D=\frac{\kappa_1}{4e^{3a}}
 \prod_{j=2}^\infty
 \frac{\kappa_j}
 {4\exp(3a[j^{1/3}-(j-1)^{1/3}])\,(j/(j-1))^{11/8}}.
\end{equation}
The logarithm of the $j$th factor is $O(j^{-4/3})$, so the product converges to a positive finite value. The leading discrepancy is summable because the denominator matches both the $j^{-2/3}$ and $j^{-1}$ terms of $\log\kappa_j$.

For the actual counting solution initialized by Proposition 5,
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:canonicalseries}
 A_\infty=A_3^*+\sum_{n=4}^\infty(A_n^*-A_{n-1}^*),
 \qquad A_n^*=\ip{\phi_n}{\Gamma_n^{-1}S_n^{-1}u_n}.
\end{equation}
This series is absolutely convergent by \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:cancel}--\eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:profilelimit}. Together \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:gamma}--\eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:canonicalseries} define $\gamma_D$ canonically using exact counts and finite-dimensional spectral data.

These formulas establish existence and uniqueness; they are not a closed form. The $O$-constants in the current proof are not effective numerical enclosures. Truncating these expressions with floating-point spectral computations does not, by itself, certify any digits of $\gamma_D$.

\section{An exact formal construction at every depth}\label{air:jp:dfa:sec:formal}
We now construct an asymptotic solution to \emph{all finite orders}, then prove that it describes the actual counts. Use the variables
\[
 t=N^{-1/3},\qquad x=(h+1)t,\qquad \varkappa=2^{2/3}a,
 \qquad A(x)=\Ai(a+2^{1/3}x).
\]
Thus $A''=(2x+\varkappa)A$. Derivatives in this section are with respect to $x$ unless explicitly stated otherwise. Put $t_j=t(1-jt^3)^{-1/3}$. The two rational coefficients of \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:walk} become
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:WD}
 W(t,x)=\frac{1-xt^2+3t^3}{1+xt^2-t^3},\qquad
 D(t,x)=\frac{t^3(1-xt^2+t^3)}{(1+xt^2-t^3)(1+xt^2-3t^3)}.
\end{equation}
In particular, the numerator in the second factor is $+t^3$ and the leading scalar is $t^3$. Both follow directly by substituting $h=x/t-1$, $N=t^{-3}$ into the DFA memory coefficient.

Seek compatible formal series
\begin{align}
 s(t)&=2+\varkappa t^2+\sum_{j\ge3}\sigma_jt^j,\notag\\
 F(t,x)&=\sum_{j\ge0}t^jf_j(x),\qquad
 f_j=P_jA+Q_jA',\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:formalansatz}\\
 P_0&=1,\quad Q_0=0,\qquad P_j,Q_j\in\mathbb Q[\varkappa,x],\notag\\
 Q_j(0)&=0,\qquad P_j(0)+Q_j'(0)=0\quad(j\ge1).\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:gauges}
\end{align}
The formal equation is
\begin{align}
 s(t)F(t,x)={}&W(t,x)F\left(t_1,(x-t)(1-t^3)^{-1/3}\right)\notag\\
 &+F\left(t_1,(x+t)(1-t^3)^{-1/3}\right)\notag\\
 &-\frac{D(t,x)}{s(t_1)s(t_2)}
 F\left(t_3,(x-t)(1-3t^3)^{-1/3}\right).\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:formal}
\end{align}
If $H_N/H_{N-1}=s(N^{-1/3})$, the denominator in the last line is simply $H_{N-1}/H_{N-3}$. No recurrence for the actual counting amplitude is being assumed.

\begin{proposition}[Triangular polynomial algorithm]\label{air:jp:dfa:prop:formal}
Equations \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:formalansatz}--\eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:formal} have a unique solution with the stated gauges at every formal degree. Every coefficient belongs to $\mathbb Q[\varkappa,x]$ or $\mathbb Q[\varkappa]$ as appropriate. Every profile vanishes exactly at $x=0$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The degree-two equation is $A''-(2x+\varkappa)A=0$. At degree $t^k$, $k\ge3$, the new profile $f_{k-2}=PA+QA'$ and scalar $\sigma_k$ occur as
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:newoperator}
 (\partial_x^2-2x-\varkappa)(PA+QA')-\sigma_k A+R_0A+R_1A'=0,
\end{equation}
where $R_0,R_1$ are already determined polynomials. Memory starts at degree three, so the new profile cannot enter it before degree $k+1$; the new scalar enters its denominator still later. Thus memory does not change the new-unknown operator.

Write $v=2x+\varkappa$. Direct differentiation gives
\[
 (\partial_x^2-v)(PA+QA')=(P''+2vQ'+2Q)A+(2P'+Q'')A'.
\]
Eliminating $P$ from \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:newoperator} gives
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:triangular}
 \mathcal TQ=2R_0-R_1'-2\sigma_k,\qquad
 \mathcal TQ=Q'''-4(2x+\varkappa)Q'-4Q.
\end{equation}
On polynomials of degree at most $d$, this operator is triangular with nonzero diagonal entries $-4(2j+1)$, $0\le j\le d$. It is therefore invertible over $\mathbb Q[\varkappa]$. Define
\begin{align}
 \widetilde Q&=\mathcal T^{-1}(2R_0-R_1'),&
 \sigma_k&=-2\widetilde Q(0),\notag\\
 Q&=\widetilde Q+\sigma_k/2,&
 P(x)&=\int_0^x\frac{-R_1(u)-Q''(u)}2\,\mathrm d u-Q'(0).\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:solver}
\end{align}
These equations satisfy both coefficients in \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:newoperator} and both gauges. They uniquely determine the new data. Induction proves the construction at arbitrary depth. Since $A(0)=0$ and $Q_j(0)=0$, every $f_j(0)=0$ exactly. In fact the second gauge also gives $f_j'(0)=0$ for $j\ge1$.
\end{proof}

At the first new degree,
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:sigma3}
 f_1=-\tfrac23x^2A,\qquad \sigma_3=\tfrac83-\tfrac14=\tfrac{29}{12}.
\end{equation}
The term $-1/4$ is precisely the leading memory term $-D/[s(t_1)s(t_2)]$. The next scalar coefficients, useful for reproduction, are
\begin{align}
 \sigma_4&=\frac{29\varkappa^2}{540},&
 \sigma_5&=-\frac{103\varkappa}{648},\notag\\
 \sigma_6&=\frac{5341815-70264\varkappa^3}{2721600},&
 \sigma_7&=-\frac{767\varkappa^2}{23328}.\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:sigmas}
\end{align}
For example,
\[
 P_2=\frac{16\varkappa+240x^4+1641x}{1080},\qquad
 Q_2=\frac{x(-4\varkappa+51x)}{270}.
\]
The source package contains all profiles through $f_5$ and checks the complete two-component formal residual through $t^7$. Proposition~\ref{air:jp:dfa:prop:formal}, rather than those finite checks, is the proof that the algorithm continues indefinitely.
\begin{writenotejp}
With $\varkappa=2^{2/3}a=2\cdot2^{-1/3}a$, the scalars here are twice Part~\ref{air:part:dfa}'s ratio coefficients: $\sigma_m=2s_m$ in \eqref{air:dfa:eq:ratio-coefficients} for $m=3,\ldots,6$ (checked at the write; Remark~\ref{air:jp:rem:sigma} proves it for every $m$). The coefficient $\sigma_7$ is new to this report; in Part~\ref{air:part:dfa}'s normalization, where the letter $a$ means $2^{-1/3}$ times the Airy zero, it reads $s_7=-767a^2/11664$. The formal coefficient $D$ of \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:WD} is Part~\ref{air:part:dfa}'s $\beta$ \eqref{air:dfa:eq:formal-coeffs}.
\end{writenotejp}

\section{Compatible carriers and genuine residuals}\label{air:jp:dfa:sec:residual}
\subsection{One carrier normalization at every depth}
There is a unique sequence $\ell_j\in\mathbb Q[\varkappa]$ satisfying the formal identity
\begin{align}
 \log\frac{s(t)}2={}&\frac{3\varkappa}{2t}\left[1-(1-t^3)^{1/3}\right]
 -\frac{29}{24}\log(1-t^3)\notag\\
 &+\sum_{j\ge1}\ell_jt^j\left[1-(1-t^3)^{-j/3}\right].\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:carrieridentity}
\end{align}
The degree-two and degree-three coefficients agree by \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:sigma3}. At degree $j+3$, the new $\ell_j$ has nonzero coefficient $-j/3$, proving existence and uniqueness successively.

For fixed $M\ge2$, take the explicit carrier with multiplicative constant one,
\begin{align}
 H_N^{[M]}&=2^N\exp\left(\tfrac32\varkappa N^{1/3}\right)N^{29/24}
 \exp\left(\sum_{j=1}^M\ell_jN^{-j/3}\right),\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:carrier}\\
 F^{[M]}(t,x)&=\sum_{j=0}^Mt^jf_j(x).\notag
\end{align}
Its exact consecutive ratio agrees with $s(t)$ through degree $M+3$. If computing $\ell_M$ requires a profile beyond $f_M$, Proposition~\ref{air:jp:dfa:prop:formal} supplies it. We do not use independent products of truncated ratios with arbitrary starting values: that would introduce depth-dependent amplitude constants.

In even time set $\eps=n^{-1/3}$ and $X=(k+1/2)\eps$. Then $t=2^{-1/3}\eps$ and $x=2^{2/3}X$. Define
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:U}
 U_n^{[M]}(k)=\frac{2^{-29/24}}{\Ai'(a)}H_{2n}^{[M]}F^{[M]}(t,x).
\end{equation}
Here $\Ai'$ is the Airy-argument derivative, not $A'$. The leading expression is
\[
 \frac{4^ne^{3an^{1/3}}n^{29/24}}{\Ai'(a)}\Ai(a+2X).
\]
At $k=0$, $\Ai(a+\eps)/\Ai'(a)=\eps+O(\eps^3)$. If
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:scales}
 B_n^{\rm end}=4^ne^{3an^{1/3}}n^{7/8},\qquad
 D_n^{\rm bulk}=4^ne^{3an^{1/3}}n^{29/24},
\end{equation}
then $U_n^{[M]}(0)=B_n^{\rm end}[1+O(\eps)]$, since $29/24-1/3=7/8$. At every finite order its endpoint Taylor series has compatible coefficients. Indeed a newly retained $t^jf_j(x)$ vanishes at $x=0$, so its relative endpoint contribution begins no earlier than $\eps^j$; the new carrier term also begins at that order. The leading coefficient is one at every depth.

\subsection{Uniform finite remainders for the one step equation}
For every fixed $M$ and $C_0$, the exact residual obtained by substituting
\[
 H_N^{[M]}F^{[M]}(t,(h+1)t)
\]
into \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:walk}, after division by $H_N^{[M]}$, is bounded on $0\le h\le C_0\sqrt N$ by
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:onestepresidual}
 Ct^{M+3}(1+x)^d e^{-cx^{3/2}}.
\end{equation}
Here $d,C,c$ may depend on the fixed depth. This is a finite-remainder estimate, not just a formal coefficient cancellation, as we now verify.

On the region in question, $x\le Ct^{-1/2}$ and $|xt^2|\le Ct^{3/2}$. The denominators in \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:WD} stay separated from zero. Finite rational expansions thus have remainder bounded by the prescribed power of $t$ times a polynomial in $x$. The delayed carrier ratios $H_{N-j}^{[M]}/H_N^{[M]}$, $j\le3$, are bounded and have ordinary finite-order expansions with bounded remainders: the apparent $t^{-1}$ in their exponential multiplies a difference vanishing at order $t^3$. The shifted profile arguments differ from $x$ by $O(t)$ uniformly, since their difference is $\pm t+O(xt^3+t^4)$.

Every derivative of a fixed profile is a polynomial--Airy pair. On the relevant Taylor intervals it obeys $C(1+x)^d e^{-cx^{3/2}}$; for bounded $x$ this follows from smoothness, and for large $x$ from Airy decay. Products of the finitely many expansions preserve such an envelope. Equation \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:formal} cancels all powers through $t^{M+2}$; memory begins at order three and creates no further loss. This proves \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:onestepresidual}.

At $h=0$, both lower evaluations have argument exactly zero, not an approximate zero. Every retained $f_j$ vanishes there before Taylor expansion. Thus this residual estimate includes the actual equation $e(N,0)=e(N-1,1)$, not merely a limiting continuum boundary condition.

\subsection{Exact elimination with residuals}
Use the same scalar factor $2^{-29/24}/\Ai'(a)$ for the odd approximation formed from $H_{2n-1}^{[M]}F^{[M]}$. Let $r_{\rm ev,n}$ and $r_{\rm od,n}$ be its residuals in \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:evenodd}. Let $K_n$ have diagonal one and subdiagonal $\alpha_n(k)$, and let $G_n$ have subdiagonal $g_n(k)$ and row zero zero. Direct elimination, retaining the residuals, gives
\begin{align}
 U_n-A_nU_{n-1}+\mathcal B_nU_{n-2}-\mathcal F_nU_{n-3}
 =r_{\rm ev,n}+K_nr_{\rm od,n}-G_nr_{\rm od,n-1}.\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:eliminateresidual}
\end{align}
On $k=O(\sqrt n)$, $K_n$ is bounded and $G_n=O(1/n)$ in its coefficients; the needed adjacent carrier ratios are bounded. Hence \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:onestepresidual} gives the same $\eps^{M+3}$ pointwise envelope after elimination. Row zero is included because $K_n$ retains the origin and $G_n$ vanishes there.

\subsection{A cutoff at every memory lag}
Choose a fixed smooth $\chi$ equal to one on $[0,1/2]$ and zero on $[1,\infty)$, and put
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:cutoff}
 \widetilde U_n^{[M]}(k)=\chi(k/\sqrt n)U_n^{[M]}(k).
\end{equation}
Whenever cutoffs at times $n,n-1,n-2,n-3$ or adjacent heights differ, $k\asymp\sqrt n$. Then $X\asymp n^{1/6}$, and each fixed profile times a bounded carrier ratio is a polynomial in $n$ times $e^{-cn^{1/4}}$. All cutoff defects are therefore smaller than every prescribed algebraic power. There is no need to cancel different lagged cutoff terms against one another.

The residual is supported at $k\le\sqrt n+O(1)$. Since the $X$-mesh is $\eps$, the sum of the squared envelope in \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:onestepresidual} is $O(\eps^{-1})$. The unnormalized residual thus has $\ell^2$ norm
\[
 O(D_n^{\rm bulk}\eps^{M+5/2}).
\]
On its support the exact factorial gauge satisfies
\[
 -\log S_n(k)=O(k^2/n+k/n)=O(1),
\]
so $S_n^{-1}$ is uniformly bounded there. Moreover \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:product} gives
\[
 D_n^{\rm bulk}/\Gamma_n=O(n^{-1/6})=O(\eps^{1/2}).
\]
It follows that the genuine normalized defect obeys
\begin{align}
 \bigl\|\Gamma_n^{-1}S_n^{-1}\bigl[\widetilde U_n^{[M]}-A_n\widetilde U_{n-1}^{[M]}
 +\mathcal B_n\widetilde U_{n-2}^{[M]}-\mathcal F_n\widetilde U_{n-3}^{[M]}\bigr]\bigr\|_2
 =O(n^{-(M+3)/3}).\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:residualnorm}
\end{align}
In particular, no bound on the inverse gauge has been extended to the distant upper edge where it can be large.

\subsection{Matching the same Perron amplitude}
Proposition~\ref{air:jp:dfa:prop:input} and \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:product} imply
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:matching}
 \normtwo{\Gamma_n^{-1}S_n^{-1}\widetilde U_n^{[M]}
 -\frac{\phi_n}{\sqrt2K_D}}=O(\eps).
\end{equation}
To check the constant, the normalized leading expression in \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:U} is $K_D^{-1}\eps^{1/2}\Ai(a+2X)/\Ai'(a)$, whereas the unit sample has factor $\sqrt{2\eps}$. The finite scalar exponential and higher profiles change this by $O(\eps)$ in the weighted norm. The gauge correction is also $O(\eps)$: on the cutoff support its logarithm is bounded and is at most $C\eps(1+X^2)$, whose weighted Airy norm is finite. Cutoff tails are exponentially small and the normalized frozen sample error is $O(\eps^2)$. This proves \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:matching}, with the identical constant at every $M$.

\section{Forced signed memory and the all orders theorem}\label{air:jp:dfa:sec:transfer}
\begin{lemma}[Transfer with zero limiting projection]\label{air:jp:dfa:lem:forced}
Suppose finite-support vectors $z_n$ satisfy
\[
 z_n=\rho_nT_nz_{n-1}-M_nz_{n-2}+N_nz_{n-3}+f_n,
\]
where $\normtwo{f_n}=O(n^{-p})$, $p>4/3$, and $\normtwo{z_n}=O(n^{-1/3})$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:forcedconclusion}
 \normtwo{z_n}=O(n^{-(p-1)}),\qquad z_n(0)=O(n^{-1/2-(p-1)}).
\end{equation}
No sign assumption is imposed on the vectors or forcing.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Put $\alpha_n=\ip{\phi_n}{z_n}$ and $\beta_n=\normtwo{(I-\phi_n\phi_n^{\mathsf T})z_n}$. Assume provisionally $\normtwo{z_n}=O(n^{-q})$, starting with $q=1/3$. The perpendicular gap estimate and the damped comparison give
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:forcedperp}
 \beta_n=O(n^{-q-1/3}+n^{-p+2/3}).
\end{equation}
The crude scalar equation gives $\Delta\alpha_n=O(n^{-q-1}+n^{-p})$. The refined projection has exactly the same cancellation coefficient as \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:cancel}:
\[
 \Delta\alpha_n=\frac1{8n}\Delta\alpha_{n-1}+\eta_n+\ip{\phi_n}{f_n},
\]
with
\begin{align*}
 |\eta_n|&\le Cn^{-4/3}(|\alpha_{n-1}|+|\alpha_{n-2}|)
 +Cn^{-2}|\alpha_{n-3}|\\
 &\quad+\frac Cn(\beta_{n-1}+\beta_{n-2})+\frac C{n^2}\beta_{n-3}.
\end{align*}
Using \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:forcedperp} and the crude increment yields
\[
 \Delta\alpha_n=O(n^{-q-4/3}+n^{-p}).
\]
The initial norm decay implies $\alpha_n\to0$. Summing the absolutely convergent tail therefore gives
\[
 \alpha_n=O(n^{-q-1/3}+n^{-p+1}).
\]
Together with \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:forcedperp}, this replaces $q$ by $\min(q+1/3,p-1)$. Finitely many repetitions reach $p-1$, proving the norm assertion, without a logarithmic loss.

Rewrite the equation as $z_n=T_nz_{n-1}+h_n$. The improved norm estimate and the $O(1/n)$, $O(1/n^2)$ memory bounds give $\normtwo{h_n}=O(n^{-p})$. Over $L=\lfloor n^{2/3}\rfloor$ steps, \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:smoothing} gives
\begin{align*}
 |z_n(0)|&\le CL^{-3/4}n^{-(p-1)}
 +Cn^{-p}\sum_{l=0}^{L-1}(l+1)^{-3/4}\\
 &=O(n^{-p+1/2})+O(n^{-p+1/6}).
\end{align*}
The first is the larger term and equals the endpoint bound in \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:forcedconclusion}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Completion of Theorem~\ref{air:jp:dfa:thm:main}]
Fix $M\ge2$ and set
\[
 z_n=v_n-\gamma_D\Gamma_n^{-1}S_n^{-1}\widetilde U_n^{[M]}.
\]
Equations \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:gamma}, \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:profilelimit}, and \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:matching} give the required initial $O(n^{-1/3})$ norm bound. The exact normalized defect \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:residualnorm} supplies forcing with $p=(M+3)/3>4/3$. Lemma~\ref{air:jp:dfa:lem:forced}, together with $\Gamma_n/B_n^{\rm end}=\Theta(n^{1/2})$, yields
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:transferend}
 \frac{u_n(0)-\gamma_D\widetilde U_n^{[M]}(0)}{B_n^{\rm end}}
 =O(n^{-M/3}).
\end{equation}
For desired order $m$, choose $M\ge\max(2,m+1)$. Expand the compatible endpoint expression of Section~\ref{air:jp:dfa:sec:residual} through order $m$; its omitted terms and \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:transferend} are $O(n^{-(m+1)/3})$. Since the cutoff equals one at zero, multiplication by $2^n n!$ proves \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:main}. Formal logarithm gives \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:logmain}. The amplitude is always the already fixed positive $\gamma_D$, not a constant reselected at each depth. Uniqueness of successive asymptotic coefficients follows by subtracting two expansions and taking the first differing order.
\end{proof}

The approximants and transfer errors may be signed. Positivity was needed only for the actual counting solution and for the relaxed comparison propagator; the positive amplitude itself was supplied by the structural compacted-tree lower bound.

\section{Explicit coefficients and numerical diagnostics}\label{air:jp:dfa:sec:coefficients}
Formal integration by \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:carrieridentity} and endpoint evaluation of $F(t,t)$ give the first four logarithmic corrections
\begin{align}
 d_1&=\frac{53a^2}{90},&d_2&=\frac{623a}{432},\notag\\
 d_3&=\frac{4248855-166912a^3}{5443200},&
 d_4&=-\frac{5429a^2}{19440}.\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:logcoeff}
\end{align}
Exponentiating the finite series yields
\begin{align}
 c_1&=\frac{53a^2}{90},\notag\\
 c_2&=\frac{a(5618a^3+46725)}{32400},\notag\\
 c_3&=\frac{8337112a^6+200508660a^3+191198475}{244944000},\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:coefficients}\\
 c_4&=\frac{a^2(220933468a^6+10228873860a^3+53801804475)}{44089920000}.\notag
\end{align}
For a direct check of the endpoint conversion, before changing from $t=(2n)^{-1/3}$ to $\eps=n^{-1/3}$ the normalized endpoint profile is
\[
 \frac{F(t,t)}{2^{1/3}\Ai'(a)t}
 =1+\frac{\varkappa}{6}t^2+\frac{29}{24}t^3+
 \frac{11\varkappa^2}{3240}t^4+O(t^5).
\]
Combining its logarithm with the scalar carrier gives \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:logcoeff}; the factor $2^{1/3}$ here is the chain-rule derivative of $A(x)$.

Taking sufficiently deep forward expansions before dividing consecutive terms gives the justified ratio expansion
\begin{align}
 \frac{b_n}{8n b_{n-1}}
 ={}&1+an^{-2/3}+\frac78n^{-1}+\frac{41a^2}{135}n^{-4/3}
 +\frac{20a}{81}n^{-5/3}\notag\\
 &+\frac{2816a^3+108135}{2721600}n^{-2}
 -\frac{881a^2}{7290}n^{-7/3}+O(n^{-8/3}).\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:ratio}
\end{align}
Merely shifting a shallow $O$-remainder does not improve its order. The all-orders theorem allows a deeper truncation, which is why the ratio remainder in \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:ratio} is legitimate.
\begin{writenotejp}
Report~103 swaps the letters of Report~101: here $d_j$ are the \emph{logarithmic} and $c_j$ the \emph{multiplicative} coefficients (Report~101: $b_j$ and $d_j$; Part~\ref{air:part:dfa}: unnamed and $\mathfrak c_j$). With $a=z$, $d_1,d_2,d_3$ are Part~\ref{air:part:dfa}'s \eqref{air:dfa:eq:log-corrections} ($4248855/5443200=3497/4480$) and $c_1,c_2,c_3$ its $\mathfrak c_1,\mathfrak c_2,\mathfrak c_3$ \eqref{air:dfa:eq:relative-coefficients}. New to this report: $d_4=-5429a^2/19440$, $c_4$, and the ratio \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:ratio} through $n^{-7/3}$ (Part~\ref{air:part:dfa} prints no ratio expansion). The conversions $d\mapsto c$ and $d\mapsto$ ratio were recomputed symbolically at the write (difference zero); through $n^{-7/3}$ the ratio involves only $a$ and $d_1,\ldots,d_4$, and its $O(n^{-8/3})$ remainder uses Theorem~\ref{air:jp:dfa:thm:main} at depth $m=7$.
\end{writenotejp}

\subsection{What the calculations establish}
The exact checker builds Proposition 5 with integer arithmetic, including $b(-1,0)=1$. It checks the rotated recurrence and every row of the eliminated memory equation, including the origin and upper supports, using rational arithmetic. Symbolic checks verify the band identities, the completed formal recurrence, the gauges, and endpoint conversions. These are finite corroborations of the algebra, not replacements for the analytic proof above.

The optional numerical producer uses exact integer dynamic programming through $n=3000$ before evaluating logarithms, factorial logarithms, and Airy constants in floating point. Define
\[
 G_n=\frac{b_n}{n!8^ne^{3an^{1/3}}n^{7/8}},\qquad
 G_n^{(4)}=\frac{G_n}{1+\sum_{j=1}^4c_jn^{-j/3}}.
\]
Representative computed values are
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{rcc}
\toprule
$n$&$G_n$&$G_n^{(4)}$\\
\midrule
1000&102.077189106&76.4392078929\\
2000&96.6685122338&76.4384838642\\
3000&94.0516522622&76.4383234197\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
These values are compatible with an amplitude near $76.438$, but no numerical tail enclosure has been proved. None of these displayed digits is a certified digit of the limit. The theorem and inverse results use the mathematical constant \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:gamma}, never an uncertified decimal substitute.
\begin{writenotejp}
The value near $76.438$ is uncertified evidence for $\gamma_D$, which is Part~\ref{air:part:dfa}'s $\gamma$; Part~\ref{air:part:dfa} prints only uncorrected normalized values, $131.985$ at $n=100$ (in its verification section, just before Section~\ref{air:dfa:sec:questions}), which decrease slowly towards it because $c_1n^{-1/3}$ is large ($c_1=53a^2/90\approx3.219$). The intake recomputed $G_{1000}=102.077189105\ldots$ (the table's $\ldots106$ is a last-digit rounding) and $G^{(4)}_{1000}=76.4392078929$ from exact counts. No enclosure is claimed.
\end{writenotejp}

\section{Rigorous inverse asymptotics}\label{air:jp:dfa:sec:inverse}
The forward theorem gives inverse expansions involving Lambert $W$, logarithms, and fractional powers. We distinguish recovery of the index of an exact sequence value from inversion of an arbitrary threshold. All inverse formulas are for sufficiently large positive inputs.

\subsection{An eventual inverse requiring no amplitude value}
For $Y>0$ put
\begin{align}
 X(Y)&=\frac{Y}{W_0(8Y/e)},&L(Y)&=\log(8X(Y)),\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:lambert}\\
 Z(Y)&=X(Y)-\frac{3aX(Y)^{1/3}+\frac{11}{8}\log X(Y)+\frac12\log(2\pi)}{L(Y)},\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:Z}
\end{align}
where $W_0$ is the principal positive real branch.

\begin{theorem}[Exact value recovery]\label{air:jp:dfa:thm:inverse}
For $Y=\log b_n$,
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:inversecoarse}
 Z(\log b_n)=n+O(1/\log n).
\end{equation}
Thus nearest-integer rounding recovers $n$ for all sufficiently large exact sequence values, without requiring a numerical value of $\gamma_D$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Even the two-sided bound \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:published}, combined with Stirling's formula, gives
\[
 \log b_n=G(n)+O(1),\qquad
 G(x)=x(\log(8x)-1)+3ax^{1/3}+\tfrac{11}{8}\log x+\tfrac12\log(2\pi).
\]
Put $G_0(x)=x(\log(8x)-1)$ and $g(x)=G(x)-G_0(x)$. The Lambert identity gives $G_0(X)=Y$ and $G_0'(X)=L$. If $h=-g(X)/L$, then $Z=X+h$ and $h=O(X^{1/3}/\log X)$. Taylor expansion gives
\[
 G(Z)-Y=g'(X)h+O(h^2/X)=O(X^{-1/3}/\log X).
\]
Here $g'(x)=ax^{-2/3}+11/(8x)$; its further Taylor remainder is smaller than the displayed bound. At $Y=\log b_n$, $X\sim Z\sim n$ and $G'(x)\sim\log x$. The mean value theorem proves \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:inversecoarse}. Since its error tends to zero, eventual nearest-integer recovery follows.
\end{proof}
This theorem has an unspecified eventual cutoff. The proof does not turn an unknown $O(1)$ amplitude term into an effective finite-range rounding certificate.

\subsection{Arbitrary thresholds need a comparison}
Equation \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:ratio} gives $b_n/b_{n-1}\sim8n$, so the sequence is strictly increasing eventually and unbounded. The finitely many earlier values do not affect sufficiently large threshold inverses. Direct differentiation of \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:Z} gives $Z'(Y)\sim1/\log X>0$.

\begin{corollary}[One comparison after rounding]\label{air:jp:dfa:cor:threshold}
For sufficiently large $y$, set $j_0=\operatorname{round}(Z(\log y))$. Then
\[
 k(y)=\max\{n:b_n\le y\}
 =\begin{cases}j_0,&b_{j_0}\le y,\\j_0-1,&b_{j_0}>y.\end{cases}
\]
The least index $j(y)$ with $b_{j(y)}\ge y$ is
\[
 j(y)=\begin{cases}j_0,&b_{j_0}\ge y,\\j_0+1,&b_{j_0}<y.\end{cases}
\]
The comparison must be exact or rigorously certified; equality belongs to the first branch in each rule.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
If $k=k(y)$, monotonicity puts $Z(\log y)$ between $k+o(1)$ and $k+1+o(1)$. For large $k$, nearest-integer rounding therefore lies in $\{k,k+1\}$. The comparison selects the correct branch. For the least-index rule the same argument gives $j_0\in\{j(y)-1,j(y)\}$; at equality the exact-value theorem applies directly.
\end{proof}
An input arbitrarily close to a sequence value cannot be safely classified by an unverified floating-point asymptotic evaluation.

\subsection{An inverse at any prescribed algebraic order}
For fixed $J\ge0$, define the smooth model
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:FJ}
 \mathcal F_J(x)=\log\Gamma(x+1)+x\log8+3ax^{1/3}+\tfrac78\log x
 +\log\gamma_D+\sum_{j=1}^Jd_jx^{-j/3}.
\end{equation}
The $\Gamma$ in this formula is Euler's gamma function, distinct from the product $\Gamma_n$ in \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:normalize}. For large $x$, $\mathcal F_J'(x)\sim\log x>0$. Let $x_J(Y)$ be the unique sufficiently large root of $\mathcal F_J(x)=Y$.

\begin{theorem}[All order inverse estimate]\label{air:jp:dfa:thm:sharpinverse}
For each fixed $J\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:sharpinverse}
 x_J(\log b_n)=n+O\!\left(\frac{n^{-(J+1)/3}}{\log n}\right).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Equation \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:logmain} says
$\log b_n=\mathcal F_J(n)+O(n^{-(J+1)/3})$. The derivative is asymptotic to $\log x$, so the mean value theorem gives the claimed inverse error.
\end{proof}
This is an unconditional mathematical theorem with the positive amplitude characterized in Section~\ref{air:jp:dfa:sec:amplitude}. It is not a certified numerical algorithm if that constant or the function evaluations are replaced by uncontrolled floating-point approximations.

\subsection{A finite Newton construction with an explicit iteration count}
The implicit root can be replaced, at every prescribed order, by finitely many specified Newton steps. Start from $x_0=X(Y)$ in \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:lambert} and iterate
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:newton}
 x_{k+1}=x_k-\frac{\mathcal F_J(x_k)-Y}{\mathcal F_J'(x_k)},\qquad
 K=\lceil\log_2(J+4)\rceil-1.
\end{equation}
The correct derivative is
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:Fprime}
 \mathcal F_J'(x)=\psi(x+1)+\log8+ax^{-2/3}+\frac7{8x}
 -\sum_{j=1}^J\frac j3d_jx^{-j/3-1},
\end{equation}
where $\psi$ is the digamma function. Then
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:newtonaccuracy}
 x_K(\log b_n)=n+O\!\left(\frac{n^{-(J+1)/3}}{\log n}\right).
\end{equation}
To prove the finite bound, let $x_*$ be the model root and put $X=x_0$, $L=\log(8X)$. The Airy term gives $x_*-X=O(X^{1/3}/L)$. On a neighborhood $X\pm o(X)$,
\[
 \mathcal F_J'\sim L,\qquad \mathcal F_J''=O(1/X).
\]
Exact Newton errors $e_k=x_k-x_*$ therefore satisfy
\[
 |e_{k+1}|\le C|e_k|^2/(XL).
\]
For large $X$, the iterates stay in this neighborhood. Induction yields
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:newtonerrors}
 e_k=O\!\left(\frac{X^{1-2^{k+1}/3}}{L^{2^{k+1}-1}}\right).
\end{equation}
The first two errors are $O(X^{-1/3}/L^3)$ and $O(X^{-5/3}/L^7)$. Since $2^{K+1}\ge J+4$, the $K$th error is no greater than the model error in \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:sharpinverse}. This proves \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:newtonaccuracy}; one Newton step alone is not arbitrarily accurate.

There is also a version using only a finite Stirling polynomial. Replace $\log\Gamma(x+1)$ in \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:FJ} by
\begin{equation}\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:stirling}
 (x+\tfrac12)\log x-x+\tfrac12\log(2\pi)
 +\sum_{r=1}^R\frac{B_{2r}}{2r(2r-1)x^{2r-1}},\qquad
 R=\max\left(0,\left\lceil\frac{J-2}{6}\right\rceil\right).
\end{equation}
Its error is $O(x^{-2R-1})$, and $2R+1\ge(J+1)/3$. Its root therefore differs from $x_*$ by at most the target inverse error. Differentiating this finite expression consistently, the same $K$ Newton steps suffice. For fixed order this uses finitely many arithmetic operations, logarithms, real fractional powers, one Lambert-$W$ evaluation, and the exact constants $a,\gamma_D,d_j$ and Bernoulli numbers.

\subsection{The first explicit fractional power corrections}
The first terms can also be written without an implicit root. Set
\[
 X=\frac{\log b_n}{W_0(8\log b_n/e)},\quad L=\log(8X),\quad
 C_D=\log\gamma_D+\tfrac12\log(2\pi).
\]
Then
\begin{align}
 n={}&X-\frac{3aX^{1/3}+\frac{11}{8}\log X+C_D}{L}\notag\\
 &+X^{-1/3}\left[-\frac{d_1}{L}+\frac{3a^2}{L^2}
 -\frac{9a^2}{2L^3}\right]+O(X^{-2/3}/L).\label{air:jp:dfa:eq:explicitinverse}
\end{align}
To verify the transcription, write $g_0(X)=3aX^{1/3}+(11/8)\log X+C_D$ and put $h_0=-g_0(X)/L$. In the Taylor expansion of $G_0(X+h_0)$, the relevant quadratic term is $h_0^2/(2X)$. The derivative of the Airy term contributes $aX^{-2/3}h_0$, and the first forward correction contributes $d_1X^{-1/3}$. Their coefficient at scale $X^{-1/3}$ is
\[
 d_1-\frac{3a^2}{L}+\frac{9a^2}{2L^2}.
\]
The second line of \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:explicitinverse}, multiplied by $L$, cancels it. The remaining residual is $O(X^{-2/3})$, including logarithmic cross terms because $\log X/L$ is bounded. The mean value theorem gives the stated remainder. This establishes the displayed fractional-power and logarithmic structure with a genuine error bound.
\begin{writenotejp}
Part~\ref{air:part:dfa}'s explicit inverse \eqref{air:dfa:eq:first-inverse} stops at the remainder $O(X^{-1/3}/\log X)$ (its denominator $1+w$ equals $L$, since $\log(8X)=1+W_0(8Y/\ee)$ at the anchor). The bracket $X^{-1/3}[-d_1/L+3a^2/L^2-9a^2/(2L^3)]$ of \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:explicitinverse} is the next term, new to this report. It was rederived at the write from $G_0(X+h)=Y+Lh+h^2/(2X)+O(h^3/X^2)$ with $h=h_0+h_1$: the terms of $h_0^2/(2X)+aX^{-2/3}h_0+d_1X^{-1/3}$ at scale $X^{-1/3}$ are $(9a^2/(2L^2)-3a^2/L+d_1)X^{-1/3}$, and $Lh_1$ cancels them, as stated.
\end{writenotejp}

\paragraph{What is and is not a transseries result.}
Repeated finite expansion of \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:newton} produces compatible algebraic--logarithmic inverse expressions to any prescribed accuracy. This is the rigorous content of the inverse expansion here. It does not establish exponentially small sectors, Stokes constants, Borel summability, or a convergent infinite expansion. Calling the entire forward or inverse object a full transseries would require additional analysis beyond this report.

\section{Further questions and boundaries of the proof}\label{air:jp:dfa:sec:questions}
\begin{writenotejp}
These questions are re-scoped in Section~\ref{air:jp:sec:questions}, with those of Reports~100 and~101.
\end{writenotejp}
\paragraph{Certified numerical amplitudes.}
The natural next objective is an interval evaluation of the finite Jacobi product and projection, together with explicit bounds for the tails in \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:canonicalproduct} and \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:canonicalseries}. Effective constants for the gap, drift, memory perturbation, and smoothing estimates would turn the observed amplitude near $76.438$ into certified digits. They would also yield finite inverse cutoffs.

\paragraph{Uniform depth and coefficient growth.}
The present argument fixes the truncation depth before taking $n\to\infty$. Bounds uniform in depth could identify the growth of $d_j$ and an optimal truncation regime. A factorial or Gevrey bound would still need proof; finite lists of coefficients cannot decide it.

\paragraph{Exponentially small Airy modes.}
The spectral gap suggests a stretched-exponential relaxation mechanism involving other Airy eigenvalues. A genuine beyond-all-orders theory must track excited-mode amplitudes, their coupling under changing matrices and signed memory, and remainders after optimal truncation. An algebraic remainder at every fixed order does not determine those sectors or their amplitudes.

\paragraph{Other alphabet sizes and memory structures.}
For a larger alphabet, the exact counting recurrence and its combinatorial boundary conditions must first be derived and checked. One may then ask whether a related positive reference operator and a summable scalar correction exist. The coefficient $1/(8n)$ in the present proof is specific to binary minimal automata; simply reusing it would not be justified.
\begin{writenotejp}
Largely answered: Part~\ref{air:part:ka} treats automata over every fixed alphabet of $k\ge3$ letters, with the exact arity-$k$ recurrence (Part~0, ``One family, indexed by the arity''), a positive relaxed reference evolution in a fixed weighted norm, and a delay that is summable for $k\ge3$, so that no drift cancellation is needed there. As this paragraph cautions, the binary $1/(8n)$ does not carry over. Uniformity in $k$ remains open.
\end{writenotejp}

\paragraph{More efficient rigorous inversion.}
The Newton theorem gives an asymptotic operation count at fixed order, not an optimized finite-input implementation. Combining certified amplitude intervals, rigorous function evaluation, and exact recurrence comparisons could give a practical index-recovery or threshold-search algorithm with a verified stopping test.

\paragraph{Source normalization and independent reproduction.}
An implementation should test the negative-index seed, $e(2,0)=1/2$, and $e(3,1)=3/4$ before larger computations. These inexpensive checks prevent a changed startup from silently altering the global amplitude while leaving local Airy coefficients unchanged. The normalization discussion in Section~\ref{air:jp:dfa:sec:exact} is a local arithmetic reconciliation, not a claim that the original asymptotic theorem is false.

\section{Reproducibility and source record}\label{air:jp:dfa:sec:reproduction}
The accompanying package contains the standalone \LaTeX\ article, its PDF, a build script, a replay script, explicit exact checker inputs, deliberate-error regression tests, and a separate optional numerical producer. The exact suite is fail-closed: unsuccessful equalities raise explicit exceptions, so checks remain active under optimized Python. Its normal and optimized runs are both tested, and deliberately corrupted cases must fail for the expected reason. Finite checks certify the finite identities that they test, not the analytic limits and remainder estimates.

\section{Further questions and research (Part V)}\label{air:jp:sec:questions}
This section was written when Part~\ref{air:part:jp} was added. It collects every question and every unproved claim of Reports~100, 101 and~103, with its source, its sketch and what is missing, and records which of them other Parts answer. No claim of the three reports was found to be wrong; none is retracted. The questions of the earlier Parts are not repeated; where they coincide, the earlier Part is named.

\subsection{Open}
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{V.\arabic*}, ref=V.\arabic*, leftmargin=*]
\item\label{air:jp:q:certified} \emph{Certified digits and effective cutoffs.} (Reports~100, 101, 103, Sections~\ref{air:jp:rb:sec:questions}, \ref{air:jp:cb:sec:reproduce}, \ref{air:jp:dfa:sec:questions}; also Parts~\ref{air:part:rb}, \ref{air:part:cb}, \ref{air:part:dfa}.) The amplitudes have convergent representations, \eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:Cproduct}--\eqref{air:jp:rb:eq:gamma}, \eqref{air:jp:cb:eq:K}--\eqref{air:jp:cb:eq:gamma} and \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:canonicalproduct}--\eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:canonicalseries}, whose summands are $O(n^{-4/3})$. Missing: explicit constants for the gap, drift, gauge, memory and smoothing estimates, an interval evaluation of the finite part and a tail enclosure. They would certify digits of $\gamma_r\approx166.952089$, $\gamma_c\approx173.126705$ and $\gamma_D\approx76.438$ (all uncertified) and make the eventual inverse statements effective; at $n=3000$ the amplitude-free inverse is still off by $0.558$ (relaxed) and $0.562$ (compacted).
\item\label{air:jp:q:gevrey} \emph{Coefficient growth, Gevrey class, optimal truncation.} (All three reports.) Each expansion is proved at every fixed depth; the envelope arguments are not uniform in the depth. Missing: bounds on the polynomial--Airy recursion uniform in the order. Finite lists of coefficients ($b_1,\ldots,b_6$, $d_1,\ldots,d_4$, $\sigma_3,\ldots,\sigma_7$) cannot decide the growth.
\item\label{air:jp:q:sectors} \emph{Exponentially small Airy sectors.} (All three reports; Part~\ref{air:part:rb}'s ``Beyond algebraic orders''.) The spectral gap suggests relative corrections of stretched-exponential size $\exp(-c\,n^{1/3})$, $c>0$, from the excited Airy modes belonging to $a_2,a_3,\ldots$. Missing: evolving excited-mode amplitudes, their coupling under the changing matrices (and, for Reports~101 and~103, under signed memory), and remainders after optimal truncation. No transseries, Stokes constant or Borel summability is claimed.
\item\label{air:jp:q:normalization} \emph{Report~103's remark on the printed normalization in the AofA 2020 paper.} Report~103 (Section~\ref{air:jp:dfa:sec:exact}) states that the transformed arrays on pages 11:6--11:7 of \cite{dfa}, normalized by $2^{m-1}$ for $m\ge1$ only, give $\frac54$ at $(3,1)$ when their rotated recurrence is read uniformly, against $\frac32$ from the piecewise normalization. The uniform-normalization values were rechecked at the write ($e(3,1)=\frac34$; piecewise $\frac32$). Missing: a check of the printed pages, which are not in the repository. The remark is about a publication's display, not about its theorem; Report~103 says so, and neither Report~103 nor Part~\ref{air:part:dfa} uses that display.
\item\label{air:jp:q:intrinsic} \emph{Can Lemma~\ref{air:jp:rb:lem:positive} replace the published lower bounds in the signed-memory cases?} (New at the write.) Report~100 proves $\gamma_r>0$ from the exact recurrence alone, by restarting the positive contraction $T_n$ from $\phi_N$ and comparing $v_N\ge\delta\phi_N$ (Remark~\ref{air:jp:rem:positivity}). Reports~101 and~103 and Parts~\ref{air:part:cb} and~\ref{air:part:dfa} still take positivity from the compacted or automaton lower bounds of Elvey Price, Fang and Wallner, and Part~\ref{air:part:ka} from the relaxed lower bound of Ghosh Dastidar and Wallner. The argument does not transfer verbatim: the signed evolutions \eqref{air:jp:cb:eq:normalized} and \eqref{air:jp:dfa:eq:normalizedmemory} do not preserve the order $v\ge\delta w$, and their tracking estimates start from the counting bound $\norm{v_n}\le Cn^{1/4}$ (resp.\ $n^{1/8}$), which is proved for the counting solution, not for a restarted one. A possible route: restart in a positive representation instead (Part~\ref{air:part:cb}'s exact four-state or three-state model, Part~\ref{air:part:dfa}'s renewal representation, Proposition~\ref{air:dfa:prop:renewal}), where $X_N\ge\delta Y_N$ propagates; what is missing is a tracking theorem for the restarted positive solution, uniform in the restart time, that shows its scalar projection stays near its initial value. The restart comparison works wherever the normalized evolution is entrywise nonnegative: bundle Reports 104 and 102 use it for tree-child networks (Parts~III and~IV of \repopath{../a213863-tree-child-networks}). For Part~\ref{air:part:ka}'s relaxed $k$-ary evolution, which has nonnegative coefficients, the restart argument may apply directly once its tracking constants are shown to be independent of the restart time.
\item\label{air:jp:q:positive} \emph{A positive route to the compacted amplitude.} (Report~100, Section~\ref{air:jp:rb:sec:questions}, ``Compacted trees''; Part~\ref{air:part:cb}, ``Positive effective operators''.) Report~100 suggests a positive multistate encoding of the forbidden local configurations. Such encodings exist (Part~\ref{air:part:cb}'s four-state, three-state and renewal models) but are not symmetric Jacobi reductions. The amplitude is now proved by signed memory (Report~101, Part~\ref{air:part:cb}); a proof through a positive encoding, which would also answer~\ref{air:jp:q:intrinsic} for compacted trees, remains open. Report~100's caution stands: upper and lower positive comparisons alone can leave different constants.
\item\label{air:jp:q:inversion} \emph{Efficient rigorous inversion.} (Report~103, Section~\ref{air:jp:dfa:sec:questions}; Parts~\ref{air:part:rb}, \ref{air:part:cb}.) The Newton theorems give an operation count at fixed order, not a finite-input algorithm. Missing: certified amplitude intervals, rigorous function evaluation and a verified stopping test, combined with exact comparisons of neighbouring counts.
\item\label{air:jp:q:sketched} \emph{Steps given as sketches.} Report~100 sketches the upper min--max bound by sampled Dirichlet trial functions and the lower bound by piecewise-linear interpolation and Rellich compactness (Section~\ref{air:jp:rb:sec:airy}), and the uniform Taylor envelopes of its residual estimates (Sections~\ref{air:jp:rb:sec:airy} and~\ref{air:jp:rb:sec:allorders}); Reports~101 and~103 sketch the same envelopes for the delayed recurrences and import Report~100's estimates without reproof. The steps are standard and no gap was found at the intake, but they are not written out in full. The same estimates for the one-step matrices are written out in Part~\ref{air:part:rb}, Sections~\ref{air:rb:exact-recurrence-and-contractive-symmetrization}--\ref{air:rb:convergence-of-the-scalar-amplitude}, and in Appendix~\ref{air:app:jacobi}; a full write-up of the two-step versions, or a derivation of them from the one-step ones, is missing.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Answered elsewhere in this report}
\begin{itemize}
\item Report~100's ``Compacted trees'' (no compacted amplitude theorem) and its conditional compacted inverse: answered by Report~101 (Theorems~\ref{air:jp:cb:thm:main}, \ref{air:jp:cb:thm:allorders}, Corollary~\ref{air:jp:cb:cor:sharpinverse}) and Part~\ref{air:part:cb}; its formal compacted expansions are genuine by Corollary~\ref{air:jp:cor:compacted}. The positive route stays open (\ref{air:jp:q:positive}).
\item Report~100's ``Higher arity and related recurrences'' and Report~103's ``Other alphabet sizes and memory structures'': answered for every fixed $k\ge3$ by Part~\ref{air:part:ka}, for relaxed trees, compacted trees and automata; uniformity in $k$ remains open there (Section~\ref{air:ka:sec:checks}).
\item Report~103's ``Source normalization and independent reproduction'' is a recommendation, carried out by its own exact checker and at the intake ($b(1,1)=1$, $e(2,0)=\frac12$, $e(3,1)=\frac34$ recomputed).
\item Report~100's statement that the inspected repository snapshot \texttt{4b874cea0} held no relaxed-amplitude result was true there and is stale now (note in Section~\ref{air:jp:rb:sec:reproduce}).
